\section{Further questions raised by manuscript 03}
\label{spf:rc:sec:questions}

Manuscript~03 poses ten research projects, as proposals only, ordered to
separate exact finite enumeration, the algebraic sector and
beyond-all-orders information. Several continue questions of Parts~I
and~II, as noted.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Prove the original A189281 recurrence} (Part~I's question~2).
The order-eight, degree-eleven recurrence remains a separate target. One
route writes the exact finite tiling sum as its stable contribution plus
explicit boundary corrections and seeks a telescoping certificate for the
whole; agreement of its formal asymptotic solution with
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:A-collapse} is a consistency check, not a proof. The first
deliverable should be a finite identity whose boundary terms are fully
specified.
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97: Part~V proves that the OEIS operator
annihilates the analytic completion of the expansion, not the counts
(\cref{spf:bc:thm:operator}), and reduces the problem to a recurrence for the
flat discrepancy (Part~V, question~2).]
\item \emph{The first beyond-all-orders term} (Part~I's question~3, Part~II's
question~2). Find the leading difference between two forest shapes with the
same $r,s,n$ and long paths, whose low moments agree until a component can
meet a whole short path: is it a single spanning component or a family of
competing profiles? A first case changes two path lengths by $+1$ and $-1$.
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97: still open. For A189281 itself Part~V proves
that the count differs from its positive-ray Borel completion by a flat term
that is nonzero in every block of four indices
(\cref{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero}); for a short path of fixed length Part~IV
finds the shape at an algebraic order (\cref{spf:mg:thm:shortpath}).]
\item \emph{Large-order growth of $c_J$} (Part~I's question~4). Use
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:subfactorial} and the composition $\mathcal E'(e^t-1)$ to
find sharp asymptotics, oscillation and signs, first at $(2,2)$ and then for
all fixed offsets; \eqref{spf:rc:eq:psi-recurrence} may be a first step.
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97: answered in part at $(2,2)$ by Part~V:
$\limsup_m(\log m)|c_{m+1}/m!|^{1/m}=2$, the series $\sum c_Jz^J$ has radius
zero, no Gevrey bound of order $s<1$ holds, and $(c_J)$ is not P-recursive
(\cref{spf:bc:thm:coeff-growth,spf:bc:thm:nonholonomic}). Sharp asymptotics
of individual $c_J$, oscillation and signs, and the other offsets remain open
(Part~V, questions~3 and~7).]
\item \emph{Summation and optimal truncation} (Part~I's question~4). Determine
the growth of $\widehat C$ in sectors and whether an accelerated or
nonstandard Laplace transform recovers a natural analytic object; a complete
answer names the function reconstructed, the directions, the optimal
truncation order in $n$, and the relation to the finite counts.
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97: answered at $(2,2)$ by Part~V, except for
optimality. The function reconstructed is the positive-ray sum
$\bcC(n)=1+\int_0^\infty e^{-nt}\widehat C(t)\,dt$, convergent exactly for
$\Re n>1$ and meromorphic in $n$ (\cref{spf:bc:thm:sum,spf:bc:cor:meromorphic});
the admissible geometry is a horizontal strip of half-width $\pi/4$, and no
open angular sector about the positive axis carries an exponential bound
(\cref{spf:bc:thm:strip,spf:bc:cor:nonsectorial}); the truncation order
$M=\lfloor n(\frac12\log n-1)\rfloor$ gives the error
$\exp(-\frac12n\log n+\bigO(n))$ (\cref{spf:bc:thm:truncation}), with no proof
of optimality; and the finite counts differ from $\bcC$ by a flat term that is
nonzero in every block of four indices (\cref{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero}). The
optimal truncation and its prefactor remain open (Part~V, question~4).]
\item \emph{A finite-algebraic proof of the single-sum lemma.} The beta
integral is used only to evaluate a finite Taylor jet; a telescoping proof
of \eqref{spf:rc:eq:double-functional}, a polynomial identity for the
reciprocal-factorial functionals, would simplify formalization and may show
a direct combinatorial cancellation.
\item \emph{Formalize the finite core} (Part~I's question~7, Part~II's formal
package). A Lean development can first define falling products, generalized
integer binomials and \eqref{spf:rc:eq:single-sum},
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:collapse}; after clearing denominators the collapse is a
finite rearrangement and a Vandermonde convolution. Stable tilings and the
analytic remainder are further layers, and a formal proof of the algebraic
identity alone must not be labelled a formal proof of the asymptotic
statement.
\item \emph{Absolute differences.} For $|\pi(i+r)-\pi(i)|\ne s$ each
component has two orientations (weight $2^c$, not $2^k$), and the
cancellation behind \eqref{spf:rc:eq:collapse} changes. Find an equally
explicit kernel, optimal denominator bounds, and which arithmetic survives;
the directed integrality theorem must not be transplanted without proof.
\item \emph{Growing offsets} (Part~I's question~5). If $\sigma$ grows, the
sharp-degree threshold, the weight $\rcw_\sigma$ and the stability range
move. Find ranges such as $r+s=\smallo(\log n)$ with a uniform theorem, and
the crossovers where Poisson$(1)$ normalization fails; the exact mean
$(n-r)(n-s)/(n(n-1))$ constrains any scaling limit.
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97: answered in local-density coordinates by
Part~IV: the expansion holds uniformly for all $1\le r,s\le n$ with the exact
parameter $\lambda_n=\theta(1-r/n)(1-s/n)$ in place of a Poisson$(1)$
normalization, the law tends to Poisson$(\theta(1-\alpha)(1-\beta))$ when
$r/n\to\alpha$ and $s/n\to\beta$ (\cref{spf:mg:cor:moving}), and the
corrections have kinks at $r/n=1/q$ (\cref{spf:mg:thm:boundary}). Part~IV's
coefficients are polynomials in local densities, not Part~III's integer
polynomials; how the degree law and the weight $\rcw_\sigma$ behave for
growing $\sigma$ remains open.]
\item \emph{Positivity and divisibility, combinatorially.} For $h\ge\sigma$
the coefficients of $u^h$ in $B_J$ are nonnegative sums of integer weights
and Stirling numbers; find a labelled-object interpretation. At $(2,2)$ the
factor $24$ suggests a four-element ordering or symmetry, but the proof
gives a convolution identity, not a bijection (compare Part~I's
question~1).
\item \emph{Several forbidden gaps} (Part~I's question~6, Part~II's
question~4). The graphs are then not unions of paths, and components may
branch or cycle. Determine which sparse families still admit stable
component polynomials and a finite hypergeometric normalization; a first
project is a two-gap directed model of bounded component width.
[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101: answered in part by Part~VII, for the
absolute model with the reciprocal gaps $(r,s)$ and $(s,r)$: components
branch and cycle, but compatible ones are triangle-free and $2$-degenerate
(\cref{spf:cg:lem:coreedges}), and finite packing dictionaries with eventual
embedding polynomials take the place of stable component polynomials
(\cref{spf:cg:thm:packing,spf:cg:prop:mobius}). The directed two-gap model
remains open.]
\end{enumerate}
Manuscript~03 suggests starting with questions~5 and~6 and the asymptotics of
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:psi-recurrence}; questions~1 and~2 are the closest to the
outstanding OEIS problem, and the Borel-growth questions~3 and~4 and the
growing offsets of question~8 are broader analytic projects.

\paragraph{Conclusion of manuscript~03, restated.} The conjecture of Part~I
is resolved by \cref{spf:rc:thm:A-collapse}, one specialization of
\cref{spf:rc:thm:collapse}, valid for every pair of directed offsets. The
integer formula \eqref{spf:rc:eq:B-Stirling} proves the arithmetic
phenomenon at every order, explains the delayed high-degree terms and gives
efficient coefficient generation; the Borel transform and the index
reconstruction are further consequences. The remaining distinction is
between the stable asymptotic information, proved here with controlled
remainders, and exact finite enumeration: the original guessed recurrence
and a complete beyond-all-orders expansion are not claimed.

\paragraph{OEIS note, as manuscript~03 proposes it.} Its
\path{data/03-rational-collapse-OEIS_proposed_note.md} drafts an OEIS
remark that every $c_J$ of A189281 is an integer, with the finite formula
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:B-Stirling} at $u=-1$, that the rational-collapse conjecture
is proved, that the A189281 recurrence is not, and that the same holds for
every directed pair including A189282--A189284. It is marked ``not
submitted''; it asks for a stable public citation and independent review
first. Nothing was submitted.

\section{Formula sheet}
\label{spf:rc:sec:formulas}

With $\sigma=r+s$ and $\rcw_j=\rise{(r-1)}j\rise{(s-1)}j/j!$ (manuscript~03,
Appendix~A):
\begin{align}
 \mu_k^*(n)&=\sum_{j=0}^k
  \frac{\rcw_j\binom{n-\sigma+1-j}{k-j}}{\fall n{k+j}},\notag\\
 R_h^{r,s}(n)&=\sum_{j=0}^h
  \frac{\rcw_j\binom{h-\sigma}{h-j}}{\fall n{h+j}},\notag\\
 B_J(u)&=\sum_{h=1}^Ju^h\sum_{j=0}^{\min(h,J-h)}
  \rcw_j\binom{h-\sigma}{h-j}\Stir{J-1}{h+j-1}\quad(J\ge1),\notag\\
 \psi_i&=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor i/2\rfloor}
  (-1)^{i-j}\rcw_j\binom{i-j-\sigma}{i-2j},\label{spf:rc:eq:psi}\\
 c_J&=\sum_{i=1}^J\psi_i\Stir{J-1}{i-1}\quad(J\ge1),\notag\\
 \Psi(z)&=(1+z)^{\sigma-1}\,{}_2F_0\Bigl(r-1,s-1;\,;-\frac{z^2}{1+z}\Bigr),\notag\\
 \widehat C(t)&=\mathcal E'(e^t-1),\qquad
 \mathcal E(\xi)=\sum_{i\ge0}\psi_i\xi^i/i!.\notag
\end{align}
The conventions for $J=0$ are $B_0=c_0=\psi_0=1$. The formulas for
$\mu_k^*$ and $R_h$ are rational identities; actual moments require
\eqref{spf:eq:stable-range}. $\Psi$ is formal, while $\mathcal E$ and
$\widehat C$ are entire.

\paragraph{The shipped files of manuscript~03.} The prefix
\path{03-rational-collapse-} marks its twelve shipped files. Its three scripts and its
\texttt{Makefile} are in \texttt{code/}; its five recorded outputs, the
b-file excerpt and its OEIS note are in \texttt{data/}; its
\texttt{SOURCES.md} is at the report root. All are byte-identical to the
delivery. \texttt{certify.py} imports its sibling by the delivered name
(\texttt{from verify import \ldots}); \texttt{verify.py} and
\texttt{inverse\_demo.py} read the excerpt by its delivered name,
\texttt{b189281\_0\_30.txt}; all three write unprefixed files into the
\texttt{data/} directory beside their own; and the \texttt{Makefile} calls
the scripts by their delivered names. In the shipped layout the scripts
would fail to import, skip the OEIS-value checks or fail to find their
input, and write beside the files of Parts~I and~II. The report README
gives a copy-based recipe.

\part{Moving gaps: uniform local expansions, short paths and finite-size boundaries}
\label{spf:mg:part}

\section{Provenance, scope and notation of Part IV}
\label{spf:mg:sec:front}

[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97.] Part~IV was added in the write of
batch~97. It is built from manuscript~05 of that batch, which drops every
hypothesis on the lengths of the paths: it proves the all-orders expansion of
the whole violation law uniformly over \emph{all} pairs of path forests on
$n$ vertices, with coefficients that are universal polynomials in normalized
subpath counts, and derives from it the first shape-dependent term of a fixed
short path, the behaviour of moving and proportional offsets, and an index
reconstruction along rational offset rays. Where manuscript~05 proves a
statement already printed in Parts~I--III, its proof is kept as a marked second
route and the earlier statement is named (\cref{spf:mg:tab:routes}).
Manuscript~05's symbols that clash with Parts~I--III are renamed or declared in
\cref{spf:mg:tab:dictionary}.

\subsection{Provenance}
\label{spf:mg:sec:provenance}

Manuscript~05 of batch~97 is the archive
\nolinkurl{ProveIt_Moving_Gap_Permutations.zip} (21~files, one of them the
checksum ledger \texttt{SHA256SUMS.txt}, which lists the other 20), which
arrived in commit \texttt{e88ed8bf6} and was placed in this report by commit
\texttt{5a69916e8}. Its article is titled \emph{Moving-Gap Permutation
Avoidance: Uniform local expansions, sharp short-path effects, and a hierarchy
of finite-size boundaries} (26~pages, dated 4~October 2026); its title page
reads ``Prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov'' and ``AI-assisted mathematical
research report'', and its PDF metadata names ``AI-assisted research report
prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov'' as author. Its pinned repository commit is
\texttt{d1680cdfa40c44abf0825416b6f36a3e9ec2662c} (2026-10-04,
18:28;~\cite{mgProveIt}). It read this report's \texttt{article.tex} as the
Git blob \texttt{73a53d9784260c2f0d083f2baa62a5535f6d6105}, which is also the
blob of this file immediately before the batch-97 write: manuscript~05 saw
Parts~I--III exactly as they stood.

Manuscript~05 states correctly what Parts~I--III contain: the fixed-offset
expansions (Parts~I and~II), the directed rational collapse and integrality
(Part~III), the exact profile identity (Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:exact}), the moment
bound (Lemma~\ref{spf:lem:moment-bound}), the one-path mechanism of Part~II
(\cref{fga:prop:onepath}) and the Lambert-$W$ inversion
(\cref{spf:thm:inverse-all}), and it claims none of them anew. Its
``Section~22'' is Part~II's \cref{fga:sec:questions}, and the ``growing gaps''
question it answers is Part~I's question~5 (\cref{spf:sec:questions}), listed
there among the overlaps. A second batch-97 manuscript, the source of Part~V,
continues this report independently; the two share no theorem.

\paragraph{Scope and non-claims of manuscript~05, kept.}
It is an AI-assisted, unrefereed research manuscript with conventional
proofs and reproducible checks, not a Lean or Rocq formalization. Its
infinite results rest on its written proofs; its finite checks support but
do not replace them. Historical priority is not established: its source
audit was targeted, not exhaustive, and ``failure to find a prior result in
this audit is not evidence that none exists''. Its expansion is an all-orders
algebraic sector, not a resurgent or exponentially complete transseries; its
index reconstruction asserts neither Borel summability nor control of
exponentially small sectors, and no numerical value of the eventual rounding
threshold. It proves no guessed OEIS recurrence, submitted no OEIS edit and
changed no repository file. Its uniform theorem concerns one source forest
and one target forest, not unions of differently coloured gap constraints.
[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101: Part~VII treats one such union, two
reciprocal fixed gaps marked separately (\cref{spf:cg:thm:main}).]
The first shape-dependent term for a shortest path that grows with $n$ is
left open; its short-path theorem is for fixed $L$, with constants not
controlled as $L\to\infty$, so it licenses no substitution $L=L(n)$; in the
directed model it proves cancellation only at the displayed order. The
correction boundaries of \cref{spf:mg:sec:moving} are kinks of correction
terms, not macroscopic phase transitions, and the cancellation at
$\sqrt2-1$ does not imply cancellation at later orders. Its inverse decimals
are not interval certificates; its rational test vectors need not be
realizable by forests, and the probability statements are asserted for actual
forests only; finitely many Bonferroni intervals do not prove the remainder.
The exact profile identity, the moment bound, connected-graph expansions,
Stirling's formula and Lambert-$W$ inversion are not advertised as new, and
Spahn--Zeilberger and Tauraso are credited.

\paragraph{Source and novelty ledger of manuscript~05, restated.}
The matching-of-tilings formula is credited to Spahn and
Zeilberger~\cite{spahn}, the fixed-gap dinner-table asymptotics to

to Parts~I and~II of this report; the OEIS sequence definitions are external.
Manuscript~05 claims as its own: arbitrary-forest uniformity, including
linearly many bounded components and vanishing leading density; the finite
locality cutoff, the finite coefficient formula and the sharpness of the
cutoff among actual forests; the highest-path sensitivity, the
reciprocal-integer boundaries and the cancellation at $\sqrt2-1$; the
leading absolute-model term of one fixed short path, its directed
cancellation and the one-target-path shape independence in its stated form;
and the moving-ray coefficients, index reconstruction and certificates. The
inequality behind its tree bound is classical and is credited in this write
(Remark~\ref{spf:mg:rem:penrose}).

Two of these carry a tempting false reading. The polynomial
$\mathsf B_J^{(\theta)}(u;\mga,\mgb)$ evaluated at the densities

polynomials are obtained only after re-expanding $e^{(\lambda-\theta)u}$ and

first order). And $\mathsf H_q$ is a polynomial in the target densities, not a
Charlier polynomial.

\section{Manuscript 05: the question, the sources, and the contribution boundary}
\label{spf:mg:sec:context}

The ProveIt report \emph{Unconditional all-orders asymptotics for two OEIS
permutation sequences} develops fixed-gap avoidance using stable path
forests \cite{mgProveIt}. Its Parts I and II prove all-orders asymptotics when
the position and value offsets are fixed. Part III proves a directed
rational-collapse and integrality statement. Those results are not claimed
again here. The present starting points are two questions that remain in
the inspected report's Section 22: growing gaps and component counts, and
the first shape-dependent term caused by a short path.
[Note of the batch-97 write: the inspected Section~22 is Part~II's
\cref{fga:sec:questions}; the growing gaps are Part~I's question~5
(\cref{spf:sec:questions}), listed there among the overlaps, and the
short-path term is its question~2.]

We work at repository revision
\begin{center}\small
\texttt{d1680cdfa40c44abf0825416b6f36a3e9ec2662c}.
\end{center}
The inspected article has blob identifier
\begin{center}\small
\texttt{73a53d9784260c2f0d083f2baa62a5535f6d6105}.
\end{center}
Pinning matters here: earlier versions explicitly conjectured statements
that Part III subsequently proved.

The public combinatorial setting predates that report. Spahn and
Zeilberger give the matching-of-tilings inclusion--exclusion formula for
fixed position and value gaps \cite{spahn}. Tauraso studies the rectangular
dinner-table problem and derives fixed-gap asymptotics \cite{tauraso}.
OEIS A189281 counts permutations avoiding
$\pi(i+2)-\pi(i)=2$; A110128 counts permutations avoiding
$|\pi(i+2)-\pi(i)|=2$ \cite{oeis189281,oeis110128}. The OEIS entries associate
higher expansions with guessed recurrence operators. The earlier ProveIt
report already supplies recurrence-independent fixed-gap expansions;
\emph{we do not present that fact as a new result of this manuscript}.

\subsection{What changes when gaps move}

A gap $r$ on $[n]$ decomposes the labels into $r$ paths. With fixed $r$,
the shortest path tends to infinity. With $r$ proportional to $n$, most
paths have bounded length, and the stable long-path polynomial is no
longer an adequate uniform description. The natural replacement is a
finite list of local statistics:
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf S_q(F)=\sum_{\text{paths of }F}\max(L-q,0),
 \qquad q\ge1,
 \label{spf:mg:eq:Sq-intro}
\end{equation}
where $L$ is the number of vertices in a path. Thus $\mathsf S_q$ counts
consecutive subpaths of \emph{$q$ edges}, not $q$ vertices.

The key observation is a filtration by the cost of connected overlap.
A cluster spanning $q$ edges cannot first affect an asymptotic coefficient
before order $q-1$. This leads both to a uniform theorem and to a sharp
formula for where new geometry becomes visible.

\subsection{Limits of the claims}

The literature review is targeted, not exhaustive. The statements proved
below are new additions to the inspected report, but the review does not
establish that every theorem is historically new. No particular guessed
OEIS recurrence is proved, no OEIS edit is submitted, and no repository
file is changed. The uniform theorem concerns one source forest and one
target forest; it does not cover an arbitrary union of differently colored
gap constraints. The first exponentially small shape-dependent term for a
shortest path that itself grows with $n$ is also left open.

\section{The model and the main uniform theorem}
\label{spf:mg:sec:main}

Let $\mathcal P$ and $\mathcal V$ be directed path forests on $n$ vertices. Every
component is a path with a consistent orientation, including paths with
one vertex. Choose a uniformly random bijection $\pi$ from the vertices
of $\mathcal P$ to those of $\mathcal V$. In the directed model an edge of $\mathcal P$
is counted when its image is a directed edge of $\mathcal V$. In the absolute
model it is counted when its image is an edge in either direction. Write
$\theta=1$ or $2$, respectively, and let $X$ be the resulting count.

Define
\begin{equation}
 G_{\mathcal P,\mathcal V}^{(\theta)}(u)=\E(1+u)^X,
 \qquad
 a_q=\frac{\mathsf S_q(\mathcal P)}n,\quad b_q=\frac{\mathsf S_q(\mathcal V)}n,
 \qquad \lambda=\theta a_1b_1.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:model}
\end{equation}
All densities lie in $[0,1]$. They may depend on $n$ in any way compatible
with actual forests. In particular $\lambda$ is an $n$-dependent leading
Poisson parameter, not the exact mean:
\begin{equation}
 \E X=\frac{\theta \mathsf S_1(\mathcal P)\mathsf S_1(\mathcal V)}{n(n-1)}
       =\frac{\lambda}{1-1/n},\qquad n\ge2.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:exact-mean}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform all-orders finite-locality theorem; manuscript~05]
\label{spf:mg:thm:main}
For $\theta\in\{1,2\}$ there are universal polynomials
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf B_j^{(\theta)}
 \in\Q[u,a_1,\ldots,a_{j+1},b_1,\ldots,b_{j+1}]
 \qquad(j\ge0),
 \label{spf:mg:eq:B-ring}
\end{equation}
with $\mathsf B_0=1$, symmetric under exchanging $\mga$ and $\mgb$, such that for
any fixed $M\ge0$ and $U<\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 G_{\mathcal P,\mathcal V}^{(\theta)}(u)
 =e^{\lambda u}\sum_{j=0}^{M}\frac{\mathsf B_j^{(\theta)}(u;\mga,\mgb)}{n^j}
   +\bigO_{M,U}(n^{-M-1}),\qquad |u|\le U.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:main}
\end{equation}
The error constant is independent of both forests. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 \deg_u \mathsf B_j\le2j,
 \qquad \mathsf B_j(0;\mga,\mgb)=0\quad(j\ge1).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:degree}
\end{equation}
No lower bound on a path length or on $a_1b_1$ is required. A finite
coefficient formula is given in \cref{spf:mg:eq:finite-B}.
\end{theorem}

The theorem is not a statement that fixed-gap coefficient polynomials can
simply be evaluated at growing offsets. Its parameters are the \emph{actual
local densities}, and its proof remains valid after the long-path
stabilization range disappears.

\begin{corollary}[Avoidance and the complete law; manuscript~05]
\label[corollary]{spf:mg:cor:law}
Uniformly over the same forests,
\begin{equation}
 \Pp(X=0)
 =e^{-\lambda}\sum_{j=0}^M n^{-j}\mathsf B_j^{(\theta)}(-1;\mga,\mgb)
  +\bigO_M(n^{-M-1}).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:avoid}
\end{equation}
Writing $\mathsf B_j(u)=\sum_{h=0}^{2j}\beta_{j,h}u^h$, define
\begin{equation}
 p_k^{[M]}=
 \sum_{j=0}^M n^{-j}\sum_{h=0}^{2j}\beta_{j,h}
 \frac{\partial^h}{\partial\lambda^h}
       \left(e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}\right).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:signed-law}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\ge0}|\Pp(X=k)-p_k^{[M]}|
 =\bigO_M(n^{-M-1}).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:l1}
\end{equation}
In particular
$\TV(\mathcal L(X),\operatorname{Poisson}(\lambda))=\bigO(n^{-1})$,
uniformly. The same conclusion holds with the exact mean in place of
$\lambda$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
Set $u=-1$ for avoidance. For the complete law, use the identity
$\partial_\lambda^h e^{\lambda(z-1)}=(z-1)^h e^{\lambda(z-1)}$.
On a fixed circle $|z|=R>1$, \cref{spf:mg:thm:main} bounds the remainder by
$C_Mn^{-M-1}$ because $|z-1|\le R+1$. Cauchy's estimate bounds its $k$th
coefficient by $C_Mn^{-M-1}R^{-k}$; summing proves \eqref{spf:mg:eq:l1}.
The derivative form in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:signed-law} is valid also at $\lambda=0$
and introduces no division by the parameter. Finally, Poisson variables
with parameters differing by $\delta$ can be coupled with mismatch
probability at most $|\delta|$, and \eqref{spf:mg:eq:exact-mean} gives
$\delta=\bigO(1/n)$.
\end{proof}

\section{Exact profiles and a moment bound independent of geometry}
\label{spf:mg:sec:profiles}

We use falling factorials $\fall{x}{v}=x(x-1)\cdots(x-v+1)$, with
$\fall{x}{0}=1$. A \emph{rod} of length $\ell$ is an interval of $\ell$
edges and $\ell+1$ vertices in a path.

\begin{definition}[Rod profile; manuscript~05]
A profile $\mgm=(m_1,m_2,\ldots)$ has finite support and nonnegative
integer entries. Put
\begin{equation}
 c=\sum_{\ell\ge1}m_\ell,\qquad
 k=\sum_{\ell\ge1}\ell m_\ell,\qquad
 v=k+c,\qquad
 d=k-c=\sum_{\ell\ge1}(\ell-1)m_\ell,
 \qquad \mgmf=\prod_{\ell\ge1}m_\ell!.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:profile}
\end{equation}
The quantity $d$ is the intrinsic defect. Let $C_F(\mgm)$ count
collections of vertex-disjoint rods in $F$ with this profile.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Exact profile identity; manuscript~05]
\label[proposition]{spf:mg:prop:exact}
For arbitrary path forests on $n$ vertices,
\begin{equation}
 G(u)=\sum_{\mgm:\,v\le n}
 \theta^c u^k C_{\mathcal P}(\mgm)C_{\mathcal V}(\mgm)
             \frac{\mgmf}{\fall nv}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:profile-id}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Second proof of Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:exact} (manuscript~05)]
Expand $(1+u)^X$ by marking a subset of the violating edges. The selected
source edges decompose into vertex-disjoint rods. Their images are rods
in the target forest with the same profile. Match rods of equal length in
$\mgmf$ ways, choose one of $\theta$ orientations for each rod, and map the
remaining $n-v$ isolated vertices arbitrarily. Each pair of configurations
therefore yields $\theta^c\mgmf(n-v)!$ bijections, with the selected subset
distinguished. Divide by $n!$ and sum. Unselected violations are allowed;
excluding them at this stage would invalidate the identity.
\end{proof}

At $u=-1$ this is the matching-of-tilings inclusion--exclusion formula of
\cite{spahn}, expressed for arbitrary path forests. Its general-$u$ form is
also used in \cite{mgProveIt}. We include the proof to keep the present
argument independent of any guessed recurrence.
[Note of the batch-97 write: this is Part~I's Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:exact},
which already holds for arbitrary position and value forests. Manuscript~05
indexes rods by their numbers of edges: its $m_\ell$ is Part~I's $b_{\ell+1}$,
and $\mgmf/\fall nv=(n-v)!\,\bfac/n!$.]

Let $\mu_k=\E\binom Xk=[u^k]G(u)$.
\begin{lemma}[Uniform factorial-moment bound; manuscript~05]
\label[lemma]{spf:mg:lem:moment}
For all forests, all $n\ge1$, and all $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 0\le\mu_k\le\frac{(\theta e^2)^k}{k!}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:moment-bound}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for $A=\theta e^2U$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{|u|\le U}\left|G(u)-\sum_{k=0}^{K}\mu_ku^k\right|
 \le e^A\frac{A^{K+1}}{(K+1)!}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:moment-tail}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Second proof of Lemma~\ref{spf:lem:moment-bound} (manuscript~05)]
Fix $k$ selected source edges, decomposing into $c$ rods and $v=k+c$
vertices. Their images can be assigned starts and directions in at most
$\theta^c n^c$ ways, even before requiring disjointness. For $v\le n$,
\begin{equation}
 \fall nv\ge(n/e)^v.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:fall-lower}
\end{equation}
Indeed the geometric mean of the largest $v$ factors in $n!$ is at least
that of all $n$ factors, while
$\log(n!)\ge\int_1^n\log t\,dt\ge n\log n-n$.
Thus the probability that every selected edge is a violation is at most
$\theta^c n^c/\fall nv\le\theta^k e^{2k}n^{-k}$.
There are at most $n^k/k!$ selected edge sets. Summing proves the bound;
if $v>n$ no such selected set occurs. The exponential-series tail gives
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:moment-tail}.
\end{proof}

This bound is available in the fixed-gap report, and is reproved here.
[Note of the batch-97 write: it is Part~I's
Lemma~\ref{spf:lem:moment-bound}, with the same proof.]
It is crucial that its constant does not deteriorate when the forest has
many short components.

\section{Connected overlaps and exact local cluster polynomials}
\label{spf:mg:sec:clusters}

The next construction replaces long-path stabilization by an exact local
identity. Temporarily label all $c$ rods individually, with lengths
$\mgl=(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_c)$. For an arbitrary placement of these rods in
$F$, its incompatibility graph joins two labels when their rods share a
vertex. Rods with identical occupied intervals are also incompatible.

\subsection{The connected graph weight}

For a graph $H$ on a nonempty label set define
\begin{equation}
 \phi(H)=
 \sum_{\substack{J\subseteq H\\J\text{ spanning and connected}}}
          (-1)^{|E(J)|}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:phi}
\end{equation}
For a singleton graph the sum equals $1$; for a disconnected graph with
more than one vertex it is zero. For a nonempty set of rod labels $B$,
let
\begin{equation}
 K_F(\ell_B)=\sum_{\text{placements of the rods in }B}\phi(H).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:K}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Locality of a connected cluster; manuscript~05]
\label[lemma]{spf:mg:lem:cluster-local}
There are integers $\kappa_{\ell_B,q}$, independent of $F$, such that
\begin{equation}
 K_F(\ell_B)=\sum_{q=1}^{k_B}\mathsf S_q(F)\kappa_{\ell_B,q},
 \qquad k_B=\sum_{i\in B}\ell_i.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:K-local}
\end{equation}
In particular $K_F(\ell_B)=n\kappa_{\ell_B}(\mga)$, where
$\kappa_{\ell_B}(\mga)=\sum_{q\le k_B}a_q\kappa_{\ell_B,q}$.
For a single rod of length $\ell$, $\kappa_{(\ell)}(\mga)=a_\ell$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
Only connected incompatibility graphs contribute. In a connected
placement the union of the rods is a single interval of some edge length
$q\le k_B$. Translate its left endpoint to zero. There are only finitely
many normalized placements of the given labeled rods with this union;
sum their integer weights \eqref{spf:mg:eq:phi} to define
$\kappa_{\ell_B,q}$. Each normalized pattern has exactly $\mathsf S_q(F)$
translations into $F$. This proves the identity.
\end{proof}

For $h\ge0$ define the merger polynomial
\begin{equation}
 F_{\mgl,h}(\mga)=
 \sum_{\substack{\rho\text{ a set partition of }[c]\\c-|\rho|=h}}
       \prod_{B\in\rho}\kappa_{\ell_B}(\mga),
 \qquad
 F_{\mgl}(\mga,x)=\sum_{h\ge0}x^hF_{\mgl,h}(\mga).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:F}
\end{equation}
Here a block of $m$ rods costs $m-1$ mergers. The empty list has
$F_{\varnothing}=1$. The leading term is
$F_{\mgl,0}(\mga)=\prod_{i=1}^c a_{\ell_i}$.

\begin{proposition}[Exact labeled placement polynomial; manuscript~05]
\label[proposition]{spf:mg:prop:placement}
For every profile $\mgm$ and any labeling $\mgl$ of its rods,
\begin{equation}
 C_F(\mgm)=\frac{n^c}{\mgmf}F_{\mgl}(\mga,1/n).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:placement}
\end{equation}
This identity holds even if the profile cannot fit in the forest, in
which case its right side vanishes.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
For each arbitrary placement, the indicator of disjointness is the
product over all rod pairs of $1-\ind\{\text{overlap}\}$. Expand the
product as a sum over graphs on the labels. Group each graph by its
connected components. The sum belonging to a block is exactly
$K_F(\ell_B)$; different blocks place their rods independently because
there is no imposed cross-block edge in that graph term. Inserting
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:K-local} gives $n^cF_{\mgl}(\mga,1/n)$ labeled disjoint
placements. Dividing by $\mgmf$ removes the labels on rods of equal length.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A coefficient bound with controlled dependence on the moment order}

\begin{lemma}[Connected-weight bound; manuscript~05]
\label[lemma]{spf:mg:lem:tree}
If $\tau(H)$ is the number of spanning trees of a finite graph $H$, then
$|\phi(H)|\le\tau(H)$, with both sides interpreted as $1$ for a singleton.
Consequently, for realizable densities, a rod list of total edge length
$k\ge1$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 |F_{\mgl,h}(\mga)|\le\frac{k^{3h}}{h!}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:F-bound}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
For completeness, the first inequality follows by deletion and
contraction. Let $w(H)=(-1)^{|V(H)|-1}\phi(H)$ for connected graphs and
$w(H)=0$ for disconnected graphs with at least two vertices. Separating
connected spanning subgraphs according as they contain an edge $e$ gives
$w(H)=w(H-e)+w(H/e)$. Parallel edges after contraction may be simplified:
the alternating sum over a nonempty subset of parallel edges is $-1$,
the same as for one edge. Induction gives $w(H)\ge0$. The spanning-tree
recurrence for the contraction with multiplicities, and the fact that
simplification cannot increase its tree count, then give $w(H)\le\tau(H)$.
Trees and singleton graphs start the induction.

For the second inequality, bound the connected weight for each block by
a sum over spanning trees. The resulting collection across blocks is a
forest with $h$ edges on the $c$ rod labels. Root each tree. Its root rod
has at most $n$ placements. Given a parent's placement, a child has at
most $\ell_i+\ell_j+1\le2k$ placements overlapping it. Hence a specified
forest has at most $n^{c-h}(2k)^h$ compatible placements. There are at
most $\binom{\binom c2}{h}$ such forests. Divide by $n^{c-h}$, as in
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:F}, to obtain
\[
 |F_{\mgl,h}(\mga)|\le(2k)^h\binom{\binom c2}{h}
 \le\frac{k^{3h}}{h!},
\]
since $c\le k$. The argument bounds sums of absolute weights, so
cancellation presents no problem.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Penrose's tree-graph inequality]
\label[remark]{spf:mg:rem:penrose}
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97; a note of the write.] The first inequality
of \cref{spf:mg:lem:tree}, $|\phi(H)|\le\tau(H)$ for the alternating sum
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:phi} over connected spanning subgraphs, is Penrose's
tree-graph inequality~\cite{penrose}, a classical tool of cluster
expansions with hard-core interactions. Manuscript~05 proves it by deletion
and contraction but does not cite it, and its source ledger does not list it.
Only the second inequality, the bound \eqref{spf:mg:eq:F-bound} on the
merger polynomials, is specific to Part~IV.
\end{remark}

\section{A finite formula for every correction polynomial}
\label{spf:mg:sec:coefficients}

Put
\begin{equation}
 D_v(x)=\prod_{i=0}^{v-1}(1-ix),\qquad
 d_j(v)=[x^j]D_v(x)^{-1}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:Dv}
\end{equation}
For fixed $j$, $d_j(v)$ is a rational polynomial in $v$ of degree at most
$2j$. This follows by expanding
$\log D_v(x)^{-1}=\sum_{m\ge1}x^m\sum_{i=0}^{v-1}i^m/m$;
each power sum is polynomial of degree $m+1$.
Also, for $v\le2k$,
\begin{equation}
 0\le d_j(v)\le(2k^2)^j,
 \label{spf:mg:eq:d-bound}
\end{equation}
because a complete homogeneous polynomial is bounded by the corresponding
power of the sum of its nonnegative arguments.

Substitution of \eqref{spf:mg:eq:placement} into \eqref{spf:mg:eq:profile-id} shows that
a profile contributes
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\theta^c u^k}{\mgmf}\,
 x^d\frac{F_{\mgl}(\mga,x)F_{\mgl}(\mgb,x)}{D_{k+c}(x)},
 \qquad x=1/n.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:normalized-profile}
\end{equation}
The total cost of a term is therefore
\begin{equation}
 J=d+h+h'+j:
 \quad\text{intrinsic defect, source mergers, target mergers, denominator order.}
 \label{spf:mg:eq:cost}
\end{equation}

For any fixed $J,k\ge0$ define the finite polynomial
\begin{equation}
 R_{J,k}(\mga,\mgb)=
 \sum_{\mgm:\,\sum\ell m_\ell=k}\frac{\theta^c}{\mgmf}
 \sum_{d+h+h'+j=J}
   F_{\mgl,h}(\mga)F_{\mgl,h'}(\mgb)d_j(k+c).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:R}
\end{equation}
The empty profile supplies $R_{0,0}=1$ and $R_{J,0}=0$ for $J>0$.
The expression is independent of the chosen rod labels.

\begin{theorem}[Finite coefficient extraction and locality; manuscript~05]
\label{spf:mg:thm:finite}
The formal series
\begin{equation}
 e^{-\lambda u}\sum_{k\ge0}R_{J,k}(\mga,\mgb)u^k
 \label{spf:mg:eq:B-infinite}
\end{equation}
is a polynomial of degree at most $2J$. It is $\mathsf B_J$ in
\cref{spf:mg:thm:main}, and it has the finite formula
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \mathsf B_J(u;\mga,\mgb)=
 \sum_{q=0}^{2J}u^q\sum_{k=0}^{q}
       \frac{(-\lambda)^{q-k}}{(q-k)!}R_{J,k}(\mga,\mgb).}
 \label{spf:mg:eq:finite-B}
\end{equation}
Only the densities $a_q,b_q$ with $q\le J+1$ occur.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
Fix a term in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:R} with cost \eqref{spf:mg:eq:cost}. Call rods with
$\ell\ge2$ long rods. There are at most $d$ long rods, and their total
edge length is at most $2d$, because $\ell\le2(\ell-1)$ for $\ell\ge2$.
A partition with $h$ mergers has at most $2h$ labels in nonsingleton
blocks: each block of size $m\ge2$ satisfies $m\le2(m-1)$.
The source and target partitions thus involve at most $2(h+h')$ rod
labels in their nonsingleton blocks.

Mark all long rods and all unit rods appearing in a nonsingleton block
of either partition. Once the finite marked pattern has been fixed,
every unmarked unit rod is a singleton in both partitions and contributes
$\theta a_1b_1u=\lambda u$. Summing an arbitrary number $t$ of these
unmarked rods with the factorial normalization gives $e^{\lambda u}$.
The only remaining dependence on $t$ is polynomial: the denominator
coefficient $d_j(k+c)$ has degree at most $2j$ in $t$. Summing
$t^{\underline m}(\lambda u)^t/t!$ gives
$(\lambda u)^m e^{\lambda u}$, so this dependence contributes at most
$2j$ additional powers of $u$.

More explicitly, summing over choices of marked labels converts their
multiplicities to falling factorials; division by the original rod
factorials leaves the factorial of the unmarked count. Taking the union
of source-marked and target-marked unit rods avoids any division by
$a_1$ or $b_1$. The argument is therefore a polynomial identity, including
when either leading edge density vanishes. The remaining $u$-degree is
at most
$2d+2h+2h'+2j=2J$. This proves the polynomial assertion and then the
finite extraction formula.

For locality, consider a connected source block with $m$ rods, intrinsic
defect $d_B$, and total edge length $k_B=d_B+m$. Its local polynomial
uses $a_q$ only for
\[
 q\le k_B=d_B+(m-1)+1\le d+h+1\le J+1.
\]
The same argument applies to target blocks. The denominator introduces
no new densities. Finally, swapping forests leaves \eqref{spf:mg:eq:R}
unchanged, and the $u^0$ coefficient vanishes for $J>0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the finite formula does and does not say; manuscript~05]
The intermediate moments in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:finite-B} go as far as $k=2J$,
and a straightforward computation may temporarily use local symbols
through that weight. The theorem proves cancellation of symbols beyond
$J+1$ in the final answer. It is not necessary to assume this cancellation
when running the coefficient engine.
\end{remark}

\section{Uniform remainder estimates: proof of the main theorem}
\label{spf:mg:sec:remainder}

Formal extraction alone would not justify avoidance at $u=-1$.
We now control both the Taylor remainder for low moments and the tail of
all higher moments.

\begin{lemma}[Low-moment Taylor estimate; manuscript~05]
\label[lemma]{spf:mg:lem:lowmoment}
For every fixed $M\ge0$ there is a constant $C_M$ such that, for $k\ge1$
and $4k^3\le n$,
\begin{equation}
 \left|\mu_k-\sum_{J=0}^M n^{-J}R_{J,k}(\mga,\mgb)\right|
 \le C_M\frac{\theta^k(1+k)^{3M+3}}{k!}\,n^{-M-1}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:low-moment}
\end{equation}
For every fixed $J$,
\begin{equation}
 |R_{J,k}(\mga,\mgb)|
 \le C_J\frac{\theta^k(1+k)^{3J}}{k!}
 \label{spf:mg:eq:R-bound}
\end{equation}
on realizable densities, with constants independent of the forests.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
We give the estimates explicitly enough to identify the uniformity.
By \cref{spf:mg:lem:tree} and \eqref{spf:mg:eq:d-bound}, the absolute coefficient
series of the kernel in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:normalized-profile}, after removing
$x^d\theta^c/\mgmf$, is bounded coefficientwise by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\exp(2k^3x)}{1-2k^2x}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:majorant}
\end{equation}
For $0\le x\le1/(4k^3)$ its tail above degree $m$ is at most
$C_m(k^3x)^{m+1}$. For example, expand the exponential and geometric
series; at total degree $p$ their coefficient is at most
$C(2k^3)^p$ times a fixed harmless constant, and the remaining geometric
ratio is at most $1/2$. Equivalently one may bound the two Taylor tails
separately. Constants here may depend on the fixed cutoff $m$, not on
$k$ or the forest.

The profile multiplicity sum at given $k$ and $d=k-c$ is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\substack{\mgm:\,\sum\ell m_\ell=k\\\sum m_\ell=k-d}}
        \frac1{\mgmf}
 =\frac{\binom{k-1}{d}}{(k-d)!}
 \le\frac{k^{2d}}{d!\,k!}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:profile-sum}
\end{equation}
The equality counts ordered compositions of $k$ into $k-d$ positive
parts and divides by $(k-d)!$. The inequality follows from
$\binom{k-1}{d}\le k^d/d!$ and
$k!/(k-d)!\le k^d$.

For $d\le M$, use the kernel remainder after order $M-d$, multiply by
$x^d$, and sum profiles using \eqref{spf:mg:eq:profile-sum} and
$\theta^c\le\theta^k$. The result is at most
\[
 \frac{C_M\theta^k}{k!}x^{M+1}
 \sum_{d=0}^{M}\frac{k^{2d+3(M+1-d)}}{d!}
 \le\frac{C'_M\theta^k(1+k)^{3M+3}}{k!}x^{M+1}.
\]
For $d>M$, bound the whole kernel by \eqref{spf:mg:eq:majorant}; it is bounded
by an absolute constant under $4k^3x\le1$. Then the exponential tail
in $k^2x$ from \eqref{spf:mg:eq:profile-sum} bounds these profiles by
\[
 \frac{C\theta^k}{k!}e^{k^2x}
       \frac{(k^2x)^{M+1}}{(M+1)!},
\]
which is smaller than the required bound. For these low moments every
profile has $v\le2k\le n$, so no inadmissible denominator was introduced.
This proves \eqref{spf:mg:eq:low-moment}. The same argument at one fixed
coefficient, rather than at a tail, gives \eqref{spf:mg:eq:R-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{spf:mg:thm:main} (manuscript~05)]
For sufficiently large $n$, choose
\begin{equation}
 K_n=\left\lfloor\frac{(M+3)\log n}{\log\log n}\right\rfloor.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:cutoff}
\end{equation}
Then $4K_n^3\le n$. Apply \cref{spf:mg:lem:lowmoment}, multiply by $|u|^k$,
and sum over $k\le K_n$. The series
$\sum_k(\theta U)^k(1+k)^{3M+3}/k!$ converges, so the accumulated error
is $\bigO_{M,U}(n^{-M-1})$, with a forest-independent constant.

The actual moment tail is bounded by \eqref{spf:mg:eq:moment-tail}.
Since $K_n\log K_n=(M+3+\smallo(1))\log n$, this tail is
$n^{-M-3+\smallo(1)}$. The omitted tails of the finitely many coefficient
series $\sum_kR_{J,k}u^k$ have the same factorial decay by
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:R-bound}. They can therefore be reinstated within the error
$\bigO_{M,U}(n^{-M-1})$. Finally, \cref{spf:mg:thm:finite} identifies each
complete coefficient series as $e^{\lambda u}\mathsf B_J(u)$. All constants
used above depend only on $M,U$, and the two possible values of $\theta$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A useful comparison consequence}

\begin{corollary}[Finite-locality comparison; manuscript~05]
\label[corollary]{spf:mg:cor:comparison}
If two forest pairs on $n$ vertices have the same $a_q,b_q$ for
$1\le q\le M+1$, then their probability generating functions and complete
laws differ by $\bigO_{M,U}(n^{-M-1})$ and
$\bigO_M(n^{-M-1})$ in the respective norms of
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:main} and \eqref{spf:mg:eq:l1}. More generally, if the common leading
parameter is fixed and every relevant density differs by at most
$\varepsilon$, the coefficient differences are $\bigO_M(\varepsilon)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
Apply the theorem to both pairs. For the last assertion use bounded
first derivatives of finitely many polynomials on the density cube.
If the leading parameter itself differs, its exponential factor must
also be included; it is not suppressed by a power of $n$.
\end{proof}

\section{Explicit first and second corrections}
\label{spf:mg:sec:explicit}

The first two orders depend on only six local densities. To keep the
formulas legible, write
\begin{equation}
 a=a_1,\quad c=a_2,\quad e=a_3,
 \qquad b=b_1,\quad d=b_2,\quad f=b_3,
 \qquad \lambda=\theta ab,
 \label{spf:mg:eq:abc}
\end{equation}
and introduce
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal A=a+2c,\qquad \mathcal B=b+2d,
 \qquad \mathcal R=a+6c+3e,\qquad \mathcal S=b+6d+3f.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:invariants}
\end{equation}
The letter $d$ in this section is the target density $b_2$, not the
profile defect of \eqref{spf:mg:eq:profile}.

Define three symmetric polynomials
\begin{align}
 C={}&2\lambda^2-\frac{\theta^2}{2}
              (a^2\mathcal B+b^2\mathcal A)+\theta cd,
 \label{spf:mg:eq:C}\\
 D={}&\frac{\theta^2}{2}
       \left[22a^2b^2-6(a^2\mathcal B+b^2\mathcal A)+\mathcal A\mathcal B\right]
          +3\theta cd,
 \label{spf:mg:eq:D}\\
 E={}&\theta^3\left[
       \frac{28}{3}a^3b^3-4ab(a^2\mathcal B+b^2\mathcal A)
       +ab\mathcal A\mathcal B+\frac{a^3\mathcal S+b^3\mathcal R}{3}\right]
       \notag\\
    &+\theta^2\left[6abcd-2ac(d+f)-2bd(c+e)\right]
       +\theta ef.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:E}
\end{align}

\begin{proposition}[Two explicit orders; manuscript~05]
\label[proposition]{spf:mg:prop:first-two}
For both orientations,
\begin{align}
 \mathsf B_1(u)&=\lambda u+Cu^2,
 \label{spf:mg:eq:B1}\\
 \mathsf B_2(u)&=\lambda u+(D+\lambda^2/2)u^2
                   +(E+\lambda C)u^3+(C^2/2)u^4.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:B2}
\end{align}
Equivalently, choosing the logarithm continuous from $u=0$, on every
fixed compact $u$-disk and for sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation}
 \log G(u)=\lambda u+
 \frac{\lambda u+Cu^2}{n}+
 \frac{\lambda u+Du^2+Eu^3}{n^2}+\bigO_U(n^{-3}).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:logG}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
We give small-profile formulas that permit a direct check, rather than
requiring trust in a symbolic output. In a forest with densities $a,c,e$,
the counts of one, two, and three disjoint unit rods, normalized by
$n,n^2,n^3$, are respectively
\begin{equation}
 a,\qquad
 \frac{a^2}{2}-\frac{x\mathcal A}{2},\qquad
 \frac{a^3}{6}-\frac{xa\mathcal A}{2}+\frac{x^2\mathcal R}{3},
 \qquad x=1/n.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:small-tilings}
\end{equation}
For two unit rods the identity is
$C_F(2,0,\ldots)=\binom{na}{2}-nc$.
For three, inclusion--exclusion on their overlapping pairs gives the last
expression. Alternatively it follows from the path-polynomial recurrence
in \cref{spf:mg:sec:algorithm}. The normalized count of one unit rod and one
two-edge rod is
\begin{equation}
 ac-2x(c+e),
 \label{spf:mg:eq:mixed-rods}
\end{equation}
because the two rods can overlap in a two-edge interval in two positions,
or meet at an endpoint in a three-edge interval in two positions. A
single three-edge rod has normalized count $e$.

For instance the exact second factorial moment is
\begin{equation}
 \mu_2=
 \frac{\theta^2(n^2a^2-n\mathcal A)(n^2b^2-n\mathcal B)}{2\fall n4}
 +\frac{\theta n^2cd}{\fall n3}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:mu2-exact}
\end{equation}
The three terms for $\mu_3$ correspond to three unit rods, a unit rod
and a two-edge rod, and a single three-edge rod. Substitute
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:small-tilings}--\eqref{spf:mg:eq:mixed-rods} in
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:profile-id}, expand the denominators through order two, and
remove $e^{\lambda u}$ as in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:finite-B}. This gives
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:C}--\eqref{spf:mg:eq:B2}. At order two the four-unit-rod contribution
supplies the coefficient $C^2/2$; it follows either by the same finite
extraction or by separating its two cost-one marked patterns. The
shipped symbolic check independently performs the full extraction
through the needed fourth moment, verifies every coefficient, and checks
this quartic identity. Finally expand the logarithm of
$1+\mathsf B_1/n+\mathsf B_2/n^2$ to obtain \eqref{spf:mg:eq:logG}. The uniform approximation
to $e^{\lambda u}$ guarantees a nonzero generating function on each
fixed disk once $n$ is sufficiently large, so this logarithm exists there.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What in \cref{spf:mg:prop:first-two} is computer-verified]
\label[remark]{spf:mg:rem:first-two-status}
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97; a note of the write.] The printed proof
supplies the small tiling counts \eqref{spf:mg:eq:small-tilings},
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:mixed-rods} and the exact second moment
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:mu2-exact}. From them $\mathsf B_1=\lambda u+Cu^2$ follows by
hand; the intake recomputed it, and at the fixed-offset densities it
reproduces \eqref{spf:mg:eq:fixed-check} and Part~I's \eqref{spf:eq:B1}. The
second-order polynomials $D$ and $E$ of \eqref{spf:mg:eq:D}--\eqref{spf:mg:eq:E}
and the quartic coefficient $C^2/2$ of \eqref{spf:mg:eq:B2} are \emph{not}
derived in print: they rest on manuscript~05's program
\texttt{symbolic\_checks.py} (shipped as
\path{code/04-moving-gaps-symbolic_checks.py}). That program runs the finite
extraction \eqref{spf:mg:eq:finite-B} with symbolic densities
$a,c,e,b,d,f$ and symbolic $\theta$ over every rod profile of defect at most
two through the fourth moment, with the profile counts of the coefficient
engine \texttt{local\_expansions.py}, and asserts in exact SymPy arithmetic:
the vanishing of the coefficients of $\mathsf B_1$ and $\mathsf B_2$ above
degrees $2$ and $4$; the coefficients $\lambda$ and $C$ of $\mathsf B_1$; the
quartic coefficient $C^2/2$; $\partial\mathsf B_2/\partial a_3=u^3\mathsf
H_3^{(\theta)}(\mgb)$, with no $a_3$ in the other coefficients; the compact
forms of $D$ and $E$; and every coefficient of $\mathsf B_2$. It sets
$a_4=b_4=0$, which is no restriction because $\mathsf B_2$ involves no
density beyond $a_3,b_3$ (\cref{spf:mg:thm:finite}). The engine's profile
counts are checked separately against independent finite path products
(1618 assertions of \cref{spf:mg:tab:checks}), and the standard-library
verifier checks the first correction and the quartic identity at sixteen
rational density vectors each. Thus \eqref{spf:mg:eq:B2}, and the terms
$n^{-2}$ of \eqref{spf:mg:eq:logG}, are exact computer-algebra identities
under the proved extraction formula, not hand derivations. The batch-97
write reran the program on a copy (all assertions passed; see the report
README); an independent hand derivation of $D$ and $E$ is not available.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Consistency with fixed gaps}

For fixed position and value gaps $r,s$ and fixed $q$, eventually
$a_q=1-qr/n$ and $b_q=1-qs/n$. Inserting these \emph{$n$-dependent}
densities into the formula gives
\begin{equation}
 \Pp(X=0)=e^{-\theta}
 \left(1+\frac{\theta(r+s-\theta)}n+\bigO(n^{-2})\right).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:fixed-check}
\end{equation}
Indeed $\lambda=\theta-\theta(r+s)/n+\bigO(n^{-2})$, whereas
$\mathsf B_1(-1)$ at $a=b=c=d=1$ is $-\theta^2$.
For $\theta=2$ and $r=s$, \eqref{spf:mg:eq:fixed-check} is the familiar
$4(r-1)/n$ correction in the fixed-gap setting \cite{tauraso,mgProveIt}.
This is a consistency check, not the main new regime.

\subsection{The matching regime}

When $r,s\ge n/2$, both offset forests consist of isolated vertices and
single edges. Let $m_P=n-r$ and $m_V=n-s$. Only unit rods are possible,
so the whole finite law has factorial moments
\begin{equation}
 \mu_k=\frac{\theta^k\fall{m_P}k\fall{m_V}k}
                 {k!\fall n{2k}},
 \qquad 0\le k\le\min(m_P,m_V),
 \label{spf:mg:eq:matching-moments}
\end{equation}
and $\mu_k=0$ thereafter. Thus
\begin{equation}
 \Pp(X=0)=\sum_{k=0}^{\min(m_P,m_V)}(-1)^k\mu_k.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:matching-avoid}
\end{equation}
This finite formula is also an independent way to test the moving-gap
expansion at the boundary $r=s=n/2$.

\section{The longest visible path: an exact sensitivity formula}
\label{spf:mg:sec:sensitivity}

The locality upper bound becomes more informative when one can determine
whether the last permissible density actually occurs. For a formal target
density sequence define
\begin{equation}
 \mathscr B(t)=\sum_{\ell\ge1}b_\ell t^\ell,
 \qquad
 \mathsf H_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb)=
 [t^q]\frac{\theta\mathscr B(t)}{1+\theta\mathscr B(t)}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:H}
\end{equation}
Only $b_1,\ldots,b_q$ matter. Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf H_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb)=
 \sum_{m=1}^q(-1)^{m-1}\theta^m
 \sum_{\substack{\ell_1+\cdots+\ell_m=q\\\ell_i\ge1}}
           b_{\ell_1}\cdots b_{\ell_m}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:H-composition}
\end{equation}
The first examples are
\begin{align}
 \mathsf H_2&=\theta b_2-\theta^2b_1^2,\notag\\
 \mathsf H_3&=\theta b_3-2\theta^2b_1b_2+\theta^3b_1^3,\notag\\
 \mathsf H_4&=\theta b_4-\theta^2(2b_1b_3+b_2^2)
                  +3\theta^3b_1^2b_2-\theta^4b_1^4.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:H-small}
\end{align}

\begin{theorem}[Highest-path sensitivity; manuscript~05]
\label{spf:mg:thm:sensitivity}
For every $q\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \frac{\partial \mathsf B_{q-1}^{(\theta)}}{\partial a_q}
     =u^qH_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb),\qquad
 \frac{\partial \mathsf B_{q-1}^{(\theta)}}{\partial b_q}
     =u^qH_q^{(\theta)}(\mga).}
 \label{spf:mg:eq:sensitivity}
\end{equation}
In particular $\mathsf B_{q-1}$ is separately linear in $a_q$ and $b_q$, and its
mixed derivative with respect to them is $\theta u^q$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
A source block whose cluster uses $a_q$ must span a $q$-edge interval.
By the locality proof its cost $d_B+(|B|-1)$ is at least $q-1$.
At total order $q-1$ this consumes the entire budget. Therefore every
other rod is a unit rod isolated in both partitions; there are no target
mergers and no positive-order denominator term. The distinguished source
block has total rod edge length exactly $q$.

A connected collection of intervals whose union has edge length equal
to the sum of their edge lengths has no repeated occupied edge. Its
intervals meet only at endpoints and form a chain covering the union.
If it has $m$ rods, their left-to-right lengths form an ordered composition
$\ell_1+\cdots+\ell_m=q$. The incompatibility graph is a path, hence its
connected weight is $(-1)^{m-1}$. The target partition has singleton
blocks, contributing $b_{\ell_1}\cdots b_{\ell_m}$, and orientation
contributes $\theta^m$.

The labeling factorials convert the chain placements into precisely the
ordered compositions in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:H-composition}. Their $u$-weight is
$u^q$. All remaining unit rods sum to $e^{\lambda u}$, which cancels in
the definition of $\mathsf B_{q-1}$. Because $q\ge2$, $\lambda$ is independent
of $a_q$. This proves the first identity; symmetry proves the second.
The only term of $\mathsf H_q(\mgb)$ containing $b_q$ is $\theta b_q$, proving
the mixed derivative assertion.
\end{proof}

One can record the entire dependence on the last two density variables as
\begin{equation}
 u^q\left[a_qH_q(\mgb)+b_qH_q(\mga)-\theta a_qb_q\right],
 \label{spf:mg:eq:last-dependence}
\end{equation}
plus a polynomial involving only densities with indices below $q$.
This identity is particularly useful at a geometric boundary.

\section{A fixed short path: the first shape-dependent term}
\label{spf:mg:sec:shortpath}

We now answer the fixed-length part of the short-path question posed in
\cite{mgProveIt}. The comparison keeps the total number of vertices and the
numbers of paths fixed; it changes only their lengths.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp absolute-model effect of one fixed short path; manuscript~05]
\label{spf:mg:thm:shortpath}
Fix integers $r\ge2$, $s\ge1$, and $L\ge1$. For each $n$, let
$\mathcal P_n^{\mathrm{short}}$ have $r$ paths, one with exactly $L$ vertices
and all the others with lengths tending to infinity. Let
$\mathcal P_n^{\mathrm{long}}$ have $r$ paths, all with lengths tending to
infinity. Both forests have $n$ vertices. Let $\mathcal V_n$ have $s$ paths,
all with lengths tending to infinity, also on $n$ vertices.

In the absolute model,
\begin{equation}
 G_{\mathrm{short}}^{(2)}(u)-G_{\mathrm{long}}^{(2)}(u)
 =\frac{2(-1)^Le^{2u}u^{L+1}}{n^{L+1}}
       +\bigO_U(n^{-L-2}),\qquad |u|\le U.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:short-G}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Pp(X_{\mathrm{short}}^{(2)}=0)
 -\Pp(X_{\mathrm{long}}^{(2)}=0)
 =-\frac{2e^{-2}}{n^{L+1}}+\bigO(n^{-L-2}).}
 \label{spf:mg:eq:short-avoid}
\end{equation}
In the directed model the difference of generating functions is instead
$\bigO_U(n^{-L-2})$; the displayed leading absolute-model order cancels.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
For each fixed $q$, once all growing paths exceed $q$ vertices,
\begin{equation}
 a_q^{\mathrm{short}}-a_q^{\mathrm{long}}
      =\frac{(q-L)_+}{n},
 \qquad
 a_q^{\mathrm{long}}=1-\frac{rq}{n},\qquad
 b_q=1-\frac{sq}{n}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:short-densities}
\end{equation}
For $q\le L$ the two source densities coincide. Their first difference
is $1/n$ at $q=L+1$. Their leading parameters $\lambda$ are equal
exactly because the number of edges is the same.

Set $q=L+1$. The terms $\mathsf B_j$ for $j<q-1$ agree by locality. At $j=q-1$,
\cref{spf:mg:thm:sensitivity} gives the exact difference
$u^qH_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb)/n$. At any higher fixed order the polynomial
coefficient difference is $\bigO(1/n)$ by \eqref{spf:mg:eq:short-densities}.
Using the main theorem through order $q$ gives
\begin{equation}
 G_{\mathrm{short}}^{(\theta)}(u)-G_{\mathrm{long}}^{(\theta)}(u)
 =n^{-q}e^{\lambda u}u^qH_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb)
    +\bigO_U(n^{-q-1}).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:short-general}
\end{equation}
All finite target densities are $1+\bigO(1/n)$. At the constant density
sequence $\mgb=(1,1,\ldots)$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\theta\mathscr B(t)}{1+\theta\mathscr B(t)}
 =\frac{\theta t}{1+(\theta-1)t},\qquad
 \mathsf H_q^{(\theta)}(1,1,\ldots)
       =\theta(1-\theta)^{q-1}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:H-long}
\end{equation}
Also $\lambda=\theta+\bigO(1/n)$. For $\theta=2$ this proves
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:short-G}; substitution $u=-1$ gives the negative coefficient
in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:short-avoid}. For $\theta=1$ and $q\ge2$,
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:H-long} vanishes, leaving the stated smaller error.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Multiplicity and scope; manuscript~05]
If exactly $h$ short paths have the same minimal fixed vertex length
$L$, while all other paths are longer than $L$ and the remaining finite
local densities have the corresponding fixed-count behavior, their
contribution at the first order is $h$ times \eqref{spf:mg:eq:short-G}.
Paths of larger fixed length first enter later. The theorem is proved
for fixed $L$; its error constants are not controlled as $L\to\infty$.
It therefore does not settle the logarithmically growing short-path
question by substituting $L=L(n)$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The preceding remark, made precise]
\label[remark]{spf:mg:rem:multiplicity}
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97; the precise hypothesis and the argument are
the write's.] The preceding remark leaves its hypothesis on ``the remaining
finite local densities'' informal. A precise version: fix integers $s\ge1$,
$L\ge1$, a finite set $I_0$ of integers $\ge L$ containing $L$, and
multiplicities $h_\ell\ge1$ ($\ell\in I_0$) with
$r>\sum_\ell h_\ell$. Let $\mathcal P_n^{\mathrm{short}}$ have $r$ paths on
$n$ vertices, exactly $h_\ell$ of them with $\ell$ vertices for each
$\ell\in I_0$ and the others with lengths tending to infinity, and let
$\mathcal P_n^{\mathrm{long}}$ and $\mathcal V_n$ be as in
\cref{spf:mg:thm:shortpath}. Then for every fixed $q$, eventually
\[
 a_q^{\mathrm{short}}-a_q^{\mathrm{long}}
 =\frac1n\sum_{\ell\in I_0}h_\ell(q-\ell)_+,
\]
so the densities agree for $q\le L$, differ by exactly $h_L/n$ at $q=L+1$,
and differ by $\bigO(1/n)$ at every fixed $q$; both forests have $n-r$ edges,
so $\lambda$ is the same. The proof of \cref{spf:mg:thm:shortpath} then
applies verbatim: $\mathsf B_j$ agree for $j<L$, the affine dependence of
$\mathsf B_L$ on $a_{L+1}$ (\cref{spf:mg:thm:sensitivity}) gives the
difference $(h_L/n)u^{L+1}\mathsf H_{L+1}^{(\theta)}(\mgb)$, and every later
$\mathsf B_j$, a polynomial with bounded gradient on the density cube, changes
by $\bigO(1/n)$. Hence
\[
 G_{\mathrm{short}}^{(\theta)}(u)-G_{\mathrm{long}}^{(\theta)}(u)
 =h_L\,n^{-L-1}e^{\lambda u}u^{L+1}\mathsf H_{L+1}^{(\theta)}(\mgb)
  +\bigO_U(n^{-L-2}),
\]
which in the absolute model is $h_L$ times \eqref{spf:mg:eq:short-G}, with
avoidance difference $-2h_Le^{-2}n^{-L-1}+\bigO(n^{-L-2})$, and in the
directed model is $\bigO_U(n^{-L-2})$. Paths of the larger lengths in
$I_0$ do not enter at this order.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Fixed-offset coefficients survive down to paths of fixed length]
\label[remark]{spf:mg:rem:threshold}
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97; this consequence of
\cref{spf:mg:thm:main,spf:mg:thm:shortpath} is the write's, not manuscript~05's.]
Fix $\theta$, $r$, $s$ and an order $M\ge1$. Let $\mathcal P_n$ have $r$
paths and $\mathcal V_n$ have $s$ paths on $n$ vertices, every path having at
least $M$ vertices. Then, uniformly on $|u|\le U$,
\[
 G^{(\theta)}_{\mathcal P_n,\mathcal V_n}(u)
 =e^{\theta u}\sum_{J=0}^{M}\frac{B_J^{(\theta)}(u;r,s)}{n^J}
  +\bigO_{M,U,r,s}(n^{-M-1}),
\]
with Part~I's fixed-offset polynomials. Indeed, compare with the offset
forests of gaps $r$ and $s$ on $[n]$, for which this is \cref{spf:thm:main}.
For $q\le M$ every path of either pair has at least $q$ vertices, so
$\mathsf S_q=n-qr$ (respectively $n-qs$) for both pairs; at $q=M+1$ the
subpath counts differ by at most $r$ (respectively $s$); the numbers of edges,
hence $\lambda$, coincide. By \cref{spf:mg:thm:main} with this $M$, the two
generating functions differ by
$e^{\lambda u}n^{-M}\bigl(\mathsf B_M(u;\mga,\mgb)-\mathsf B_M(u;\mga',\mgb')\bigr)
+\bigO(n^{-M-1})$, and the bracket is $\bigO(1/n)$. The length $M$ cannot be
lowered to $M-1$ in the absolute model when $M\ge2$: by
\cref{spf:mg:thm:shortpath} with $L=M-1$, one path of $M-1$ vertices changes
the generating function by $2(-1)^{M-1}e^{2u}u^Mn^{-M}+\bigO(n^{-M-1})$. So
Part~II's sufficient condition \eqref{fga:eq:weaklength}, a shortest path
long compared with $\log n/\log\log n$, is far from necessary at each fixed
order: a fixed length $M$ suffices at order $M$, on every disk. The growing
regime of Part~II's question~2 is a different problem, since a fixed
statement for every $M$ at once needs constants uniform in $M$
(\cref{spf:mg:q:growing}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Exact directed shape blindness with one target path}

\begin{proposition}[A one-path identity; manuscript~05]
\label[proposition]{spf:mg:prop:onepath}
If $\mathcal V$ is one directed path and $\mathcal P$ has $r$ paths, then in the
directed model
\begin{equation}
 \mu_k=\binom{n-r}{k}\frac{(n-k)!}{n!}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:onepath}
\end{equation}
Thus the complete law depends on the source forest only through its edge
count $n-r$, and two such forests with the same $r$ have exactly equal
laws, not merely equal asymptotic expansions.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
Fix any $k$ selected source edges, with $c$ components and $v=k+c$
vertices. The number of labeled disjoint placements of their rods in
one directed target path is $\fall{n-k}c$: contract each rod to a tile
and order the labeled rods among the other tiles. After mapping the
unused vertices, the number of bijections is
$\fall{n-k}c(n-v)!=(n-k)!$, independent of the component profile.
There are $\binom{n-r}{k}$ selected source edge sets. Divide by $n!$.
\end{proof}

The underlying one-path inclusion--exclusion mechanism is part of the
classical fixed-gap framework and appears in the repository's one-path
family. The formulation for an arbitrary source shape makes clear why
some proposed ``universal short-path effects'' must fail in the directed
model.

For reproducible absolute-model comparisons one can similarly use
\begin{equation}
 \mu_k=\frac{(n-k)!}{n!}[t^k]
                \prod_{L\in\mathcal P}R_{L-1}(t;\theta),
 \label{spf:mg:eq:onepath-run}
\end{equation}
where $R_m$ enumerates subsets of a row of $m$ edges, with a factor
$\theta$ for each nonempty run. A two-state recurrence, distinguishing
whether the last edge is selected, gives
\begin{equation}
 R_0=1,\qquad R_1=1+\theta t,\qquad
 R_m=(1+t)R_{m-1}+(\theta-1)tR_{m-2}\quad(m\ge2).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:Rrun}
\end{equation}
Equation \eqref{spf:mg:eq:onepath-run} follows from the preceding proof with the
additional factor $\theta^c$ for a selected set having $c$ runs. It
supplies the independent rational short-path certificates in
\cref{spf:mg:sec:verification}.

\section{Moving gaps and reciprocal-integer correction boundaries}
\label{spf:mg:sec:moving}

For offsets $1\le r,s\le n$ define
\begin{align}
 X^{(1)}_{r,s,n}&=\sum_{i=1}^{n-r}
        \ind\{\pi(i+r)-\pi(i)=s\},\notag\\
 X^{(2)}_{r,s,n}&=\sum_{i=1}^{n-r}
        \ind\{|\pi(i+r)-\pi(i)|=s\}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:offset-stats}
\end{align}
The subpath counts are particularly simple:
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf S_q(\mathcal P)=\max(n-qr,0),\qquad
 \mathsf S_q(\mathcal V)=\max(n-qs,0).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:offset-S}
\end{equation}
Indeed a $q$-edge source interval starts at an integer $i$ with
$i+qr\le n$.

\begin{corollary}[Arbitrary moving offsets; manuscript~05]
\label[corollary]{spf:mg:cor:moving}
The all-orders expansion \eqref{spf:mg:eq:main} holds uniformly for all
$1\le r,s\le n$, after the exact substitutions
\begin{equation}
 a_q=\left(1-q\frac rn\right)_+,
 \qquad b_q=\left(1-q\frac sn\right)_+,
 \qquad
 \lambda_n=\theta\left(1-\frac rn\right)
                   \left(1-\frac sn\right).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:offset-densities}
\end{equation}
If $r/n\to\alpha$ and $s/n\to\beta$, then the law converges to
$\operatorname{Poisson}(\theta(1-\alpha)(1-\beta))$.
The error relative to the Poisson law with parameter $\lambda_n$ is
uniformly $\bigO(1/n)$, regardless of the rates of convergence.
\end{corollary}

This includes fixed offsets, offsets growing slower than $n$, proportional
offsets, and offsets tending to $n$. The limiting parameter alone does
not encode the correction terms.

For real $\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]$, regard
$a_q(\alpha)=(1-q\alpha)_+$ and
$b_q(\beta)=(1-q\beta)_+$ as macroscopic density coordinates. At order
$j$, $\mathsf B_j(u;\alpha,\beta)$ is piecewise polynomial and can have new
boundaries only at $\alpha=1/q$ or $\beta=1/q$, with
$2\le q\le j+1$.

\begin{theorem}[First appearance and jump at a boundary; manuscript~05]
\label{spf:mg:thm:boundary}
Fix $q\ge2$ and a target ratio $\beta$. No coefficient of order below
$n^{-(q-1)}$ has a boundary at $\alpha=1/q$. The right derivative minus
the left derivative of the coefficient $\mathsf B_{q-1}$ there is
\begin{equation}
 q u^qH_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb(\beta)).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:boundary-jump}
\end{equation}
For the corresponding coefficient of the avoidance probability,
including its $e^{-\lambda}$ factor, the derivative jump is
\begin{equation}
 q(-1)^q e^{-\lambda}\mathsf H_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb(\beta)),
 \qquad\lambda=\theta(1-1/q)(1-\beta).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:avoid-jump}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
For indices below $q$, the functions $(1-\ell\alpha)_+$ are affine in
a neighborhood of $1/q$. The new density $a_q$ has derivative $-q$
on the left and $0$ on the right, and is continuous at the boundary.
Locality excludes it from all earlier coefficients. Apply
\cref{spf:mg:thm:sensitivity} to the first coefficient where it can appear.
The exponential factor is smooth in $\alpha$, and the coefficient itself
is continuous, so its derivative jump is obtained by multiplying by
$e^{-\lambda}$.
\end{proof}

These boundaries are not singularities of the limiting Poisson law.
They are kinks in successively smaller finite-size correction terms.
That distinction prevents an artificial claim of a macroscopic phase
transition where the limiting distribution remains smooth.

\subsection{Sharpness at every order}

\begin{proposition}[A nonvanishing family; manuscript~05]
\label[proposition]{spf:mg:prop:sharp}
In the directed model, for $q\ge2$ and $0<\beta\le1/q$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf H_q^{(1)}(\mgb(\beta))=-\beta^2(1+\beta)^{q-2}\ne0.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:sharp-family}
\end{equation}
For each $\theta\in\{1,2\}$, the locality cutoff $M+1$ in
\cref{spf:mg:thm:main} cannot in general be decreased to $M$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
Through degree $q$, $b_\ell=1-\ell\beta$. Thus for the coefficient in
question we may replace $\mathscr B(t)$ by
\[
 \frac{t}{1-t}-\frac{\beta t}{(1-t)^2}.
\]
A direct simplification gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\mathscr B(t)}{1+\mathscr B(t)}
 =\frac{t(1-\beta-t)}{1-(1+\beta)t}
 \quad\text{through degree }q.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:H-rational}
\end{equation}
For $q\ge2$ its coefficient is
$(1-\beta)(1+\beta)^{q-1}-(1+\beta)^{q-2}$, equal to
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:sharp-family}. For a stronger, information-theoretic form of sharpness valid in both
models, first note that a matching target with $b_1=1/2$ and $b_j=0$
for $j\ge2$ gives
$\mathsf H_q^{(\theta)}=(-1)^{q-1}\theta^q/2^q\ne0$. Compare
forests made from repeated two-path blocks of vertex lengths $(q-1,q+1)$
and $(q,q)$. Each block has $2q$ vertices, and both have
$\mathsf S_j=2q-2j$ for $1\le j\le q-1$, but their $\mathsf S_q$ values are $1$ and $0$.
Thus the normalized lower densities coincide exactly, while their $a_q$
values differ by $1/(2q)$. Use this common matching target, and let $n$ run through multiples
of $2q$.
The sensitivity identity gives a nonzero difference
$u^qH_q(\mgb)/(2q)$ between their order-$q-1$ correction polynomials.
No rule using only the lower densities can distinguish these two pairs.
In particular the cutoff is sharp even among actual path forests.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An exact cancellation at an irrational ratio}

Nonvanishing is generic, not universal. In the directed model, take
$q=3$ and $1/3<\beta<1/2$. Then $b_3=0$ and
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf H_3^{(1)}=b_3-2b_1b_2+b_1^3
 =(1-\beta)(\beta^2+2\beta-1).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:H3-cancel}
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\beta=\sqrt2-1}
 \label{spf:mg:eq:irrational-cancel}
\end{equation}
annuls the $\alpha=1/3$ boundary in the \emph{entire} second correction
polynomial $\mathsf B_2(u)$, not only in the avoidance specialization $u=-1$.
For this fixed macroscopic target ratio, $\mathsf B_2$ is independent of $a_3$
and is smooth across $\alpha=1/3$.

This does not imply cancellation at every later order. Nor can an
integer offset ratio equal this irrational value exactly: with
$s=\lfloor(\sqrt2-1)n\rfloor$, the discrepancy in the ratio is
$\bigO(1/n)$ and can reintroduce an effect one order later. Determining
the next surviving boundary is an explicit further question.
[Note of the batch-97 write: ``can reintroduce an effect one order later''
is a conjectural remark, not a proved statement; it is recorded with
\cref{spf:mg:q:cancellation} in \cref{spf:mg:sec:questions}.]

\section{Floor effects and critical windows}
\label{spf:mg:sec:floors}

A uniform formula can be weakened accidentally by replacing a moving
parameter with its limit too early. Let
$r_n=\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor$ and
$s_n=\lfloor\beta n\rfloor$. Exactly,
\begin{equation}
 a_q(n)=\left(1-q\alpha+\frac{q\{\alpha n\}}{n}\right)_+,
 \qquad
 b_q(n)=\left(1-q\beta+\frac{q\{\beta n\}}{n}\right)_+.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:floors}
\end{equation}
The fractional parts already influence ordinary inverse powers of $n$.
Thus a constant-coefficient expansion based only on $\alpha,\beta$ is
not generally correct beyond leading order.

For rational ratios, restricting $n$ to one residue class modulo a
common denominator makes these phases constant. Restricting to multiples
of that denominator makes them zero. One then obtains ordinary
constant-coefficient expansions, to which \cref{spf:mg:sec:inverse} applies.
For irrational ratios the bounded phases need not converge; the exact
uniform expression \eqref{spf:mg:eq:main} remains valid with the densities in
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:floors}. No assertion that the resulting coefficients converge
is needed.

At a reciprocal boundary $q\alpha=1$, the density becomes
\begin{equation}
 a_q(n)=\frac{q\{\alpha n\}}n.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:boundary-floor}
\end{equation}
Since it first enters $\mathsf B_{q-1}$, this boundary-floor effect first
contributes at order $n^{-q}$, one order after the macroscopic kink.
More generally, in a critical window $r_n/n=1/q+\zeta/n$,
\begin{equation}
 a_q(n)=\frac{(-q\zeta)_+}{n}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:critical-window}
\end{equation}
The same sensitivity polynomial $\mathsf H_q$ gives its leading contribution.
There is no omitted smoothing layer in this statement: the positive-part
formula is exact for the discrete path count. Any additional dependence
of $\zeta$ on the integer rounding should be retained explicitly.

\begin{remark}[Why fixed-gap substitution can look plausible and still fail; manuscript~05]
For small fixed $q$, the identity $\mathsf S_q=n-qr$ holds only while $qr\le n$.
The first order at which replacing it by an unrestricted linear
polynomial can fail is exactly the order predicted by
\cref{spf:mg:thm:sensitivity}. A formal substitution of $r\asymp n$ into a
long-path expression may therefore reproduce a few early terms and
then become wrong at a specific, geometrically determined order.
\end{remark}

\section{Exact coefficient generation and certified finite checks}
\label{spf:mg:sec:verification}

\subsection{A path-polynomial implementation}
\label{spf:mg:sec:algorithm}

The cluster proof supplies a finite algorithm, but direct enumeration of
all labeled connected graphs is unnecessary. Introduce variables
$z_\ell$ marking rods of edge length $\ell$, and let $T_N(\mathbf z)$ be
the rod-tiling polynomial for one path on $N$ vertices. Then
\begin{equation}
 T_0=T_1=1,\qquad
 T_N=T_{N-1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N-1}z_\ell T_{N-\ell-1}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:T-rec}
\end{equation}
The alternatives are an unused first vertex or a rod beginning there.
For a forest, $T_F=\prod_{L\in F}T_L$. Define formal local logarithms
\begin{equation}
 L_q(\mathbf z)=\log T_{q+1}(\mathbf z)
             -2\log T_q(\mathbf z)+\log T_{q-1}(\mathbf z).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:Lq}
\end{equation}
The logarithm here is its formal expansion at constant term $1$.

\begin{proposition}[Exact logarithmic local decomposition; manuscript~05]
\label[proposition]{spf:mg:prop:log-local}
One has the formal identity
\begin{equation}
 \log T_F(\mathbf z)=\sum_{q\ge1}\mathsf S_q(F)L_q(\mathbf z).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:log-local}
\end{equation}
If $z_\ell$ is given weight $\ell$, $L_q$ contains no term of weighted
degree below $q$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
For a single path the identity is discrete twice-summation of
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:Lq}:
$\log T_N=\sum_{q=1}^{N-1}(N-q)L_q$, because
$\log T_0=\log T_1=0$. Sum over components to obtain
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:log-local}.

For the weight assertion, the connected-graph expansion of the logarithm
is the connected part of the graph expansion in
\cref{spf:mg:prop:placement}. A connected rod configuration of total edge
weight $k$ spans an interval of at most $k$ edges, by
\cref{spf:mg:lem:cluster-local}. For a fixed profile, its coefficient in
$\log T_N$ is therefore a linear combination of $(N-p)_+$ with
$p\le k$. The second difference in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:Lq} vanishes if $q>k$.
All formal operations at fixed weight are finite.
\end{proof}

Writing $x=1/n$, define
\begin{equation}
 \overline C_{\mga}(\mgm,x)
 =[\mathbf z^{\mgm}]
 \exp\left(x^{-1}\sum_{q\ge1}a_q
       L_q(xz_1,xz_2,\ldots)\right).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:normalized-T}
\end{equation}
For actual forest densities this equals $n^{-c}C_F(\mgm)$ at $x=1/n$.
A logarithmic monomial containing $c$ rods acquires a factor $x^{c-1}$,
which is the merger cost. In terms of the preceding construction,
$F_{\mgl}(\mga,x)=\mgmf\,\overline C_{\mga}(\mgm,x)$.
The contribution to a factorial moment is therefore
\begin{equation}
 \theta^c\mgmf\,x^{k-c}
 \frac{\overline C_{\mga}(\mgm,x)
       \overline C_{\mgb}(\mgm,x)}{D_{k+c}(x)}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:algorithm-kernel}
\end{equation}
To compute $\mathsf B_0,\ldots,\mathsf B_M$, retain rod weight through $2M$ and powers
of $x$ through $M$, then apply \eqref{spf:mg:eq:finite-B}.

The standard-library engine \texttt{code/local\_expansions.py} implements
these identities with sparse polynomial dictionaries and
\texttt{fractions.Fraction}; no floating point enters this calculation.
It also has an independent product-of-path-polynomials enumerator for
finite forest counts. [The engine is shipped as
\path{code/04-moving-gaps-local_expansions.py}.] For illustration, the
initial local logarithmic
coefficients are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 [z_1]\sum_q a_qL_q&=a_1,&
 [z_1^2]\sum_q a_qL_q&=-a_1/2-a_2,\\
 [z_1^3]\sum_q a_qL_q&=a_1/3+2a_2+a_3,&
 [z_2]\sum_q a_qL_q&=a_2,\\
 [z_1z_2]\sum_q a_qL_q&=-2a_2-2a_3.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{spf:mg:eq:log-examples}
\end{equation}
These reproduce the small-profile formulas in \cref{spf:mg:sec:explicit}.

\subsection{Coefficients along rational rays}

Let $r=\alpha n$ and $s=\beta n$ be integers, and write
$c_j=\mathsf B_j(-1;\mga(\alpha),\mgb(\beta))$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\#\{\pi:X(\pi)=0\}}{n!}
 =e^{-\lambda}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{M}\frac{c_j}{n^j}
                       +\bigO_M(n^{-M-1})\right),
 \qquad c_0=1.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:ray-expansion}
\end{equation}
\Cref{spf:mg:tab:coeff-half,spf:mg:tab:coeff-third} give exact rational coefficients,
not fitted numerical estimates. The supplementary JSON
(\path{data/04-moving-gaps-coefficients.json}) also records
all coefficients of the full polynomials $\mathsf B_j(u)$ through order six
for the rays $(1/2,1/2)$, $(1/3,1/2)$, and $(2/5,1/2)$, in both models.

\subsection{Rational Bonferroni enclosures}

For any nonnegative integer-valued $X$ the alternating factorial-moment
sums obey
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=0}^{2K+1}(-1)^k\mu_k
 \le\Pp(X=0)\le
 \sum_{k=0}^{2K}(-1)^k\mu_k.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:bonferroni}
\end{equation}
For a direct proof, when $X=j\ge1$ the truncated binomial sum is
$(-1)^m\binom{j-1}{m}$ at cutoff $m$; when $j=0$ it is $1$.
Taking expectations gives the inequalities.

When the target forest is a matching, only source matchings contribute.
If $M_k(\mathcal P)$ counts $k$ disjoint source edges, then
\begin{equation}
 \mu_k=\frac{\theta^kM_k(\mathcal P)\binom{n-s}{k}k!}{\fall n{2k}}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:target-matching}
\end{equation}
For a source path of $L$ vertices its matching polynomial has coefficient
$\binom{L-k}{k}$; multiplying these polynomials computes $M_k$ exactly.
All three numerical rays above have a matching target, so the program
obtains exact rational lower and upper avoidance probabilities using
cutoffs $31$ and $30$ in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:bonferroni}.

The normalization $e^\lambda$ is also enclosed rationally. For
$0\le z<102$, put
\begin{equation}
 E_{100}(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{100}\frac{z^j}{j!}.
 \quad\text{Then}\quad
 E_{100}(z)\le e^z\le E_{100}(z)+
       \frac{z^{101}/101!}{1-z/102}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:exp-enclosure}
\end{equation}
The upper bound is the geometric bound on the positive Taylor tail.
The printed decimal endpoints below are rounded outward from the
resulting rational endpoints.

The bounded scaled residual in \cref{spf:mg:tab:certified} is consistent with
the order-six theorem. These four intervals are not themselves a proof
of an asymptotic remainder for all $n$; that proof is
\cref{spf:mg:sec:remainder}.

For the short-path theorem, compare source lengths $(L,n-L)$ with
$(n/2,n/2)$, and take a single target path. Use
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:onepath-run} to compute moments exactly and Bonferroni cutoffs
$41$ and $40$. Subtract the two rational intervals and multiply by a
rational enclosure of $e^2$, reversing the appropriate endpoints for a
negative interval. The result is \cref{spf:mg:tab:short-cert}.
