\subsection{What was actually checked}

The delivered run of \texttt{code/verify.py} (shipped as
\path{code/04-moving-gaps-verify.py}) completed 3,209 exact
assertions, recorded in \path{data/04-moving-gaps-verification.txt}.
The breakdown is printed in \cref{spf:mg:tab:checks}. Its
permutation enumeration covers every $n=2,\ldots,7$, every
$1\le r,s<n$, and both orientations. The tiling checks range over every
integer partition of $n\le9$, at rod weight at most five. The symbolic
identities in \cref{spf:mg:sec:explicit} are independently checked by
\texttt{code/symbolic\_checks.py} using SymPy
(Remark~\ref{spf:mg:rem:first-two-status}). The standard-library
verification script does not require SymPy.

The OEIS checks use only twelve initial values at $n=2,\ldots,7$ from
\cite{oeis189281,oeis110128}; no downloaded b-file is needed. The coefficient
engine's tests at abstract rational density vectors check polynomial
identities even when those particular vectors do not come from a forest.
The probabilistic estimates themselves are asserted only for actual
forests.

\section{Index inversion along rational moving-gap rays}
\label{spf:mg:sec:inverse}

Fix rational $0<\alpha,\beta<1$, and let $D_0$ be a common denominator.
For positive multiples $n$ of $D_0$ set
\begin{equation}
 A(n)=\#\{\pi\in S_n:X^{(\theta)}_{\alpha n,\beta n,n}(\pi)=0\}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:An}
\end{equation}
All densities and $\lambda=\theta(1-\alpha)(1-\beta)$ are now constant
along the ray. We invert along the actual values of this discrete
sequence. No unspecified interpolation of $A(n)$ is assumed.

\subsection{Centered logarithmic coordinates}

Write formally
\begin{equation}
 \log\left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}c_jn^{-j}\right)
       =\sum_{j\ge1}\chi_jn^{-j}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:psi}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \chi_1=c_1,\qquad
 \chi_2=c_2-\frac{c_1^2}{2},\qquad
 \chi_3=c_3-c_1c_2+\frac{c_1^3}{3}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:psi3}
\end{equation}
Set $\hat n=n+1/2$. Centering Stirling's expansion
\cite{dlmfGamma} gives
\begin{equation}
 \log\Gamma(\hat n+1/2)
 =\hat n\log \hat n-\hat n+\frac12\log(2\pi)
       -\frac1{24\hat n}+\frac7{2880\hat n^3}+\bigO(\hat n^{-5}).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:center-stirling}
\end{equation}
The all-orders version and \cref{spf:mg:thm:main} imply
\begin{equation}
 \log A(n)=\hat n\log \hat n-\hat n+\frac12\log(2\pi)-\lambda
                   +\sum_{j=1}^{M}h_j\hat n^{-j}
                   +\bigO_M(\hat n^{-M-1}),
 \label{spf:mg:eq:logA}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 h_1=\chi_1-\frac1{24},\qquad
 h_2=\chi_2+\frac{\chi_1}{2},\qquad
 h_3=\chi_3+\chi_2+\frac{\chi_1}{4}+\frac7{2880}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:h3}
\end{equation}
Higher $h_j$ follow by the finite substitution $n=\hat n-1/2$.

For a large input $y>0$ define
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda=\log y+\lambda-\frac12\log(2\pi),\qquad
 N=\frac{\Lambda}{W_0(\Lambda/e)},\qquad
 \Delta=\log N.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:inverse-coordinates}
\end{equation}
The principal Lambert function $W_0$ satisfies $W_0(z)e^{W_0(z)}=z$
\cite{dlmfW}, so $N\log N-N=\Lambda$ exactly. Absorbing the constant
in this coordinate keeps the subsequent expressions short.

\begin{theorem}[All-orders index reconstruction; manuscript~05]
\label{spf:mg:thm:inverse}
There are rational functions $d_j(\Delta)$, computable recursively from
$h_1,\ldots,h_j$, such that for $y=A(n)$ on the chosen ray,
\begin{equation}
 n=N-\frac12+\sum_{j=1}^{J}d_j(\Delta)N^{-j}
             +\bigO_J\left(\frac{N^{-J-1}}{\Delta}\right).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:inverse-all}
\end{equation}
The first three terms are
\begin{align}
 n={}&N-\frac12-\frac{h_1}{N\Delta}-\frac{h_2}{N^2\Delta}\notag\\
    &-\frac1{N^3}\left(
           \frac{h_3}{\Delta}+\frac{h_1^2}{\Delta^2}
                       +\frac{h_1^2}{2\Delta^3}\right)
       +\bigO\left(\frac{N^{-4}}{\Delta}\right).
 \label{spf:mg:eq:inverse-three}
\end{align}
For sufficiently large sequence values, rounding a sufficiently accurate
truncation to the nearest multiple of $D_0$ recovers the exact index.
No numerical value for that eventual threshold is asserted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~05)]
Substitute $\hat n=N+\delta$ into \eqref{spf:mg:eq:logA} and subtract
$N\log N-N=\Lambda$. Taylor expansion gives
\begin{equation}
 0=\Delta\delta+\frac{\delta^2}{2N}-\frac{\delta^3}{6N^2}+\cdots
                       +\sum_{j\ge1}h_j(N+\delta)^{-j}.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:inverse-equation}
\end{equation}
Set $\delta=\sum_{j\ge1}d_j(\Delta)N^{-j}$. The first three equations are
\begin{align*}
 \Delta d_1+h_1&=0,\\
 \Delta d_2+h_2&=0,\\
 \Delta d_3+\tfrac12d_1^2-h_1d_1+h_3&=0.
\end{align*}
They yield \eqref{spf:mg:eq:inverse-three}. At every later order the new
coefficient appears only as $\Delta d_j$, and all remaining terms have
already been determined. This proves the formal recursive assertion.

For the remainder, use a smooth truncated carrier from
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:logA}. Substitution of the computed inverse truncation leaves
a residual $\bigO(N^{-J-1})$. Its derivative is
$\log \hat n+\bigO(1/\hat n^2)$ near $N$, hence is comparable to $\Delta$.
The mean value theorem divides the residual by this derivative.
The actual sequence value differs from the same smooth carrier by the
remainder in \eqref{spf:mg:eq:logA}, so the same estimate applies along $y=A(n)$.
The error tends to zero, and distinct admissible indices are separated
by $D_0$, proving eventual rounding recovery.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The remainder estimate of \cref{spf:mg:thm:inverse}, completed]
\label[remark]{spf:mg:rem:inverse-remainder}
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97; the argument is the write's.] The last
paragraph of the proof above is a sketch: it does not show that the
coefficients $d_j(\Delta)$ stay bounded as $\Delta\to\infty$, on which the
uniformity of the residual depends. The following completes it. Put
$\varepsilon=1/N$ and $\phi_M(x)=x\log x-x+\sum_{j\le M}h_jx^{-j}$, so that
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:logA} reads $\Lambda=\phi_M(\hat n)+\bigO(\hat n^{-M-1})$
for $y=A(n)$, while $N\log N-N=\Lambda$.

(i) \emph{Each $d_j$ is a polynomial in $1/\Delta$ with zero constant term.}
For any $\delta$ with $|\varepsilon\delta|<1$,
\[
 \phi_M(N+\delta)-\Lambda=\Delta\delta+g(\delta,\varepsilon)
   +\sum_{j\le M}h_j\varepsilon^j(1+\varepsilon\delta)^{-j},\qquad
 g(\delta,\varepsilon)=\sum_{m\ge2}\frac{(-1)^m\delta^m\varepsilon^{m-1}}{m(m-1)},
\]
which is \eqref{spf:mg:eq:inverse-equation}. Insert
$\delta=\sum_jd_j\varepsilon^j$. In the coefficient of $\varepsilon^j$ every
term other than $\Delta d_j$ contains only $d_1,\ldots,d_{j-1}$ (each term of
$g$ carries the extra factor $\varepsilon^{m-1}$, $m\ge2$, and each
$h_i$-term the factor $\varepsilon^i$, $i\ge1$), and none contains $\Delta$.
Hence $d_j=-P_j(d_1,\ldots,d_{j-1})/\Delta$ with a polynomial $P_j$ free of
$\Delta$, and induction proves the claim. In particular $|d_j|\le C_j$ and
$|\Delta d_j|\le C_j$ for $\Delta\ge1$.

(ii) \emph{The residual is uniform.} Let
$\delta_J=\sum_{j\le J}d_j\varepsilon^j$ and take $M\ge J$. By (i), for
$\Delta\ge1$ the function
$\varepsilon\mapsto\phi_M(N+\delta_J)-\Lambda$, given by the display above
with $\varepsilon$ as a complex variable, is holomorphic and bounded by a
constant $B$ on $|\varepsilon|\le\varepsilon_0$, where $\varepsilon_0>0$ and
$B$ depend only on $J$, $M$ and $h_1,\ldots,h_M$ (choose $\varepsilon_0$ with
$|\varepsilon\delta_J|\le\frac12$). Its Taylor coefficients through
$\varepsilon^J$ vanish by the choice of $d_1,\ldots,d_J$. Cauchy's estimate
gives $|\phi_M(N+\delta_J)-\Lambda|\le2B(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_0)^{J+1}$
for $0<\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0/2$, that is, $\bigO(N^{-J-1})$ uniformly in
$\Delta\ge1$.

(iii) \emph{Inversion.} $\phi_M$ is eventually increasing, with
$\phi_M'(x)=\log x+\bigO(x^{-2})$. Since $\phi_M(\hat n)=\Lambda+o(1)$ and
$\phi_M(N)-\Lambda=\bigO(1/N)$, monotonicity and the mean value theorem give
$\hat n-N=\bigO(1/(N\Delta))$; also $\delta_J=\bigO(1/N)$. Hence
$\phi_M(\hat n)-\phi_M(N+\delta_J)=\bigO(\hat n^{-M-1})+\bigO(N^{-J-1})$,
and $\phi_M'=\Delta+\bigO(1/N)$ between the two points, so
$|\hat n-N-\delta_J|\le C\,N^{-J-1}/\Delta$. As $n=\hat n-\frac12$, this is
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:inverse-all}, and eventual rounding recovery follows as
stated. The constants come from \eqref{spf:mg:eq:logA}, hence from the
non-explicit constants of \cref{spf:mg:thm:main}; no effective threshold
follows (\cref{spf:mg:q:constants}).
\end{remark}

This is a specialization of the familiar Lambert-$W$ inversion strategy
used in the existing report \cite{mgProveIt}; it is not a new definition or
new property of $W$. The new input is the moving-gap coefficient sequence.
Expanding $W_0$ itself in nested logarithms converts
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:inverse-all} into a logarithmic asymptotic transseries in $y$.
Neither that formal expansion nor the direct $W_0$ carrier asserts Borel
summability or controls exponentially small sectors.
[Note of the batch-97 write: the strategy is Part~I's
\cref{spf:thm:inverse-all} (Remark~\ref{spf:rem:inverse-apparatus}), in the
coordinates of Part~II's \eqref{fga:eq:coords} and Part~III's
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:coords} with $\lambda$ in place of $\theta$; the coordinates
$\Lambda$, $N$, $\Delta$ and the input $y$ are manuscript~05's $\mathcal L$,
$Y$, $\ell$ and $T$ (\cref{spf:mg:tab:dictionary}). No novelty is claimed
for the method.]

Along the ray, $A(n+D_0)/A(n)\sim n^{D_0}$ by
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:ray-expansion}. Thus the counts are eventually strictly
increasing. Inverting arbitrary thresholds between consecutive values
would additionally require a stated staircase convention; the theorem
above deliberately concerns index recovery from actual sequence values.

\subsection{Numerical reconstruction from exact integer counts}

For $r=s=n/2$ with even $n$, the exact integer count used in the numerical
check is
\begin{equation}
 A(n)=\sum_{k=0}^{n/2}(-\theta)^k
       \binom{n/2}{k}^{\!2}k!(n-2k)!.
 \label{spf:mg:eq:integer-matching}
\end{equation}
The input is generated by integer arithmetic, not supplied by an
asymptotic approximation. The script then evaluates logarithms and
$W_0$ with 100 decimal digits of working precision.

\section{Further questions and research}
\label{spf:mg:sec:questions}

[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97.] This section collects every unproved
claim of manuscript~05: its eight questions, printed as proposals, and the
conjectural remark at the end of \cref{spf:mg:sec:moving}. Manuscript~05
states no claim that this write found wrong. After each question a note of
the write says which earlier questions of this report it continues and
records the evidence the intake collected; evidence is not proof. The
precise form of Remark~\ref{spf:mg:rem:multiplicity}, the completed
remainder of Remark~\ref{spf:mg:rem:inverse-remainder} and the computer
verification of Remark~\ref{spf:mg:rem:first-two-status} are not open.

The following questions are proposed directions, not statements proved
by the preceding theorems. They deliberately separate algebraic-order
extensions from beyond-all-orders problems.

\begin{question}[The next directed short-path term; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:directed}
For fixed $r,s\ge2$ and one source path of fixed vertex length $L$, what
is the first nonzero term of the directed shape difference? The leading
absolute-model order cancels by \cref{spf:mg:thm:shortpath}, while $s=1$ gives
exact equality. Determine the precise exponent and the dependence on
$r,s,L$, including any further exceptional cancellations.
\end{question}
The finite coefficient engine provides a direct experimental route,
but a useful theorem should isolate a connected-pattern mechanism rather
than merely tabulate a fixed number of small $L$.

[Note of the batch-97 write.] This continues the fixed-length half of
Part~II's question~2 (\cref{fga:sec:questions}) in the directed model.
\emph{Evidence, not proof:} the batch-97 intake computed the directed and
absolute differences exactly, with its own implementation of the profile
identity \eqref{spf:mg:eq:profile-id} (validated against brute force on eight
forest pairs at $n=7$; not shipped), for a two-path target $(n/2,n/2)$ and
sources $(L,n-L)$ against $(n/2,n/2)$, so $r=s=2$. The scaled differences
$n^{L+1}e^2\bigl(\Pp(X_{\mathrm{short}}=0)-\Pp(X_{\mathrm{long}}=0)\bigr)$ at
$n=20,30,40$ were
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule
& $n=20$ & $n=30$ & $n=40$\\
\midrule
absolute, $L=1$ & $-2.645$ & $-2.391$ & $-2.281$\\
absolute, $L=2$ & $-3.155$ & $-2.672$ & $-2.473$\\
directed, $L=1$ & $-0.0196$ & $-0.0077$ & $-0.0041$\\
directed, $L=2$ & $0.0295$ & $0.0100$ & $0.0049$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The absolute rows approach the proved $-2$ like $-2+\bigO(1/n)$. The directed
rows decay like $n^{-2}$, which suggests a directed difference of order
$n^{-L-3}$ at $(r,s)=(2,2)$, one order below the proved $\bigO(n^{-L-2})$; the
sign appears to depend on $L$. A partial mechanism: for $b_q=1-sq/n$,
\eqref{spf:mg:eq:sharp-family} with $\beta=s/n$ gives
$\mathsf H_q^{(1)}(\mgb)=-(s/n)^2(1+s/n)^{q-2}$, so the order-$(q-1)$ term is
$\bigO(n^{-q-2})$; the contributions of $\mathsf B_q$ must cancel at order
$n^{-q-1}$ as well, which is not proved. A larger run ($n=60$) did not finish.

\begin{question}[Cancellation loci and their next visible layer; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:cancellation}
Classify the zeros of $\mathsf H_q^{(\theta)}(\mgb(\beta))$ on each interval
between reciprocal integers. At $q=3$, $\theta=1$, and
$\beta=\sqrt2-1$, determine the first later coefficient whose dependence
on the $\alpha=1/3$ boundary survives. How do irrational rounding phases
interact with that delayed boundary?
\end{question}
Each fixed $q$ reduces the first part to finitely many polynomial root
problems. An all-$q$ description would organize cancellations across the
whole hierarchy.

[Note of the batch-97 write.] The last sentence of \cref{spf:mg:sec:moving},
that the rounding discrepancy of $s=\lfloor(\sqrt2-1)n\rfloor$ ``can
reintroduce an effect one order later'', belongs here: it is offered without
proof. Its sketch is the floor formula \eqref{spf:mg:eq:floors}, which moves
$b_q$ by $\bigO(1/n)$; what is missing is the computation of the resulting
order-$n^{-3}$ term and a proof that it does not vanish.

\begin{question}[A growing locality scale; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:growing}
Can the constants in \cref{spf:mg:thm:main,spf:mg:thm:shortpath} be controlled jointly
in the order and in the short-path length? In particular, identify the
first shape-dependent sector when $L\asymp c\log n/\log\log n$.
\end{question}
The current proof fixes $M$ before letting $n$ grow. Its moment cutoff
and tree bound establish uniformity over geometry, but do not justify
letting $M$ or $L$ grow without additional quantitative work.

[Note of the batch-97 write.] This is what remains of Part~I's question~5
(the transition when path lengths are comparable to $\log n/\log\log n$), of
the growing half of Part~II's question~2, and of the beyond-all-orders
questions of Part~I (question~3) and Part~III (question~2) for long paths.
At each fixed order Remark~\ref{spf:mg:rem:threshold} settles the threshold
for fixed lengths.

\begin{question}[Large-order structure; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:large-order}
What is the growth of $\mathsf B_M(-1;\mga,\mgb)$ on compact subsets of a fixed
proportional-gap cell? Is there a useful Gevrey bound, and what is the
optimal truncation error? Are the dominant large-order mechanisms local
cluster proliferation, finite-permutation constraints, or both?
\end{question}
An answer would be the necessary bridge from the present algebraic
sector to any justified exponentially improved transseries.

[Note of the batch-97 write.] This is Part~I's question~4 for moving gaps.
At the fixed directed offsets $(2,2)$ Part~V determines the growth of the
coefficients $c_J$ (\cref{spf:bc:thm:coeff-growth}) and proves a truncation
rule for a Borel completion (\cref{spf:bc:thm:truncation}); nothing of this
kind is known on a proportional-gap cell.

\begin{question}[Several gap pairs and cycles; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:several}
Extend the construction to two or more simultaneous gap constraints,
including a joint law with separate marking variables. Which finite
colored subgraph counts replace $\mathsf S_q$? At what order do cycles and
orientation inconsistencies first affect the expansion?
\end{question}
The exact rod description relies on a single path structure in each
forest. Merely adding the single-gap answers would miss shared vertices,
coincident events, and new connected components.
[Note of the batch-97 write: this continues Part~I's question~6, Part~II's
question~4 and Part~III's question~10.]
[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101: answered for reciprocal pairs by
Part~VII. The joint law with separate marks is \cref{spf:cg:thm:main}. The
finite coloured subgraph counts that replace $\mathsf S_q$ are the products
$i_S(H;n)\,i_T(H;n)$ of injective embedding counts of selected coloured
graphs into the source and target graphs, eventual integer polynomials in
$n$ (\cref{spf:cg:prop:identity,spf:cg:prop:mobius}). Cycles first affect
the expansion at order $n^{-2}$, through the alternating square, and
incompatible cycles, the orientation inconsistencies, contribute exactly
zero (\cref{spf:cg:rem:cycles}). Part~VII has fixed gaps and no
local-density form; more colours, gap pairs sharing a gap, the directed
model and moving gaps remain open (\cref{spf:cg:sec:questions}).]

\begin{question}[Growing observed counts; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:counts}
For what ranges $j=j(n)\to\infty$ does the local probability expansion
remain relatively accurate, rather than only absolutely accurate in
$\ell^1$? Can one derive uniform moderate-deviation estimates through
$G(u)$ with a growing complex disk?
\end{question}
The present theorem fixes the disk radius. A growing saddle point would
require new control of both the cluster sum and the factorial tail.
[Note of the batch-97 write: this continues Part~II's question~5.]

\begin{question}[Explicit constants and certified inversion thresholds; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:constants}
Turn the remainder proof into usable finite constants, then combine
those constants with the inverse residual argument to certify a specific
index-rounding threshold on a rational ray. Can structural cancellations
reduce the conservative $k^{3h}$ cluster majorant sufficiently to make
such bounds practical?
\end{question}
The delivered Bonferroni certificates validate selected finite inputs;
they do not yet constitute a uniform threshold theorem with a numerical
cutoff.
[Note of the batch-97 write: this continues Part~I's question~8 and Part~II's
question~6.]

\begin{question}[A small formal verification target; manuscript~05]
\label[question]{spf:mg:q:formal}
Formalize the exact profile identity, the local-log recurrence, and the
finite coefficient extraction in Lean. A first deliverable could be a
verified checker for rational coefficient tables, followed by the
cluster locality and factorial-moment inequalities.
\end{question}
This would separate finite algebraic certification from the analytic
uniform-remainder layer. No formalization is claimed in this package.
[Note of the batch-97 write: this continues Part~I's question~7 and
Part~III's question~6; nothing in Part~IV is formalized.]

\section{Conclusion of manuscript 05}

Growing gaps do not destroy the all-orders Poisson description; they
change the correct geometric coordinates. Replacing fixed path counts by
normalized short-subpath counts yields a theorem uniform over every
path-forest pair, including the regime where most paths stay bounded.
Connected-overlap cost explains why order $M$ sees only $M+1$ edge
lengths, and the highest-path sensitivity formula identifies exactly how
the new length enters.

The same mechanism resolves a concrete fixed-short-path question, gives
sharp finite-size boundaries for proportional offsets, and exhibits an
explicit cancellation that a leading Poisson limit cannot detect.
Exact coefficient generation and rational finite-input checks make the
results reproducible, while the separation of algebraic asymptotics,
rounding phases, and exponentially small effects keeps the remaining
questions precise.

\section{Manuscript 05's reproducibility notes and shipped files}
\label{spf:mg:app:reproduce}

The main document is self-contained LaTeX with an internal bibliography.
The PDF was compiled from the delivered source. The package contains the
following files and directories:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{.43\textwidth}p{.50\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
File & Purpose\\
\midrule
\texttt{article.tex}, \texttt{article.pdf} & Complete manuscript and compiled PDF\\
\texttt{README.md}, \texttt{SOURCES.md} & Run instructions, claims, and source audit\\
\texttt{Makefile}, \texttt{requirements-optional.txt} & PDF and verification commands; optional packages\\
\texttt{code/local\_expansions.py} & Exact local-log coefficient engine and finite counting routines\\
\texttt{code/verify.py} & Standard-library exact checks and rational certificates\\
\texttt{code/symbolic\_checks.py} & Symbolic verification of the first two corrections\\
\texttt{code/inverse\_demo.py} & Uncertified high-precision inverse diagnostics\\
\texttt{data/} & Exact coefficients, interval endpoints, and recorded outputs\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Run from the package root:
\begin{verbatim}
python code/verify.py
python code/symbolic_checks.py
python code/inverse_demo.py
latexmk -pdf -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{verbatim}
The first command requires only Python's standard library. The second
requires SymPy, and the third requires mpmath. Generated output is written
under \texttt{data/} using paths relative to the scripts, not the caller's
working directory. The pseudo-random rational identity checks use a
fixed seed; the main mathematical and numerical outputs are exact and
deterministic. The recorded execution duration is machine-dependent and
is not a mathematical datum.

\paragraph{The shipped files of manuscript~05.} [Added 4 October 2026,
batch 97.] Manuscript~05's article, PDF, README and checksum ledger are not
shipped (the article is this Part; the ledger was verified at placement,
20/20), and neither is its \texttt{requirements-optional.txt}, a byte copy
of \path{data/02-fixed-gap-requirements.txt} (SymPy 1.14.0, mpmath 1.3.0).
Its other sixteen files are shipped byte-identical with the prefix
\path{04-moving-gaps-}: the four scripts and the \texttt{Makefile} in
\texttt{code/}, the ten recorded outputs in \texttt{data/}, and
\texttt{SOURCES.md} at the report root; four of the CSV files are CRLF as
delivered. The scripts import their sibling by the delivered name
(\texttt{from local\_expansions import \ldots}) and write unprefixed files
into the \texttt{data/} directory beside their own, and the \texttt{Makefile}
calls them by their delivered names; its \texttt{pdf} and \texttt{clean}
targets would act on this report's \texttt{article.tex}. The commands above
therefore work only in a copy that restores the delivered layout; the report
README gives that recipe, which the batch-97 write ran. Its source ledger
cites the first author of~\cite{spahn} as ``R.~Spahn''; the arXiv record
names George Spahn.

\section{Proof dependencies and non-claims of manuscript 05}
\label{spf:mg:app:dependencies}

The logical dependency chain is as follows. The exact profile identity
and the elementary moment bound are proved first. Connected graph
expansion gives exact local cluster polynomials. Their merger-cost
filtration gives a finite coefficient formula and finite locality. The
tree bound and the factorial tail then promote formal extraction to an
all-orders theorem. Highest-path sensitivity is a separate refinement of
the same filtration; it implies the fixed-short-path and correction-boundary
results. The inverse theorem additionally uses the classical Stirling
expansion and the defining identity for $W_0$.

No theorem depends on a guessed recurrence, a high-precision fit, or the
success of a finite test. The fixed-gap report is the source of the
research questions and a prior-work comparison, not an unverified black
box needed for the new proofs. Its mathematical infrastructure that is
used here is reproved in the manuscript. The primary external analytic
input in the inversion section is the standard all-orders Stirling
expansion, with its fixed-order remainder.

The citations below support definitions, provenance, and established
context. They do not establish publication priority for this manuscript.
The source audit was restricted to the listed repository report, the two
OEIS entries, the cited permutation papers, and the stated special-function
references. In particular, failure to find a prior result in this audit
is not evidence that none exists.

\part{Beyond all algebraic orders in A189281: a Borel completion}
\label{spf:bc:part}

\section{Provenance, scope and notation of Part V}
\label{spf:bc:sec:front}

[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97.] Part~V was added in the write of
batch~97. It is built from manuscript~06 of that batch, which continues
Part~III's \cref{spf:rc:sec:kernel,spf:rc:sec:Borel} for A189281, the
directed offsets $(2,2)$. It gives the entire Borel transform
$\widehat C(t)=\mathcal E'(e^t-1)$ of \cref{spf:rc:thm:Borel} an explicit
representation through Dawson's function, sums the algebraic sector along the
positive ray into an analytic completion $\bcC(n)$, shows that no angular
sector admits an exponential bound, determines the sharp large-order growth
of the coefficients $c_J$, proves a growing-order truncation rule for
$\bcC$, shows that the OEIS order-13 operator annihilates the completion
$\bcA(n)=\Gamma(n+1)\bcC(n)/e$, and proves that the counts are \emph{not}
recovered: the flat discrepancy $\delta_n=e\,a_n/n!-\bcC(n)$ is nonzero at
least once in every four consecutive indices. Its inputs from Parts~I
and~III are cited, not re-proved; its re-derivations are second routes
(\cref{spf:bc:tab:routes}). Manuscript~06's symbols that clash with the rest
of the report are renamed or declared in \cref{spf:bc:tab:dictionary}.

\subsection{Provenance}
\label{spf:bc:sec:provenance}

Manuscript~06 of batch~97 is the archive
\nolinkurl{ProveIt_A189281_Borel_Completion.zip} (16~files, one of them the
checksum ledger \texttt{SHA256SUMS.txt}, which lists the other 15), which
arrived in commit \texttt{e88ed8bf6} and was placed in this report by commit
\texttt{5a69916e8}. Its article is titled \emph{Beyond all algebraic orders
in OEIS A189281: An explicit Dawson kernel, sharp Borel growth,
non-holonomic correction coefficients, and an unavoidable flat remainder}
(21~pages, dated 4~October 2026); its author line reads ``Prepared for
Vladimir Reshetnikov, AI-assisted mathematical research report'', and its PDF
metadata names ``AI-assisted research report prepared for Vladimir
Reshetnikov'' as author. Its pinned repository commit is
\texttt{79e7aab60ee856862b36c38cf32cfdb1be95313f} (2026-10-04,
18:07;~\cite{bcProveIt}). It read this report's \texttt{article.tex} as the
Git blob \texttt{73a53d9784260c2f0d083f2baa62a5535f6d6105} and its README as
the blob \texttt{59ad8b15b9522102e1f6d48f68c1db358a3ac044}; both are the
blobs of these files immediately before the batch-97 write, so manuscript~06
saw Parts~I--III exactly as they stood. It was written independently of
manuscript~05 (Part~IV); the two share no theorem.

Manuscript~06 states correctly what Parts~I--III contain: the all-orders
expansion (\cref{spf:thm:main}), the series $\Psi$ and its Stirling transform
(\eqref{spf:rc:eq:Psi}, \eqref{spf:rc:eq:c-Stirling}), the differential
equation and recurrence of $\Psi$ (\eqref{spf:rc:eq:Psi-ode},
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:psi-recurrence}), the entire Borel transform
(\cref{spf:rc:thm:Borel}), the Lambert-$W$ inversion, and the caveat that the
Borel transform does not by itself give summability, growth or truncation.

\paragraph{Scope and non-claims of manuscript~06, kept.}
It is an AI-assisted, unrefereed research manuscript, not a Lean-checked
formalization; its numerical quadrature is diagnostic and not
interval-certified (its \path{data/05-borel-completion-numerical_status.json}
records \texttt{"interval\_certified": false}). ``New'' means an extension of
the inspected report; historical priority beyond the sources reviewed is not
asserted, and the absence of a proof on an OEIS page is not evidence that no
published proof exists. It does \emph{not} prove the conjectured recurrence
of A189281 of order eight or the order-13 presentation: the operator
identities and the fourth-order equation concern the analytic completion
$\bcA$, not the counts, and no minimal-order claim is made. Its
non-P-recursiveness theorem concerns the correction coefficients $c_J$, not
$a_n$ and not $\bcA(n)$. Its truncation theorem is for $\bcC$, not for
$e\,a_n/n!$, with no optimality, lower bound or explicit onset; the $M$
column of its table only illustrates the rule. ``Canonical'' names the
positive-ray prescription, not a uniqueness claim; $\bcC$ is not asserted to
be a resurgent transseries, and the Gaussian sector changes of the kernel do
not determine the Stokes data of the counting problem. The amplitude, scale
and sign of $\delta_n$ are not determined; the flat-discrepancy theorem does
not give $\delta_n\ne0$ for every $n$, and the observed signs are numerical
only. The Lambert-$W$ inversion and the tiling formula are not new. The
limsup law is an envelope law, not a termwise equivalent. No repository file
or OEIS entry was modified.

\label{spf:bc:sec:claims}

\paragraph{Source and novelty ledger of manuscript~06, restated.}
The OEIS entry, its values, its conjectured recurrences and Mark van Hoeij's
$T$--$U$--$V$ factorization are external~\cite{bcOeis}; the matching-of-tilings
method is Spahn and Zeilberger's~\cite{spahn}; recurrence guessing is
discussed by Kauers and Koutschan~\cite{kauers}; the special-function facts
are from the DLMF~\cite{bcDlmfDawson,bcDlmfErf,bcDlmfEone,bcDlmfWatson,bcDlmfStokes,dlmfGamma}.
Manuscript~06 credits to this report the all-orders expansion, the rational
collapse and integrality, the series $\Psi$ with its equation and recurrence,
the Stirling transform, the entire Borel transform and the Lambert-$W$
inversion. It claims as its own: the Dawson--Volterra representation, the
real asymptotics with both integration constants, positive-ray summation and
meromorphic continuation of the completion, the strip threshold and the
failure of sectorial bounds, the double-exponential growth and the root-growth
law, the non-P-recursiveness of $(c_J)$, the truncation rule for $\bcC$, the
shift and fourth-order equations and the analytic realization of the OEIS
factorization, the arithmetic proof of a nonzero flat discrepancy, and its
propagation to the smooth inverse.

\subsection{Notation}
\label{spf:bc:sec:notation}

Throughout Part~V, $\theta=1$ and $(r,s)=(2,2)$, so $c_J=c_J^{(1)}(2,2)$,
$\psi_i$, $\Psi$ and $\mathcal E$ are Part~III's at $(2,2)$, and
$a_n=\Aone(n)$, which Part~III writes $A(n)$. Manuscript~06's symbols that
collide with the rest of the report are renamed as in
\cref{spf:bc:tab:dictionary}; no normalization is changed. Real and
imaginary parts are written $\Re$ and $\Im$ (manuscript~06:
$\operatorname{Re}$, $\operatorname{Im}$), and Euler's number in italic.

The following symbols keep manuscript~06's meaning inside Part~V and mean
something else elsewhere in the report: $a_n$, the count (Part~I's $a_j$ are
logarithmic coefficients, Part~IV's $a_q$ densities); $H=\mathcal E'$ (Part~I's
Charlier $H_h(q;\theta)$, Part~IV's $\mathsf H_q$); Dawson's function $D$, the
kernel $K$, the Green function $g_v$ and $K_0$ (Part~I's $D$, $\mathcal D$ and
$K$); $E_1$, the exponential integral; $\delta_n$, the flat discrepancy (in
Part~III's proof of \cref{spf:rc:prop:inverse}, $\delta$ is a shift); the
operators $T$, $U$, $V$ and the polynomials $P$, $Q$, $\mathcal R$ of
\cref{spf:bc:sec:operator} (Part~I's $R_h$, Part~IV's $R_{J,k}$); the
radius $R$, the order $M$ and the maximum modulus $M_f(R)$; $\beta_j$, the
coefficients of $e^{-jt}$ in the proof of \cref{spf:bc:cor:meromorphic}; and
$L(p)=-\gamma-\log p$ in the proof of \cref{spf:bc:thm:real}.

Two renamings carry a tempting false reading. $\widehat C$ is the Borel
transform of the asymptotic \emph{coefficients}, an entire function of $t$;
$\bcC(n)$ is its Laplace integral, a function of $n$ that has the same
asymptotic expansion as $e\,a_n/n!$ but is not equal to it
(\cref{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero}). And $\hat c_m=c_{m+1}/m!$ is not a
coefficient of the inverse-power series.

\section{Manuscript 06: the question selected from ProveIt}
\label{spf:bc:sec:context}

Let $a_n$ be the number of permutations $\pi$ of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$ for which
\begin{equation}
 \pi(i+2)-\pi(i)\ne2\qquad(1\le i\le n-2).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:definition}
\end{equation}
The convention at $n=0$ gives $a_0=1$. This is OEIS A189281
\cite{bcOeis}. The initial values are
\[
 1,\ 1,\ 2,\ 5,\ 18,\ 75,\ 410,\ 2729,\ 20906,\ 181499,\ldots.
\]
The OEIS entry displays a conjectured recurrence of order eight and degree
eleven, an equivalent order-thirteen presentation, and a factorization of the
latter. Its higher asymptotic coefficients were originally attached to those
conjectural recurrences. The underlying counting problem and methods of
recurrence guessing are discussed in \cite{spahn,kauers}.

The ProveIt report \emph{Unconditional all-orders asymptotics for two OEIS
permutation sequences} develops a different route: exact path-forest moments,
stabilization, and controlled inclusion--exclusion. Its Part III proves a
rational-collapse identity and universal integrality for directed offsets
\cite{bcProveIt}. We continue its Sections 29--30 rather than repeat its finite
coefficient computations. [Note of the batch-97 write: these are
\cref{spf:rc:sec:kernel,spf:rc:sec:Borel} of this report.]

\subsection{The exact prior input}

The following facts are inputs from \cite{bcProveIt}. They are not claimed as new.
For every fixed integer $M\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{e a_n}{n!}
 =\sum_{J=0}^{M}\frac{c_J}{n^J}+\bigO_M(n^{-M-1}),\qquad n\to\infty,
 \quad c_0=1.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:prior-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
Define the \emph{formal} power series
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(z)=\sum_{i\ge0}\psi_i z^i
 =(1+z)^3\sum_{j\ge0}j!\left(-\frac{z^2}{1+z}\right)^j.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:psi}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 c_J=\sum_{i=1}^{J}\psi_i\Stir{J-1}{i-1}\quad(J\ge1),
 \qquad
 \mathcal E(x)=\sum_{i\ge0}\frac{\psi_i}{i!}x^i
 \label{spf:bc:eq:stirling}
\end{equation}
is entire, and the order-one Borel transform of the nonconstant asymptotic
sector is
\begin{equation}
 \widehat C(t)=\sum_{J\ge1}\frac{c_J}{(J-1)!}t^{J-1}
      =\mathcal E'(e^t-1).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:prior-borel}
\end{equation}
We put $H=\mathcal E'$. The first auxiliary coefficients and correction coefficients
are respectively
\begin{align}
 (\psi_0,\ldots,\psi_{11})
   &=(1,3,2,-1,1,2,-6,0,24,-24,-96,216),\\
 (c_0,\ldots,c_{10})
   &=(1,3,2,1,0,3,26,101,124,-1409,-13266).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:first-c}
\end{align}
No guessed recurrence for $a_n$ is used in any proof below.
[Note of the batch-97 write: \eqref{spf:bc:eq:prior-asymptotic} is
\cref{spf:thm:main} at $\theta=1$, $(r,s)=(2,2)$, $u=-1$, with
$e\,a_n/n!=e\,\Pp(X=0)$; \eqref{spf:bc:eq:psi} is \eqref{spf:rc:eq:Psi} at
$(2,2)$, where $\rcw_j=j!$; \eqref{spf:bc:eq:stirling} is
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:c-Stirling} and \eqref{spf:rc:eq:E}, with the variable $x$
for Part~III's $\xi$; \eqref{spf:bc:eq:prior-borel} is
\cref{spf:rc:thm:Borel}. The values \eqref{spf:bc:eq:first-c} are those of
\cref{spf:rc:sec:kernel} and \eqref{spf:eq:A189281}.]

The distinction between the roles of these inputs is important. All analytic
statements about $H$, $\widehat C$ and the completion constructed below follow directly
from the explicit formal series \eqref{spf:bc:eq:psi}. Only the statements comparing
that completion with the original integer counts use
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:prior-asymptotic}. The full combinatorial stabilization proof is in
\cite{bcProveIt}; this manuscript does not present that earlier proof as its own.

\subsection{What was open, and what is resolved here}

The earlier report explicitly warns that an entire Borel transform can grow
too rapidly for a positive-axis Laplace integral. It also leaves large-order
growth and a justified growing-order truncation rule open. The conclusions
here are more differentiated than a single yes-or-no answer to summability.

\begin{center}
\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.18}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.39\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.52\linewidth}}
\toprule
Question & Conclusion in this manuscript\\
\midrule
Does the positive-ray Borel integral exist? & Yes, exactly for $\Re n>1$.\\
Is there an exponentially bounded open angular Borel sector at direction zero?
 & No; horizontal strips, not angular sectors, describe the admissible geometry.\\
Does the ordinary inverse-power series converge? & No; a sharp root-growth law proves zero radius.\\
Are the correction coefficients P-recursive? & No. This is not a claim about P-recursiveness of $a_n$.\\
Can the canonical sum be truncated at a growing order? & Yes, with error $\exp[-\tfrac12 n\log n+\bigO(n)]$.\\
Does that sum recover all integer counts? & No; every block of four indices starting at $n\ge2$ contains a discrepancy.\\
Is the full counting recurrence proved? & No. Its order-13 operator is proved to annihilate the analytic completion only.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
