\section{An explicit Dawson--Volterra representation}
\label{spf:bc:sec:kernel}

Dawson's entire function is
\begin{equation}
 D(z)=e^{-z^2}\int_0^ze^{u^2}\,\mathrm d u
     =\frac{\sqrt\pi}{2}e^{-z^2}\operatorname{erfi}(z).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:dawson}
\end{equation}
The integral is path independent. Definitions and its relation with error
functions are given in \cite{bcDlmfDawson}. For an entire function $f$, write
\begin{equation}
 \bcI f(x)=\int_0^x f(u)\,\mathrm d u,\qquad
 \bcI^2f(x)=\int_0^x(x-u)f(u)\,\mathrm d u.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:volterra}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Explicit kernel; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:kernel}
Define
\begin{equation}
 K(x)=D\!\left(\frac{x+1}{2}\right)
       +e^{-x}D\!\left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:K}
\end{equation}
Then the entire function $H=\mathcal E'$ from \eqref{spf:bc:eq:stirling} is exactly
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{H(x)=K''(x)+4K'(x)+6K(x)+4\bcI K(x)+\bcI^2K(x).}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:H}
\end{equation}
In particular, \eqref{spf:bc:eq:H} and $\widehat C(t)=H(e^t-1)$ give an explicit entire
representation of the Borel transform, without summing a divergent
hypergeometric series as an analytic power series.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{A coefficientwise Gamma integral}

Expanding first in the formal variable $z$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(z)=\int_0^\infty e^{-v}
              \frac{(1+z)^4}{1+z+vz^2}\,\mathrm d v.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:formal-integral}
\end{equation}
Here the integral is \emph{coefficientwise}: each coefficient of the rational
integrand is a polynomial in $v$, integrated using
$\int_0^\infty e^{-v}v^j\,\mathrm d v=j!$. Equation
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:formal-integral} does not assert that $\Psi$ has a convergent Taylor
series near zero.

For $v\ge0$, let $g_v$ solve
\begin{equation}
 g_v''+g_v'+vg_v=0,\qquad g_v(0)=0,\quad g_v'(0)=1.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:green-ode}
\end{equation}
Its explicit entire solution is
\begin{equation}
 g_v(x)=e^{-x/2}
   \frac{\sin\!\bigl(x\sqrt{v-1/4}\bigr)}{\sqrt{v-1/4}},
 \label{spf:bc:eq:green}
\end{equation}
where the quotient is continued at $v=1/4$ and is understood via its power
series when $v<1/4$. On every compact $x$-set, it and all its $x$-derivatives
are bounded by a polynomial in $v$ times $\exp(C\sqrt v)$. Consequently
\begin{equation}
 K_0(x)=\int_0^\infty e^{-v}g_v(x)\,\mathrm d v
 \label{spf:bc:eq:K-integral}
\end{equation}
is entire, and differentiation under the integral is justified locally
uniformly.

\subsection{Evaluating the Green integral}

For real $x$, splitting \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K-integral} at $v=1/4$ and substituting
$v=1/4\pm u^2$ gives
\begin{equation}
 K_0(x)=2e^{-1/4-x/2}
 \left\{D(x/2)+\int_0^{1/2}e^{u^2}\sinh(xu)\,\mathrm d u\right\}.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:K-positive}
\end{equation}
In the first integral one uses
$\int_0^\infty e^{-u^2}\sin(xu)\,\mathrm d u=D(x/2)$; this follows by differentiating
the integral in $x$ and solving the same first-order equation as Dawson's
function. Completing squares in the second integral yields
\begin{equation}
 K_0(x)=\frac{\sqrt\pi}{2}e^{-(x+1)^2/4}
 \left\{\operatorname{erfi}\!\left(\frac{x+1}{2}\right)
       +\operatorname{erfi}\!\left(\frac{x-1}{2}\right)\right\}.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:K-erfi}
\end{equation}
This is \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K}; equality for complex $x$ follows by the identity theorem.
It also follows from \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K-positive} that
\begin{equation}
 K(x)>0\quad(x>0),\qquad K(0)=0,\quad K'(0)=1.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:K-positive-values}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Recovering the exponential generating function}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{spf:bc:thm:kernel} (manuscript~06)]
Put $d_m(v)=g_v^{(m)}(0)$. Equation \eqref{spf:bc:eq:green-ode} implies
$d_{m+2}=-d_{m+1}-vd_m$, and hence, formally,
\[
 \sum_{m\ge0}d_{m+1}(v)z^m=\frac1{1+z+vz^2}.
\]
Let $k_m=K^{(m)}(0)$. Integrating this identity coefficientwise gives
\[
 \mathcal Q(z):=\sum_{m\ge0}k_{m+1}z^m
      =\int_0^\infty\frac{e^{-v}}{1+z+vz^2}\,\mathrm d v.
\]
For the right side $\widetilde H$ of \eqref{spf:bc:eq:H}, using $k_0=0$ and $k_1=1$
yields
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{m\ge0}\widetilde H^{(m)}(0)z^m
 &=\frac{\mathcal Q(z)-1}{z}+4\mathcal Q(z)+6zQ(z)+4z^2\mathcal Q(z)+z^3\mathcal Q(z)\\
 &=\frac{(1+z)^4\mathcal Q(z)-1}{z}
  =\frac{\Psi(z)-1}{z}.
\end{align*}
Thus $\widetilde H^{(m)}(0)=\psi_{m+1}$ for every $m$. Both sides of
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:H} are entire, so equality of all Taylor coefficients proves the
claim. This also gives a direct proof of the entireness needed here.
\end{proof}

\section{Positive-axis asymptotics, including the integration constants}
\label{spf:bc:sec:real}

The two primitives in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:H} make integration constants important:
merely integrating a formal asymptotic series would not determine them. A
Laplace-transform identity fixes both constants.

\begin{lemma}[Laplace transform of the kernel; manuscript~06]
\label[lemma]{spf:bc:lem:LK}
For real $p>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathscr L K(p):=\int_0^\infty e^{-px}K(x)\,\mathrm d x
 =\int_0^\infty\frac{e^{-v}}{p^2+p+v}\,\mathrm d v
 =e^{p^2+p}E_1(p^2+p),
 \label{spf:bc:eq:LK}
\end{equation}
where $E_1(q)=\int_q^\infty e^{-u}\,\mathrm d u/u$ for $q>0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Taking the Laplace transform of \eqref{spf:bc:eq:green-ode} gives
$\mathscr L g_v(p)=1/(p^2+p+v)$. Fubini is legitimate for $p>0$: for
$v\ge1/4$, use \eqref{spf:bc:eq:green} and a polynomial bound near $v=1/4$;
for $0\le v\le1/4$, the hyperbolic-sine expression is bounded by a constant
times $1+x$. Both bounds are integrable after multiplication by
$e^{-px-v}$. The last equality is the substitution $u=p^2+p+v$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact leading real asymptotics; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:real}
As $x\to+\infty$,
\begin{align}
 K(x)&=\frac1x-\frac1{x^2}+\frac3{x^3}-\frac7{x^4}
                 +\bigO(x^{-5}),\label{spf:bc:eq:Kasym}\\
 \bcI K(x)&=\log x+\frac1x-\frac3{2x^2}
                    +\frac7{3x^3}+\bigO(x^{-4}),\label{spf:bc:eq:IKasym}\\
 \bcI^2K(x)&=x\log x-x+\log x+\frac3{2x}
                         -\frac7{6x^2}+\bigO(x^{-3}),\label{spf:bc:eq:I2Kasym}\\
 H(x)&=(x+5)\log x-x+\frac{23}{2x}-\frac{103}{6x^2}
                               +\bigO(x^{-3}).\label{spf:bc:eq:Hasym}
\end{align}
Each has a full expansion in descending integer powers, with the logarithms
displayed above. Consequently, as real $t\to+\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\widehat C(t)=e^t(t-1)+4t+7e^{-t}-8e^{-2t}
                                  +\bigO(e^{-3t}).}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Basym}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
The standard Dawson expansion, obtained from the error-function expansions
in \cite{bcDlmfErf}, is
\[
 D(z)\sim\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(2j-1)!!}{2^{j+1}z^{2j+1}}
\]
on the positive real axis and uniformly on closed subsectors of
$|\arg z|<\pi/4$. Its coefficients also follow from $D'+2zD=1$.
Substitution in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K} proves \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Kasym} to every order; the
second term of \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K} is exponentially small there. Integration first
gives, with undetermined constants $\nu_1,\nu_2$,
\begin{align*}
 \bcI K(x)&=\log x+\nu_1+x^{-1}-\tfrac32x^{-2}+\cdots,\\
 \bcI^2K(x)&=x\log x+(\nu_1-1)x+\log x+\nu_2
                              +\tfrac32x^{-1}+\cdots.
\end{align*}
These expansions with constants are rigorous: after subtracting sufficiently
many terms, the remaining derivative is integrable at infinity.

Let $L(p)=-\gamma-\log p$. The convergent small-argument expansion of $E_1$
\cite{bcDlmfEone} and \eqref{spf:bc:eq:LK} give
\begin{equation}
 \mathscr L K(p)=L(p)+pL(p)
          +p^2\left(\tfrac32L(p)+\tfrac54\right)
          +\bigO(p^3|\log p|),\qquad p\downarrow0.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:LK-small}
\end{equation}
Since $\mathscr L(\bcI K)=\mathscr L K/p$, comparison with
$\mathscr L(\log x)=L(p)/p$ shows $\nu_1=0$: a nonzero $\nu_1$ would produce a
term $\nu_1/p$, whereas the remaining difference is only
$\bigO(|\log p|)$. Likewise
\[
 \mathscr L(x\log x-x)=L(p)/p^2,\qquad
 \mathscr L(\bcI^2K)=\mathscr L K/p^2.
\]
With $\nu_1=0$, subtracting the transforms of $x\log x-x+\log x$ leaves
only $\bigO(|\log p|)$ in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:LK-small}, so $\nu_2=0$.
The Abelian comparisons used here are justified directly by splitting each
integral at $x=1$; a remainder $\bigO(1/x)$ contributes
$\bigO(|\log p|)$. No Tauberian inference is being made.

Substitution into \eqref{spf:bc:eq:H}, and differentiation of the Dawson expansions,
now gives \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Hasym}. Finally set $x=e^t-1$, expand
$\log x=t+\log(1-e^{-t})$, and collect powers of $e^{-t}$. This gives
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:Basym} and its all-orders continuation.
\end{proof}

\section{The canonical positive-ray sum}
\label{spf:bc:sec:sum}

\begin{definition}[Canonical completion; manuscript~06]
For $\Re n>1$, define
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bcC(n)=1+\int_0^\infty e^{-nt}\widehat C(t)\,\mathrm d t
       =1+\int_0^\infty\frac{H(x)}{(1+x)^{n+1}}\,\mathrm d x.}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Cstar}
\end{equation}
The real logarithm is used in $(1+x)^{n+1}$ for $x\ge0$. Define also
\begin{equation}
 \bcA(n)=\frac{\Gamma(n+1)}{e}\bcC(n).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Astar}
\end{equation}
The word canonical refers to this specified positive-ray prescription. It
is not an assertion that the algebraic sector uniquely determines a counting
function among all functions sharing its asymptotic expansion.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Existence and Poincar\'e expansion; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:sum}
The integral in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Cstar} converges absolutely exactly in
$\Re n>1$ and is holomorphic there. It fails to converge as an ordinary
improper integral at every $n$ with $\Re n\le1$. For each fixed $M\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \bcC(n)=\sum_{J=0}^M\frac{c_J}{n^J}+\bigO_M(n^{-M-1})
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Cstar-asym}
\end{equation}
as positive real $n\to\infty$. The corresponding expansion is uniform in
closed cones $|\arg n|\le\pi/2-\varepsilon$ as $|n|\to\infty$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Equation \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Basym} proves absolute convergence and locally uniform
domination in the stated half-plane. At $\Re n=1$ the leading integrand is
$(t-1)e^{-\mathrm i(\Im n)t}$, which has no convergent improper integral;
for $\Re n<1$ its antiderivative grows exponentially. The remaining terms
cannot cancel this leading obstruction.

For the asymptotic expansion, take a fixed small $t_0>0$, Taylor-expand the
entire function $\widehat C$ through degree $M-1$ on $[0,t_0]$, and use
$\int_0^\infty e^{-nt}t^{J-1}\,\mathrm d t=(J-1)!n^{-J}$.
The local Taylor remainder contributes $\bigO(n^{-M-1})$, and
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:Basym} bounds the tail by a polynomial times
$e^{-(n-1)t_0}$. The same proof with $\Re n\ge |n|\sin\varepsilon$
gives the cone-uniform version. This is the usual Watson-lemma argument
\cite{bcDlmfWatson}, written here to specify the actual integral and tail bound.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A single positive quadrature}

Put $\mathcal J(a)=\int_0^\infty K(x)(1+x)^{-a}\,\mathrm d x$, initially for $\Re a>0$.
Integration by parts in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Cstar} gives
\begin{align}
 \bcC(n)={}&(n+1)(n+2)\mathcal J(n+3)+4(n+1)\mathcal J(n+2)+6\mathcal J(n+1)\notag\\
          &+\frac4n\mathcal J(n)+\frac1{n(n-1)}\mathcal J(n-1).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Jformula}
\end{align}
Indeed the $K''$ term contributes $-1+(n+1)(n+2)\mathcal J(n+3)$, cancelling the
initial $1$; all other boundary terms vanish. The primitives are removed by
Fubini, for example
\[
 \int_0^\infty\frac{\bcI^2K(x)}{(1+x)^{n+1}}\,\mathrm d x
 =\frac{\mathcal J(n-1)}{n(n-1)}.
\]
Thus $\bcC(n)>0$ for real $n>1$, by \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K-positive}. The substitution
$y=(1+x)^{-1}$ gives the numerically convenient formula
\begin{align}
 \bcC(n)=\int_0^1 &K(y^{-1}-1)y^{n-3}\biggl[
 (n+1)(n+2)y^4+4(n+1)y^3+6y^2\notag\\
 &\hspace{39mm}+\frac{4y}{n}+\frac1{n(n-1)}\biggr]\,\mathrm d y.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:quadrature}
\end{align}
No nested primitive or numerical differentiation is needed in this quadrature.

\subsection{Meromorphic continuation}

\begin{corollary}[Pole structure of the completion; manuscript~06]
\label[corollary]{spf:bc:cor:meromorphic}
The function $\bcC$ continues meromorphically to the whole $n$-plane. Its only
possible poles are $1,0,-1,-2,\ldots$. At the first four locations its principal
parts are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{array}{c|c}
 n=1&(n-1)^{-2}-(n-1)^{-1}\\
 n=0&4n^{-2}\\
 n=-1&7(n+1)^{-1}\\
 n=-2&-8(n+2)^{-1}.
 \end{array}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:poles}
\end{equation}
All possible poles at negative integers are simple. A coefficient may vanish,
so no assertion that every possible pole occurs is needed.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
For any $q$, \cref{spf:bc:thm:real} gives
\[
 \widehat C(t)=e^t(t-1)+4t+\sum_{j=1}^q\beta_je^{-jt}
                           +\bigO_q(e^{-(q+1)t}),
 \qquad \beta_1=7,\quad\beta_2=-8.
\]
Split the Laplace integral at a fixed positive $t_0$ and subtract the displayed
terms on its tail. The subtracted integral is holomorphic in
$\Re n>-(q+1)$; integrating the finite explicit sum gives the claimed
principal parts. These continuations agree on overlaps, and $q$ is arbitrary.
\end{proof}

\section{Complex growth and the sharp strip geometry}
\label{spf:bc:sec:complex}

The real asymptotics alone do not settle sectorial summability. The decisive
feature is the Gaussian term in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K-erfi}, which is small in one cone
and very large in another.

\begin{theorem}[Growth of the entire kernel; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:complex-H}
The entire function $H$ has order two and type $1/4$. More explicitly,
\begin{equation}
 \log\max_{|x|\le R}|H(x)|=\tfrac14R^2+\bigO(R+\log R).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Htype}
\end{equation}
Along the positive imaginary axis,
\begin{equation}
 H(\mathrm i y)=-\frac{\mathrm i\sqrt\pi}{4}e^{-1/4}y^2
                  \exp\!\left(\frac{y^2}{4}-\frac{\mathrm i y}{2}\right)
                  \left(1+\bigO(y^{-1})\right).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Himag}
\end{equation}
Uniformly on every closed cone in $\pi/4<\arg x<3\pi/4$,
\begin{equation}
 H(x)=\frac{\mathrm i\sqrt\pi}{4}x^2e^{-(x+1)^2/4}
                         \left(1+\bigO(|x|^{-1})\right).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Hcone}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
The integral form
$D(z)=z\int_0^1\exp[-z^2(1-u^2)]\,\mathrm d u$ gives the global bound
$|D(z)|\le |z|\exp(|z|^2)$. Applied to \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K}, it gives
\[
 |K(x)|\le C(1+|x|)\exp\!\left(\tfrac14|x|^2+C|x|+C\right).
\]
Cauchy's derivative estimates on circles of radius one, and direct estimates
on the primitives along $[0,x]$, give the same quadratic exponent for $H$.
This proves the upper bound in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Htype}.

In a closed cone inside $\pi/4<\arg x<3\pi/4$, the error-function asymptotics
\cite{bcDlmfErf} give
\begin{gather*}
 \operatorname{erfi}((x+1)/2)=\mathrm i+\text{exponentially small terms},\\
 \operatorname{erfi}((x-1)/2)=\mathrm i+\text{exponentially small terms}.
\end{gather*}
Consequently $K(x)\sim\mathrm i\sqrt\pi e^{-(x+1)^2/4}$. Differentiating its
explicit expression twice gives the leading factor $x^2/4$. The terms $K'$
and $K$ in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:H} are lower by powers of $x$. Endpoint integration,
or one integration by parts in the quadratic exponential, shows
$\bcI K=\bigO(K/x)$ and $\bcI^2K=\bigO(K/x^2)$ in these cones; contributions
from a fixed bounded initial segment are negligible. These bounds are uniform
on each closed cone, since $-\Re x^2\ge c|x|^2$ there. This proves
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:Hcone}. Its specialization to $x=\mathrm i y$ is \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Himag},
which proves the lower bound in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Htype} and fixes the type.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp horizontal-strip threshold; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:strip}
For every $0<\delta<\pi/4$, there is a constant $C_\delta$ such that
\begin{equation}
 |\widehat C(s+\mathrm i\theta)|\le C_\delta(1+s)e^s
 \quad(s\ge0,\ |\theta|\le\delta).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:strip-bound}
\end{equation}
For each fixed $\theta\in(\pi/4,\pi/2)$,
\begin{align}
 \widehat C(s+\mathrm i\theta)
 &=\frac{\mathrm i\sqrt\pi}{4}(e^{s+\mathrm i\theta}-1)^2
    \exp\!\left(-\frac{e^{2s+2\mathrm i\theta}}4\right)
                      (1+\bigO(e^{-s})),\label{spf:bc:eq:strip-outside}\\
 \log|\widehat C(s+\mathrm i\theta)|
 &=-\frac{\cos(2\theta)}4e^{2s}+\bigO(s).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:strip-outside-log}
\end{align}
Thus no wider open horizontal strip centered on the positive real axis has
an exponential bound of the form $C\exp(A\Re t)$. The threshold statement
concerns open strips; no boundary claim at $|\Im t|=\pi/4$ is required.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
For $x=e^{s+\mathrm i\theta}-1$ and $|\theta|\le\delta<\pi/4$, the algebraic
Dawson expansion gives $K(x)=\bigO(1/|x|)$. Its primitives are respectively
$\bigO(\log|x|)$ and $\bigO(|x|\log|x|)$, uniformly in a slightly larger
closed cone. For the second Dawson term in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:K}, the factor
$e^{-x}$ supplies exponential decay. Equation \eqref{spf:bc:eq:H} then proves
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:strip-bound}, with bounded $s$ absorbed into the constant.
For fixed $\theta>\pi/4$, apply \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Hcone} and use
$x+1=e^{s+\mathrm i\theta}$ exactly. Any strip of half-width greater than
$\pi/4$ contains such a horizontal line with $\theta<\pi/2$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Why a positive-ray sum is not sectorial 1-summability}

\begin{definition}[The sectorial convention used here; manuscript~06]
A formal series in $1/n$ is \emph{sectorially 1-summable in direction zero}
if its order-one Borel germ continues to an open angular sector
$|\arg t|<\varepsilon$ about that direction and satisfies an exponential
bound $C\exp(A|t|)$ on some smaller such sector for large $|t|$.
Its positive-ray Borel--Laplace sum, by contrast, requires only an appropriate
continuation and growth bound along the integration ray. These are distinct
requirements in this manuscript.
\end{definition}

\begin{corollary}[Ray summation without an angular sector; manuscript~06]
\label[corollary]{spf:bc:cor:nonsectorial}
The formal expansion \eqref{spf:bc:eq:prior-asymptotic} has the positive-ray sum
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:Cstar}, but is not sectorially 1-summable in direction zero under
the stated convention.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Take $t_y=\log(1+\mathrm i y)$, with the principal logarithm. Then
$\arg t_y\to0$ and $\widehat C(t_y)=H(\mathrm i y)$. By \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Himag},
\[
 \log|\widehat C(t_y)|=\tfrac14y^2+\bigO(\log y),\qquad |t_y|\sim\log y.
\]
Every open angular sector about the positive $t$-axis eventually contains
these points, so no exponential bound in $|t|$ is possible there.
The positive-ray assertion is \cref{spf:bc:thm:sum}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[No finite Borel singularity is involved; manuscript~06]
The function $\widehat C$ is entire and $2\pi\mathrm i$-periodic. The obstruction above is
at infinity, not a finite singularity crossed by a contour. Gaussian sector
changes in the explicit Dawson kernel explain the strip boundary, but do not
by themselves determine the Stokes data or the nonperturbative terms of the
original counting problem. General cautions about exponentially improved
asymptotics and Stokes phenomena are discussed in \cite{bcDlmfStokes}.
\end{remark}

\section{Sharp large-order growth and non-holonomicity}
\label{spf:bc:sec:growth}

For an entire function $f$, put $M_f(R)=\max_{|z|\le R}|f(z)|$.

\begin{theorem}[Double-exponential maximum growth; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:Btype}
The Borel transform satisfies the sharp limit
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\lim_{R\to\infty}e^{-2R}\log M_{\widehat C}(R)=\frac14.}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Btype}
\end{equation}
In particular it is an entire function of infinite order.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
For $|t|\le R$, one has $|e^t-1|\le e^R+1$. The global estimate in the
proof of \cref{spf:bc:thm:complex-H} gives
\[
 \log M_{\widehat C}(R)\le\tfrac14(e^R+1)^2+\bigO(e^R+R),
\]
which proves the upper bound in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Btype}.
For the lower bound choose $y=y(R)>0$ so that
$|\log(1+\mathrm i y)|=R$. Such a $y$ exists uniquely for large $R$, and
\[
 \log y=R-\frac{\pi^2}{8R}+o(R^{-1}),\qquad y/e^R\longrightarrow1.
\]
Applying \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Himag} at $t=\log(1+\mathrm i y)$ gives
$\log M_{\widehat C}(R)\ge y^2/4+\bigO(\log y)$, proving the lower bound.
Thus $\log\log M_{\widehat C}(R)=2R-\log4+o(1)$, so
$\log\log M_{\widehat C}(R)/\log R\to\infty$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact root-growth scale of the coefficients; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:coeff-growth}
With $\hat c_m=c_{m+1}/m!$, one has
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\limsup_{m\to\infty}(\log m)|\hat c_m|^{1/m}=2.}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:rootgrowth}
\end{equation}
The ordinary formal power series $\sum_{J\ge0}c_Jz^J$ has radius of
convergence zero. Its coefficients are Gevrey one of type zero, meaning
that for every $A>0$ there is $C_A$ with $|c_J|\le C_A A^J J!$.
They are not Gevrey of any order $s<1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Cauchy's inequality and \cref{spf:bc:thm:Btype} give
$|\hat c_m|\le M_{\widehat C}(R)R^{-m}$. Choose
$R=\tfrac12\log m-\log\log m$ for sufficiently large $m$. Since
$\log M_{\widehat C}(R)=\bigO(m/(\log m)^2)$, we obtain
\[
 |\hat c_m|^{1/m}\le\frac{\exp(\bigO((\log m)^{-2}))}
                       {\tfrac12\log m-\log\log m},
\]
proving that the limsup in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:rootgrowth} is at most two.

For the reverse bound, suppose there were numbers $a<a'<2$ such that
$|\hat c_m|\le(a/\log m)^m$ for all sufficiently large $m$. Put
$N_R=\lceil e^{a'R}\rceil$. For $m>N_R$, one has
$|\hat c_m|R^m\le(a/a')^m$, so that part of the absolute Taylor sum is bounded
by a geometric tail. For $m\le N_R$, Cauchy's bound at radius one gives the
uniform estimate $|\hat c_m|\le M_{\widehat C}(1)$; for $R>1$ the sum of these terms is
at most $M_{\widehat C}(1)(N_R+1)R^{N_R}$. Thus
\[
 \log\log M_{\widehat C}(R)\le a'R+o(R),
\]
contradicting \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Btype}. This proves \eqref{spf:bc:eq:rootgrowth}.

Along a subsequence, $|\hat c_m|^{1/m}$ is bounded below by a positive constant
times $1/\log m$. Stirling's formula then gives coefficient roots for
$c_{m+1}$ of order at least $m/\log m$, which tend to infinity. This proves
zero radius. The type-zero Gevrey-one bound follows from entireness of
$\widehat C$ by Cauchy's inequality at an arbitrary fixed radius. If a Gevrey-$s$
bound held with $s<1$, the same subsequence, divided by $((m+1)!)^s$, would
have roots at least a constant times $m^{1-s}/\log m$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

[Note of the batch-97 write.] The type-zero Gevrey-one bound is Part~III's
\eqref{spf:rc:eq:subfactorial}, proved there by the same Cauchy estimate on
the entire function $\widehat C$; what is new here is the exact limsup and
the failure of every Gevrey bound of order $s<1$, which answer the growth half
of Part~III's question~3 and of Part~I's question~4 for $(2,2)$. Signs,
zeros and a termwise equivalent remain open (\cref{spf:bc:sec:questions},
question~3).

\begin{remark}[What the limsup does not say; manuscript~06]
Equation \eqref{spf:bc:eq:rootgrowth} is an exact envelope-scale result, not a
termwise asymptotic equivalent for $c_J$. It does not describe the signs,
zeros, or oscillatory cancellation of individual coefficients. In particular
it is not legitimate to replace every $c_J$ by
$J!(2/\log J)^J$ in a numerical approximation.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[The correction coefficients are not P-recursive; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:nonholonomic}
There is no nonzero finite-order linear recurrence with polynomial
coefficients in $J$ satisfied eventually by the sequence $(c_J)$. Equivalently,
its formal ordinary generating series is not D-finite.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Recall that a sequence is P-recursive if it satisfies such a recurrence;
its ordinary generating series is then D-finite, meaning that it satisfies
a nonzero linear differential equation with polynomial coefficients.
This equivalence follows by multiplying the recurrence by powers of the
series variable and translating $J$ into the Euler differential operator.
The converse follows by comparing Taylor coefficients.

If $c_J$ were P-recursive, then so would be $\hat c_m=c_{m+1}/m!$: substitute
$c_{m+1+k}=(m+k)!\hat c_{m+k}$ in its recurrence and divide by $m!$; factorial
ratios for fixed $k$ are polynomials, after an index shift if necessary.
Therefore the entire function $\widehat C(t)=\sum \hat c_mt^m$ would be D-finite.

Every entire D-finite function has finite order. For completeness, take its
polynomial differential equation, choose a circle outside every zero of the
leading polynomial, and write the equation as a first-order system
$Y'=A(z)Y$ for the function and its derivatives. Outside that circle, the
rational matrix $A$ satisfies $\|A(z)\|\le C|z|^d$ for some integer $d\ge0$.
The initial vector is uniformly bounded on the circle. Integration along
radial segments and Gronwall's inequality give
$\|Y(z)\|\le C'\exp(C'|z|^{d+1})$ in every direction, uniformly. Thus its
first component has finite order. This contradicts \cref{spf:bc:thm:Btype}.
\end{proof}

This theorem concerns the asymptotic correction array, not the original
integer sequence $a_n$, and not the sampled analytic function $\bcA(n)$.
Indeed, \cref{spf:bc:sec:operator} proves a polynomial recurrence for the latter.
There is no rule asserting that asymptotic coefficient extraction preserves
P-recursiveness.

\section{A justified growing-order truncation}
\label{spf:bc:sec:truncation}

The fixed-order theorem \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Cstar-asym} does not permit its truncation
order to grow with $n$ without further estimates. The explicit complex growth
bound supplies them.

\begin{theorem}[Superexponential truncation for the canonical completion; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:truncation}
For positive real $n$ sufficiently large, put
\begin{equation}
 R=\tfrac12\log n,\qquad t_0=R-\tfrac12,\qquad
 M=\lfloor n(R-1)\rfloor.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:truncation-rule}
\end{equation}
There are constants $A,n_0$ such that, for $n\ge n_0$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\left|\bcC(n)-\sum_{J=0}^{M}\frac{c_J}{n^J}\right|
       \le\exp\!\left(-\tfrac12n\log n+An\right).}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:truncation}
\end{equation}
The rule uses $M\sim\tfrac12n\log n$ coefficients. The theorem does not
claim an optimal lower bound or an explicit numerical onset $n_0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Write $P_{M-1}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\hat c_mt^m$. By the global estimate underlying
\cref{spf:bc:thm:Btype}, $M_{\widehat C}(R)\le\exp(Cn)$ for the value of $R$ in
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:truncation-rule}. On $0\le t\le t_0$, the geometric Taylor-tail bound is
\[
 |\widehat C(t)-P_{M-1}(t)|\le
 \frac{M_{\widehat C}(R)}{1-t_0/R}\left(\frac tR\right)^M.
\]
Thus its Laplace integral over $[0,t_0]$ is at most
\begin{equation}
 \frac{M_{\widehat C}(R)}{1-t_0/R}\frac{M!}{n^{M+1}R^M}.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:local-truncation}
\end{equation}
Since $M=n(R-1)+\bigO(1)$, Stirling's formula gives
\[
 \log\left(\frac{M!}{n^{M+1}R^M}\right)
 =M\log\frac{M}{nR}-M+\bigO(\log(nM))
 =-nR+\bigO(n).
\]
The prefactor in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:local-truncation} changes the exponent by only
$\bigO(n)$.

The actual Borel tail on $[t_0,\infty)$ is controlled by
$|\widehat C(t)|\le C(1+t)e^t$, so its integral is at most
\[
 C\left(\frac{t_0+1}{n-1}+\frac1{(n-1)^2}\right)e^{-(n-1)t_0}
 =\exp(-nR+\bigO(n)).
\]
It remains to bound the tail of the Taylor polynomial, because the complete
monomial Laplace integrals, not only their parts up to $t_0$, produce the
coefficients in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:truncation}.
For $0\le m<M$, the inequality
$\log(t/t_0)\le(t-t_0)/t_0$ gives
\[
 \int_T^\infty e^{-nt}t^m\,\mathrm d t
 \le\frac{e^{-nt_0}t_0^m}{n-m/t_0}.
\]
The denominator is at least $n/(2t_0)$, since $M\le n(R-1)$ and
$t_0=R-1/2$. Summing Cauchy's bounds $|\hat c_m|\le M_{\widehat C}(R)R^{-m}$ yields
\[
 \int_T^\infty e^{-nt}|P_{M-1}(t)|\,\mathrm d t
 \le M_{\widehat C}(R)e^{-nt_0}\frac{2t_0}{n}\frac1{1-t_0/R}
 =\exp(-nR+\bigO(n)).
\]
Adding these three bounds and using $nR=\tfrac12n\log n$ proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The essential limitation.}
Equation \eqref{spf:bc:eq:truncation} is proved for $\bcC(n)$, not for
$e a_n/n!$. The earlier combinatorial theorem supplies fixed-$M$ bounds;
it does not, merely by being true for every fixed $M$, supply the
$M\asymp n\log n$ uniformity needed here. Transferring the exponential
remainder estimate to the actual counts requires an independent estimate of
the flat discrepancy studied below.

\section{A differential equation and an exact shift equation}
\label{spf:bc:sec:shift}

The prior formal series obeys
\begin{equation}
 z^3(1+z)\Psi'(z)+(2+3z+3z^2-2z^3)\Psi(z)=(z+2)(z+1)^4.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Psi-ode}
\end{equation}
This identity follows directly by substituting
$F(w)=\sum_{j\ge0}j!w^j$ and its formal equation
$w^2F'(w)+(w-1)F(w)=-1$ into \eqref{spf:bc:eq:psi}; it is also recorded in
\cite{bcProveIt}. [Note of the batch-97 write: it is \eqref{spf:rc:eq:Psi-ode},
and $F$ is Part~III's $\mathcal F$ of \eqref{spf:rc:eq:Euler-series}; the next
display is \eqref{spf:rc:eq:psi-recurrence}.] Coefficient comparison gives
\begin{equation}
 2\psi_i+3\psi_{i-1}+(i+1)\psi_{i-2}+(i-5)\psi_{i-3}=0
 \qquad(i\ge6).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:psi-rec}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Entire differential equations; manuscript~06]
\label[proposition]{spf:bc:prop:ode}
The functions $H$ and $\widehat C$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
 2H'''+(x+3)H''+(x+5)H'-H=6+x,
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Hode}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{align}
 2\widehat C'''+(-6+2e^t+e^{2t})\widehat C''
 +(4-2e^t+3e^{2t}+e^{3t})\widehat C'
 -e^{3t}\widehat C=e^{4t}+5e^{3t}.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Bode}
\end{align}
The initial data are $\widehat C(0)=3$, $\widehat C'(0)=2$, $\widehat C''(0)=1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
The coefficient of $x^m/m!$ on the left of \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Hode} is
\[
 2\psi_{m+4}+3\psi_{m+3}+(m+5)\psi_{m+2}+(m-1)\psi_{m+1}.
\]
It vanishes for $m\ge2$ by \eqref{spf:bc:eq:psi-rec}; its values for $m=0,1$ are
$6,1$, respectively. Since $H$ is entire, this coefficient proof is an
identity of entire functions. Apply $x=e^t-1$ and the chain rule, then
multiply by $e^{3t}$, to get \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Bode}. The initial data follow from
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:first-c}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Inhomogeneous shift equation; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:shift}
For $\Re n>4$, and hence meromorphically wherever both sides are defined,
\begin{align}
 &2n(n-1)(n-2)\bcC(n)+2(n-1)(n-2)\bcC(n-1)\notag\\
 &\hspace{13mm}+(n-2)(n+1)\bcC(n-2)+(n-4)\bcC(n-3)=\mathcal R(n),
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Cshift}\\
 &\mathcal R(n)=2n^3+3n^2-7n+\frac1{n-4}+\frac5{n-3}.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:R}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Set $S(n)=\bcC(n)-1=\mathscr L\widehat C(n)$. The real asymptotics of $\widehat C$ and its
first three derivatives justify integration by parts for $\Re n>4$.
For example,
\[
 \mathscr L\widehat C'(n)=nS(n)-3,\quad
 \mathscr L\widehat C''(n)=n^2S(n)-3n-2,\quad
 \mathscr L\widehat C'''(n)=n^3S(n)-3n^2-2n-1.
\]
Multiplication by $e^{kt}$ shifts $n$ to $n-k$. Applying these rules to
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:Bode} gives the four coefficients in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Cshift}, acting
on $S$, together with the boundary polynomial $-6n^2+5n-2$. The transform
of the right side is $1/(n-4)+5/(n-3)$. Replacing $S$ by $\bcC-1$ and using
\[
 2n(n-1)(n-2)+2(n-1)(n-2)+(n-2)(n+1)+(n-4)
 =2n^3-3n^2-2n-2
\]
proves \eqref{spf:bc:eq:R}. Meromorphic continuation follows from
\cref{spf:bc:cor:meromorphic}.
\end{proof}

\section{The OEIS operator annihilates the analytic completion}
\label{spf:bc:sec:operator}

The OEIS entry records the following factorization of its conjectured
order-thirteen recurrence, attributed there to Mark van Hoeij \cite{bcOeis}.
For functions of $n$, define
\begin{align}
 (Tf)(n)={}&f(n+3)+f(n+2)+\left(\frac n2+2\right)f(n+1)
                           +\frac{n-1}{2}f(n),\label{spf:bc:eq:T}\\
 (Ug)(n)={}&g(n+4)+\left(\frac n2+2\right)g(n+2)
                          -\frac12g(n+1)+\frac{1-n}{2}g(n),\label{spf:bc:eq:U}\\
 (Vh)(n)={}&h(n+6)-(n+11)h(n+5)
                  -\left(2n+\frac{75}{4}\right)h(n+4)\notag\\
          &+\frac{3-n}{4}h(n+3)-\frac12h(n+2)
                         -\frac{7n+22}{4}h(n+1)-nh(n).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:V}
\end{align}
The displayed conjecture is $(VUTa)(n)=0$. We prove the corresponding
identity for $\bcA$, not for $a$.

\begin{theorem}[Analytic realization of the factorization; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:operator}
Define
\begin{align}
 Q(n)={}&2n^5+19n^4+44n^3-5n^2-54n-5,\label{spf:bc:eq:Qpoly}\\
 P(n)={}&4n^8+132n^7+1745n^6+11926n^5+45249n^4\notag\\
       &+94552n^3+99549n^2+39956n+4.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Ppoly}
\end{align}
For $\Re n>1$,
\begin{equation}
 (T\bcA)(n)=\frac{\Gamma(n-1)}{2e}Q(n),\qquad
 (UT\bcA)(n)=\frac{\Gamma(n)}{4e}P(n),\qquad
 (VUT\bcA)(n)=0.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:factor-realization}
\end{equation}
Moreover $\bcA$ satisfies the fourth-order polynomial-coefficient equation
\begin{equation}
 Q(n)(T\bcA)(n+1)-(n-1)Q(n+1)(T\bcA)(n)=0.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:order-four}
\end{equation}
No minimal-order assertion is made.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Replace $n$ by $n+3$ in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Cshift} and multiply by
$\Gamma(n+1)/(2e)$. Using \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Astar} gives
\begin{equation}
 (T\bcA)(n)=\frac{\Gamma(n+1)}{2e}\mathcal R(n+3).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Tforcing}
\end{equation}
A direct expansion shows
\[
 \mathcal R(n+3)=2n^3+21n^2+65n+60+\frac1{n-1}+\frac5n
               =\frac{Q(n)}{n(n-1)}.
\]
This proves the first formula in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:factor-realization}; initially
$\Re n>1$ follows from the half-plane used in \cref{spf:bc:thm:shift}.
Substituting that formula in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:U}, using the Gamma shift identity,
and collecting powers of $n$ gives the second formula with the polynomial
$P$ in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Ppoly}.

For a transparent certificate of the last identity, write $\ell_k(n)$ for
the coefficients of $h(n+k)$ in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:V}, $1\le k\le6$. After dividing
by $\Gamma(n+1)/(4e)$ and clearing the remaining denominators, the desired
identity is the polynomial identity
\begin{equation}
 -4P(n)+\sum_{k=1}^6 4\ell_k(n)\rise{(n+1)}{k-1}P(n+k)=0.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:certificate}
\end{equation}
The six polynomials $4\ell_k$ are, in order,
\[
 -(7n+22),\quad-2,\quad3-n,\quad-(8n+75),\quad-4(n+11),\quad4.
\]
Expansion with the displayed $P$ gives the zero polynomial. The accompanying
standard-library program \path{code/factor_certificate.py} (shipped as
\path{code/05-borel-completion-factor_certificate.py}) performs precisely
this integer-polynomial identity, returning the single coefficient list
\texttt{[0]}; no sampling or recurrence guessing is involved.
Finally, applying $\Gamma(n)=(n-1)\Gamma(n-1)$ to the first formula proves
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:order-four}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Why this is not the counting recurrence.}
The same differential and shift equations that determine an analytic
completion do not select the integer counts from all functions with the
same algebraic asymptotics. The fourth-order equation above is an especially
clear warning: it describes the analytic core, not a newly proved shorter
recurrence for A189281. In fact the next section proves that the two objects
cannot agree everywhere.

\section{An unavoidable beyond-all-orders correction}
\label{spf:bc:sec:flat}

For integers $n\ge2$, define the normalized discrepancy
\begin{equation}
 \delta_n=\frac{e a_n}{n!}-\bcC(n).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:delta}
\end{equation}
Combining the prior counting theorem \eqref{spf:bc:eq:prior-asymptotic} with
\cref{spf:bc:thm:sum} immediately gives
\begin{equation}
 \delta_n=\bigO_M(n^{-M})\quad\text{for every fixed }M.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:flat}
\end{equation}
We call this \emph{flat at inverse-power scale}. This does not mean that it
is zero or that its precise exponential scale is known.

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic obstruction to exact recovery; manuscript~06]
\label{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero}
For every integer $m\ge2$, at least one of
\begin{equation}
 \delta_m,\quad\delta_{m+1},\quad\delta_{m+2},\quad\delta_{m+3}
 \label{spf:bc:eq:block}
\end{equation}
is nonzero. In particular, the positive-ray Borel completion does not recover
the original count at every integer, even after discarding any finite set of
initial indices. The nonzero flat discrepancy has no gap of four consecutive
indices.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Suppose all four quantities in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:block} vanished. Then
$\bcA(j)=a_j\in\Z$ for $j=m,m+1,m+2,m+3$, and \eqref{spf:bc:eq:T} would make
$(T\bcA)(m)$ rational (indeed a half-integer). But \cref{spf:bc:thm:operator} gives
\begin{equation}
 (T\bcA)(m)=\frac{(m-2)!Q(m)}{2e}.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:irrational-forcing}
\end{equation}
The numerator is a nonzero integer: $Q(m)=m(m-1)\mathcal R(m+3)>0$ for
$m\ge2$, as seen from \eqref{spf:bc:eq:R}. Since $e$ is irrational, the right
side of \eqref{spf:bc:eq:irrational-forcing} is irrational, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The arithmetic input is elementary; manuscript~06]
Only the irrationality of $e$, not its transcendence, is used. One standard
proof is to assume $e=p/q$ with $q\ge2$, multiply its factorial series by
$q!$, and observe that the remaining tail is a strictly positive number less
than one whereas the difference of the two other terms is an integer.
Thus the obstruction does not rely on a numerical test of equality or on an
unproved transcendence assertion about special-function values.
\end{remark}

\subsection{What information an algebraic sector loses}

There are now three rigorously distinguished levels. The formal coefficients
$c_J$ determine the entire Borel transform uniquely. The chosen positive-ray
Laplace prescription determines $\bcC$ uniquely. But neither of those facts
forces $\bcA(n)$ to be the original integer count. The difference is not
merely a hypothetical freedom to add an arbitrary flat function:
\cref{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero} proves that this specific counting problem actually
needs such a correction.

The arithmetic theorem is stronger than proving one accidental discrepancy
at a small index. It forbids eventual exact agreement and locates a discrepancy
in every block of four. It is weaker than asserting $\delta_n\ne0$ for every
$n$, and it gives neither a universal sign nor a leading asymptotic equivalent.
The numerical diagnostics in \cref{spf:bc:sec:verification} even suggest a sign
change within the tested range. No sign theorem is inferred from them.
[Note of the batch-97 write: the table there samples only $n\equiv0\pmod4$
and $n=30$; the full range $2\le n\le30$ shows fourteen sign changes, not one
(Remark~\ref{spf:bc:rem:corrections}).]

The decomposition
\begin{equation}
 a_n=\frac{n!}{e}\bigl(\bcC(n)+\delta_n\bigr)
 \label{spf:bc:eq:decomposition}
\end{equation}
is therefore a genuine separation of a canonical analytic sector from
unresolved beyond-all-orders information. It is not yet a full transseries
for the exact sequence.

\section{Index inversion and the propagation of flat information}
\label{spf:bc:sec:inverse}

The ProveIt transseries program and the earlier path-forest report already
provide Lambert-$W$ inversion of Gamma-scale counting functions \cite{bcProveIt}.
We do not claim that inversion method as new. The completion allows a precise
comparison of its smooth inverse with inversion along the exact counts.

\begin{proposition}[Smooth inverse and flat displacement; manuscript~06]
\label[proposition]{spf:bc:prop:inverse}
The real function $\bcA(x)$ is positive and strictly increasing for all
sufficiently large $x$. Let $\bcN$ denote its inverse on that final interval.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \bcN(a_n)-n
 =\frac{\delta_n}{\bcC(n)(\log n+\bigO(n^{-1}))}
                     +\bigO\!\left(\frac{\delta_n^2}{\log n}\right).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:flat-inverse}
\end{equation}
In particular $\bcN(a_n)-n=\bigO_M(n^{-M})$ for every fixed $M$, and eventual
nearest-integer recovery of $n$ holds. The discrepancy in the smooth inverse
is nonzero at some index in every block of four sufficiently large indices.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~06)]
Positivity follows from \eqref{spf:bc:eq:Jformula}. Differentiating the Laplace
integral, with the same local Taylor argument as in \cref{spf:bc:thm:sum}, gives
$\bcC'(x)=-3x^{-2}+\bigO(x^{-3})$. The differentiated Stirling expansion
\cite{dlmfGamma} gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\bcA'(x)}{\bcA(x)}
 =\frac{\Gamma'(x+1)}{\Gamma(x+1)}+\frac{\bcC'(x)}{\bcC(x)}
 =\log x+\bigO(x^{-1}).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:logderivative}
\end{equation}
Thus an increasing inverse exists eventually. Since
$a_n/\bcA(n)=1+\delta_n/\bcC(n)$, Taylor expansion of that inverse about
$\bcA(n)$ gives \eqref{spf:bc:eq:flat-inverse}. The second derivative estimate needed
for the error follows by differentiating \eqref{spf:bc:eq:logderivative}, or directly
from the Gamma and Laplace representations:
$\bcA''(x)=\bcA(x)\bigl((\log x)^2+\bigO((\log x)/x)\bigr)$.
The error is therefore $\bigO(\delta_n^2/\log n)$.
The flatness and rounding conclusions follow from \eqref{spf:bc:eq:flat}; inverse
injectivity and \cref{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero} give the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The familiar explicit Lambert coordinate}

For completeness, let
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda=\log y+1-\tfrac12\log(2\pi),\qquad
 N=\frac{\Lambda}{W_0(\Lambda/e)},\qquad \Delta=\log N.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Lambert-coordinate}
\end{equation}
The branch $W_0$ is real and positive for the large arguments used here.
Then $N(\log N-1)=\Lambda$ and
\begin{equation}
 \bcN(y)=N-\frac12-\frac{71}{24N\Delta}+\frac1{N^2\Delta}
                      +\bigO\!\left(\frac1{N^3\Delta}\right).
 \label{spf:bc:eq:Lambert-inverse}
\end{equation}
This is the specialization already available in \cite{bcProveIt}.
[Note of the batch-97 write: it is \cref{spf:rc:prop:inverse} at $(2,2)$, where
$1/24-(\sigma-1)=-71/24$ and $(r-1)(s-1)=1$, and Part~II's
\cref{fga:prop:inverse} and Part~I's \cref{spf:thm:inverse-first} in other
coordinates; the coefficients below, $37/12=c_1+\frac1{12}$ and
$-5/2=c_2-\frac12c_1^2$, are Part~III's $a_1$ and $a_2$.] To verify the
coefficients, combine \eqref{spf:bc:eq:first-c} with Stirling's formula to obtain
\[
 \log\bcA(x)+1-\tfrac12\log(2\pi)
 =x(\log x-1)+\tfrac12\log x+\frac{37}{12x}-\frac5{2x^2}
                                      +\bigO(x^{-3}).
\]
Insert $x=N-1/2+u/N+v/N^2$. The coefficients at orders $N^{-1}$ and
$N^{-2}$ vanish for $u=-71/(24\Delta)$ and $v=1/\Delta$. Higher terms follow recursively.
The new information in \cref{spf:bc:prop:inverse} is not these algebraic coefficients
but the propagation law for the unresolved flat correction.

\section{Exact checks and numerical diagnostics}
\label{spf:bc:sec:verification}

\subsection{Three independent exact constructions}

The supplied code keeps exact arithmetic separate from quadrature.
\path{code/core.py} (shipped as \path{code/05-borel-completion-core.py})
generates $\psi_i$ from \eqref{spf:bc:eq:psi-rec} and applies
the shifted Stirling transform. A separate construction expands
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:psi} directly by generalized binomial coefficients. A third
construction produces the derivative polynomials of $g_v$ from
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:green-ode}, integrates them using $v^j\mapsto j!$, and checks
\eqref{spf:bc:eq:H}. These independent routes catch coefficient, sign, and
normalization errors that a single recurrence implementation would not.

The integer counts are computed independently by matching tilings of two
paths, with no use of the asymptotic coefficients or any guessed recurrence.
For a nondecreasing tuple of tile sizes $\varrho$, let $m_j(\varrho)$ be the
multiplicity of size $j$, and let $\Theta_n(\varrho)$ count tilings with that combined
profile on paths of lengths $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ and $\lceil n/2\rceil$.
Then the exact formula is
\begin{equation}
 a_n=\sum_{\varrho\vdash n}(-1)^{n-\ell(\varrho)}
             \Theta_n(\varrho)^2\prod_{j\ge1}m_j(\varrho)!.
 \label{spf:bc:eq:tilings}
\end{equation}
For clarity, a proof is included in \cref{spf:bc:app:tilings}. This is the known
matching-of-tilings mechanism of \cite{spahn,bcProveIt}, not a new enumeration
formula. [Note of the batch-97 write: it is Part~I's
Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:exact} at $u=-1$ for the two paths of each forest,
with tiles counted by vertices.] Our partition-based implementation is intended for independent
finite validation, not as an asymptotically fast counting algorithm.

\begin{center}
\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.73\linewidth}r}
\toprule
Exact check & Number\\
\midrule
Auxiliary coefficients from the recurrence and direct formal expansion & 401\\
Dawson--Green--Volterra Taylor derivative identities & 399\\
Coefficient identities in the third-order equation for $H$ & 396\\
Displayed OEIS asymptotic coefficients reproduced & 11\\
OEIS integer values checked against independent tilings, $n=0,\ldots,21$ & 22\\
Permutations exhaustively tested, sizes $0,\ldots,8$ & 46,234\\
Full cleared polynomial certificate \eqref{spf:bc:eq:certificate} & 1\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The program additionally generates exact $a_n$ through $n=30$ and both
coefficient arrays through order 400. Finite checks do not prove theorems for
all orders; those proofs are in the preceding sections. The exact count
checks and the analytic statements are not certified by a proof assistant.

The following values use the single quadrature \eqref{spf:bc:eq:quadrature}, at
65-decimal working precision. They are high-precision diagnostics, not
outward-rounded interval enclosures. The sign and displayed digits in a row
are numerical observations, not a substitute for \cref{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero}.

\begin{remark}[Two corrections to the table above]
\label[remark]{spf:bc:rem:corrections}
[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97; the computations are the write's.] Two
statements about the table are wrong as printed; the table and the
delivered files are kept as delivered.

(a) \emph{The $M$ column at $n=8$.} The rule \eqref{spf:bc:eq:truncation-rule}
gives $M=\lfloor8(\frac12\log8-1)\rfloor=\lfloor0.3178\ldots\rfloor=0$, not
$1$. The program that produced the column, shipped as
\path{code/05-borel-completion-verify.py}, computes
\texttt{max(1, int(n*(log(n)/2-1)))}: it clamps the order at $1$ and truncates
towards zero, so it also records $M=1$ at $n=4$, where the rule gives
$\lfloor-1.227\ldots\rfloor=-2$, in
\path{data/05-borel-completion-verification.txt} and
\path{data/05-borel-completion-numerical_diagnostics.csv}, whose column
\texttt{C\_star\_minus\_partial} at $n=4$ and $n=8$ uses that clamped
order. The other rows agree with the rule ($12\cdot0.2425=2.91$,
$16\cdot0.3863=6.18$, $20\cdot0.4979=9.96$, $24\cdot0.5890=14.14$,
$30\cdot0.7006=21.02$). No theorem is affected: \cref{spf:bc:thm:truncation}
concerns large $n$ only.

(b) \emph{The signs of $\delta_n$.} \Cref{spf:bc:sec:flat} says that these
diagnostics ``suggest a sign change within the tested range''. The rows
printed above show one change, between $n=24$ and $n=30$, but they sample only
$n\equiv0\pmod4$. Over the whole range the signs follow the period-four
pattern $(-,-,+,+)$, beginning at $n=2$: $\delta_n<0$ for $n\equiv2,3$ and
$\delta_n>0$ for $n\equiv0,1\pmod4$, for every $2\le n\le30$. In particular
$\delta_6,\delta_7<0<\delta_8$ and $\delta_{30}<0$, and there are fourteen
sign changes, not one:

The write computed these values from the quadrature identity

\path{data/05-borel-completion-exact_values.json}, which equal the b-file

checks support them: the computed $\bcC(n)$ satisfy the shift equation

agree with the table above to all printed digits. They are floating-point

\subsection{Reproduction}

From the extracted archive root, run [note of the batch-97 write: in the
delivered layout only; see the end of this paragraph]
\begin{verbatim}
python code/verify.py --order 400
python code/factor_certificate.py
python -m pip install -r requirements-optional.txt
python code/verify.py --order 400 --numerical --dps 65
./build.sh
\end{verbatim}
The first two commands use only the Python standard library. Numerical
functions require \texttt{mpmath}; they are not used by the exact checks.
All paths are anchored at the scripts, and every output is written into
\path{data/}. Running the verification intentionally regenerates the
corresponding output files. The build script is anchored at the archive root
and does not depend on the calling working directory.

\paragraph{The shipped files of manuscript~06.} [Added 4 October 2026,
batch 97.] Manuscript~06's article, PDF, README and checksum ledger are not
shipped (the article is this Part; the ledger was verified at placement,
15/15). Its other twelve files are shipped byte-identical with the prefix
\path{05-borel-completion-}: \texttt{build.sh} and the three scripts in
\texttt{code/}, the six recorded outputs and \texttt{requirements-optional.txt}
(the line \texttt{mpmath==1.3.0}) in \texttt{data/}, and \texttt{SOURCES.md}
at the report root; \texttt{numerical\_diagnostics.csv} is CRLF as delivered.
\texttt{verify.py} imports its sibling by the delivered name
(\texttt{from core import \ldots}), both scripts write files under their
delivered names into the \texttt{data/} directory beside their own, and
\texttt{build.sh} builds an \texttt{article.tex} in its own directory, which
in \texttt{code/} does not exist. The commands above therefore work only in a
copy that restores the delivered layout; the report README gives that
recipe, which the batch-97 write ran (all checks passed, outputs equal to the
shipped ones up to line endings).

\section{Further questions and research}
\label{spf:bc:sec:questions}

[Added 4 October 2026, batch 97.] This section keeps manuscript~06's ten
questions, under its title ``Further research questions and concrete next
targets''. Together with the two corrections of
Remark~\ref{spf:bc:rem:corrections} they are every unproved or refuted claim
of manuscript~06 that this write found; its heuristic remark on complex saddle
pairs (question~3) is one of them. Notes of the write after the list say
which earlier questions of this report each continues, and record evidence;
evidence is not proof.

The results narrow the missing information rather than end the problem.
The following questions are ordered roughly from the most direct continuation
to wider applications. None is represented as solved in this manuscript.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Q\arabic*.},leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The leading flat term of the exact count.}
Find an asymptotic equivalent, or a finite oscillatory sum of equivalents,
for $\delta_n$. Determine whether its scale is
$\exp[-A_1n\log n+A_2n+o(n)]$, whether parity matters, and whether the
observed sign change reflects a systematic oscillation. A starting point is
the boundary correction between stable moments and the finite path tilings.

\item \textbf{A proof of the counting recurrence.}
Prove or disprove $(VUTa)(n)=0$ by an exact finite-tiling or dynamic-programming
identity. Since $VUT\bcA=0$ is now proved, this is equivalent, after the
Gamma normalization, to an exact recurrence problem for the flat sector.
The equivalence does not reduce to matching finitely many asymptotic terms.

\item \textbf{A sharper large-order formula.}
Strengthen \eqref{spf:bc:eq:rootgrowth} to a saddle-point description of $c_J$,
including phases, cancellations, zeros, and sign blocks. The explicit kernel
suggests complex saddle pairs of the exponential composition rather than a
single positive real saddle. [Note of the batch-97 write: this
suggestion is heuristic; no saddle-point analysis is given.]

\item \textbf{Optimal truncation and its prefactor.}
Determine the true least-term index and the leading remainder for $\bcC$.
The proven rule $M\sim\tfrac12n\log n$ has the right natural scale suggested
by the coefficient envelope, but the theorem does not prove optimality,
a limiting prefactor, or a sharp lower bound.

\item \textbf{An exponential error bound for the actual sequence.}
Obtain effective uniform bounds on the path-forest stabilization error strong
enough to compare $\delta_n$ with the canonical truncation error. This would
turn a theorem about an analytic completion into a controlled exponentially
improved approximation of the integer count.

\item \textbf{Exact recovery at individual indices.}
Can $\delta_n$ vanish at even one integer $n\ge2$? The arithmetic argument
only excludes four consecutive zeros. Investigate shorter zero blocks,
quantitative separation from zero, and whether irrationality estimates for
$e$ can give useful lower bounds for combinations of discrepancies.

\item \textbf{Other directed offsets.}
For fixed $r,s\ge2$, the earlier kernel is
$(1+z)^{r+s-1}{}_2F_0(r-1,s-1;;-z^2/(1+z))$.
Use Gamma-integral weights or differential operators in the kernel parameter
to seek analogues of \eqref{spf:bc:eq:H}. Determine whether the order-two inner
kernel, strip threshold $\pi/4$, and logarithmic root constant two are universal.
The degenerate one-path cases must be separated, since their earlier kernels
terminate.

\item \textbf{The absolute-difference model A110128.}
Construct an equally explicit Borel representation when
$|\pi(i+2)-\pi(i)|=2$ is forbidden. The directed rational collapse does not
apply automatically: each nontrivial selected component has two possible
orientations, altering the moment weights.

\item \textbf{Summation geometry at infinity.}
Give a precise acceleration or strip-based summation theory adapted to
$H(e^t-1)$. Distinguish growth boundaries from genuine singularity-driven
Stokes jumps, and specify exactly which analytic continuations and contour
changes remain admissible. The fact that $\widehat C$ is entire does not remove
all summation geometry.

\item \textbf{Formal verification.}
A manageable first target is a Lean proof of the finite coefficient
identities, the shift-operator polynomial certificate, and the arithmetic
four-index obstruction. A later analytic development would need entire
function growth, Dawson asymptotics, and uniform Laplace estimates. The
present Python assertions are not a substitute for that formalization.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Notes of the batch-97 write on these questions.}
Question~1 continues Part~I's question~3 and Part~III's question~2 for
A189281: \cref{spf:bc:thm:flat-nonzero} is the first proof in this report that
the count has a nonzero beyond-all-orders correction, relative to $\bcC$; its
amplitude, scale and sign are open. The values of
Remark~\ref{spf:bc:rem:corrections} bear on the parity question: for
$2\le n\le30$ the sign has period four, $(-,-,+,+)$ from $n=2$, and
$|\delta_n|$ decreases faster than every power of $n$, consistent with a
scale $\exp(-A_1n\log n+A_2n)$ but not a proof of it. Question~2 continues
Part~I's question~2 and Part~III's question~1: \cref{spf:bc:thm:operator}
reduces the recurrence of the counts to a recurrence for $\delta_n$.
Questions~3 and~4 continue Part~III's questions~3 and~4 and Part~I's
question~4, which Part~V answers in part (the dated notes there say how).
Question~5 is what Part~I's question~4 still asks for the counts themselves.
For question~6, no $\delta_n$ with $2\le n\le30$ vanishes numerically; the
smallest is $|\delta_{30}|\approx1.5\cdot10^{-20}$, at 50-digit precision.
Question~7 extends Part~III's questions~3 and~4 to every directed pair of
offsets, and question~8 continues Part~III's question~7 (absolute
differences); question~10 continues Part~I's question~7 and Part~III's
question~6. Nothing in Part~V is formalized.

\section{Conclusion of manuscript 06}

The explicit kernel resolves the summation question for the inspected A189281
algebraic sector, but with an important qualification: positive-ray summation
works whereas angular-sector 1-summability fails. It also supplies a sharp
large-order growth scale and proves that the correction coefficients are not
P-recursive. Those conclusions require the global analytic structure of the
Borel transform; they could not be inferred safely from its entireness alone.

The same representation defines a positive analytic completion, yields a
meromorphic continuation and exact shift equations, and gives a justified
superexponential truncation rule. Its compatibility with the conjectured OEIS
operator is exact, yet does not identify it with the integer count. The
irrational forcing in the rightmost factor proves that beyond-all-orders
information is genuinely present in the original combinatorial problem.
A full transseries for the count must recover that information rather than
silently equate a preferred sum with the sequence.

\section{Independent exact enumeration by path tilings (manuscript 06)}
\label{spf:bc:app:tilings}

The directed position graph has edges $i\to i+2$ and is the disjoint union
of its odd and even vertex paths. The directed value graph has the same form.
Inclusion--exclusion over selected forbidden events chooses a subset of the
position edges. Its connected components, including isolated vertices, form
a tiling of the two position paths by directed intervals.

If a selected component has $j$ vertices, its image under a permissible
bijection must be a directed interval of $j$ vertices in the value graph.
Because the map is injective, the images form a tiling of the value paths
with the same multiset of interval sizes. Conversely, any two such tilings,
with a matching of equal-sized intervals, define exactly one permutation
satisfying all the selected events: orientation is forced by the positive
signed difference. For profile $\varrho$, the matching count is
$\prod_jm_j(\varrho)!$. The number of selected edges is
$n-\ell(\varrho)$, hence the sign in \eqref{spf:bc:eq:tilings}.

For a single path of length $L$, a profile with $k$ tiles and multiplicities
$m_j$ has $k!/\prod_jm_j!$ ordered tilings. Convolving these profile counts
for the two paths gives $\Theta_n(\varrho)$. This proves \eqref{spf:bc:eq:tilings},
including empty paths and $n=0$. The implementation enumerates integer
partitions, not all permutations, while the separate brute-force check for
$n\le8$ tests the original forbidden-difference condition directly.
[Note of the batch-97 write: this is a second proof of the case $u=-1$,
$(r,s)=(2,2)$, $\theta=1$ of Part~I's Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:exact},
the matching-of-tilings formula of Spahn and Zeilberger.]

\section{Coefficient and certificate conventions of manuscript 06}
\label{spf:bc:app:conventions}

All coefficient arrays in \path{data/coefficients.json} (shipped as
\path{data/05-borel-completion-}\allowbreak\path{coefficients.json}) start at index zero.
The ordinary series $\Psi(z)$ is formal; the exponential generating function
$\mathcal E(x)$ and Borel series $\widehat C(t)$ are entire. The transform convention is
\[
 \frac{c_J}{n^J}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
 \frac{c_J}{(J-1)!}t^{J-1}\quad(J\ge1),
\]
with the constant coefficient kept outside the integral. Losing the shift
by one in this convention changes both the kernel and the Laplace boundary
terms.

Polynomial arrays in \path{code/factor_certificate.py} are stored in
ascending degree order. That program multiplies, shifts, and adds integer
polynomials exactly. Its certificate concerns the known analytic forcing
$\Gamma(n)P(n)/(4e)$; the integer counts never enter it. Conversely, the
exact enumeration in \path{code/core.py} never uses the analytic forcing.
This separation is deliberate.

The numerical status file explicitly records
\texttt{"interval\_certified": false}. No theorem in this manuscript depends
on rounding a floating-point residual to zero. The numerical table is useful
for finding transcription errors and for suggesting further questions, not
for certifying a Stokes multiplier, a zero of $\delta_n$, or an onset for
nearest-integer inversion.

\part{Fixed-displacement permutations: a third derivation and its additions}
\label{spf:fd:part}

\section{Provenance, scope and notation of Part VI}
\label{spf:fd:sec:front}

[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101.] Part~VI was added in the write of
batch~101. It is built from Report~83 of the session bundle and, on the
precedent of Part~II, prints only what that report adds to Parts~I--V.
Report~83 proves, for the oriented model $\theta=1$ at every fixed pair of
offsets, Part~I's all-orders expansion of the avoidance probability, the
stabilization of long path forests, a path-length comparison bound and a
threshold inverse. Every one of these theorems is already in Parts~I
and~II, in most cases in a stronger form; they are recorded as second (in
places third or fourth) routes in \cref{spf:fd:tab:routes} and not
reprinted. What is new is printed: three exact Bonferroni enclosures of
the A189281 probability at $n=100$, $200$ and $1000$
(\cref{spf:fd:sec:bonferroni}); the two-sided ceiling bracket for the
threshold, stated explicitly, and the smooth-model inverse in the exact
digamma coordinate (\cref{spf:fd:sec:inverse}); and four literature items
that the rest of the report does not cite (\cref{spf:fd:sec:literature}).

\subsection{Provenance}
\label{spf:fd:sec:provenance}

Report~83 is the archive
\nolinkurl{Fixed_Displacement_Permutation_Asymptotics_Source.zip} of the
session bundle of Reports 1--243, which arrived in commit
\texttt{60f54ea06}; batch~101 placed it in this report in commit
\texttt{f7c612c72}. It holds 22 files in one directory with a subdirectory
\texttt{independent\_review/}, one of them the checksum manifest
\texttt{manifest.json}, which lists the other 21 (all matched at the
intake). Its article is titled \emph{Complete Asymptotic Expansions for
Fixed-Displacement Permutations} (7~pages, dated 1~October 2026); its
author line and its PDF metadata both read ``Research note prepared for
Vladimir Reshetnikov with OpenAI''. Its pinned repository commit is
\texttt{1512ef8356d10fef99e7caaa2753620f0c900c83} (2026-10-01,
08:38~$-$07:00;~\cite{fdProveIt}), at which it inspected the transseries
companion \path{Combinatorial_Transseries_Inverses} for its gamma-core and
reversion tools.

\paragraph{An independent same-day derivation.}
The pin predates every source of this report. Manuscript~60 (Part~I)
arrived in commit \texttt{aa43cc555} at 13:54 on 1~October 2026,
manuscript~03 of batch~74 (Part~II) in \texttt{c664fc2f0} at 19:03, and
Part~I was written in \texttt{2f13bec21} at 19:56; none of these commits,
nor the placement \texttt{6e193dd4f}, is an ancestor of the pin. The
archive of Report~83 was created at 16:33~UTC, before manuscript~60 even
arrived. Report~83 is therefore a third derivation of Part~I's main
theorem, written on the same day as the first two and independently of
both; its text shares 76 of 3{,}425 word 8-grams (2.2\,\%) with this
article, all of them bibliography strings, coefficient digits and the
macro text of falling and rising factorials.

\paragraph{Scope and non-claims of Report~83, kept.}
It does not prove the guessed order-eight, degree-eleven recurrence of
A189281 and uses no guessed recurrence: the work ``neither settles nor
relies on'' it. The expansion is asymptotic, with ``no assertion of
convergence''. It does not identify every flat or exponentially small
sector, nor Stokes data, and its comparison theorem ``does not select a
preferred analytic interpolation''. Its exact Bonferroni intervals are
stored first; their decimal summaries are ``regression evidence, not the
remainder proof''. It does not need or audit the global closure argument of
Padhi's holonomicity note, which by its own README is not submitted or
externally reviewed and contains no asymptotic expansion. Tauraso's and
Mendelsohn's work are ``established antecedent methods, not new principles
here''; Mendelsohn ``assumes the complete expansion exists''; and the
bounded literature search ``is not a claim that no such source exists''.
The proofs ``have been checked by direct derivation and exact
computations'' and ``have not been externally refereed or formally
verified''; its plain-text \path{06-fixed-disp-proof.md} is marked
``awaiting independent review''. The gamma-core and reversion tools are the
repository companion's, ``reused with credit''.

\paragraph{Stale statements of Report~83.}
[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101.] Report~83 states that a bounded search
``located no source containing the complete directed-family expansion,
quantitative path-length comparison, or threshold inverse stated here'',
and its \path{06-fixed-disp-source_status.md} that the ProveIt companion
``does not name A189281 or the fixed-displacement permutation family''.
Both were true at its pin. They are not true of the repository today: all
three results are in Part~I of this report (\cref{spf:thm:main},
\cref{spf:thm:universality} and the last subsection of
\cref{spf:sec:inverse}), placed in the evening of the same day. The
delivered texts are kept; this note corrects them.

\subsection{What Part VI adds}
\label{spf:fd:sec:claims}

\Cref{spf:fd:tab:routes} lists every result of Report~83 against the place
where Parts~I--V already prove it. Report~83's coefficients of A189281
through $n^{-10}$, $1,3,2,1,0,3,26,101,124,-1409,-13266$, are Part~I's
\eqref{spf:eq:A189281} and are not new; Part~I's \cref{spf:tab:extra} goes to
order~16 and Part~III to order~100. Report~83's closed forms of the first
three coefficients for general $(r,s)$,
\[
 c_1^{(1)}=r+s-1,\qquad c_2^{(1)}=\frac{r^2-r+s^2-s}{2},\qquad
 c_3^{(1)}=\frac{(r+s-1)(r^2-4rs+4r+s^2+4s-6)}{6},
\]
are Part~II's (\cref{fga:prop:c3}) and Part~III's
(\cref{spf:rc:sec:integrality}). The new material is:
\begin{itemize}
\item three exact rational Bonferroni enclosures of the A189281 avoidance
 probability, at $n=100$, $200$ and $1000$, the largest index certified
 anywhere in this report (\cref{spf:fd:tab:bonferroni});
\item the two-sided ceiling bracket for the threshold
 $N(y)=\min\{n:A^{(1)}_{r,s}(n)\ge y\}$ stated as a theorem
 (\cref{spf:fd:thm:bracket}); Part~I describes the mechanism in words and
 names \texttt{p0:thm:staircase}, of which the bracket is an instance;
\item the smooth-model inverse of A189281 in the exact digamma coordinate,
 through relative order $X^{-3}$ (\cref{spf:fd:prop:digamma});
\item Mendelsohn's 1956 paper, Teguia Tabuguia's 2025 guessing paper, Padhi's
 holonomicity note in its repository form, and the August 2026 update of
 the Spahn--Zeilberger author page (\cref{spf:fd:sec:literature});
\item a third coefficient checker, by Newton forward differences, written
 independently of both Report~83's own generator and Part~I's
 (\cref{spf:fd:sec:files}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The comparison theorem is weaker, not wrong.}
Report~83's Theorem~5.1 takes $J=\lfloor\gamma n\rfloor$ Bonferroni terms,
which agree for the two forest pairs by stabilization when every path has at
least $2J\le\delta n$ vertices, and bounds each truncation error by its
Lemma~2.2 at $J+1\le n/4$. The ranges are consistent and the proof is
complete as far as the intake checked it. Part~I's
\cref{spf:thm:universality} proves more: a total-variation bound for the
whole law, with a larger range of exponents.

\section{Exact Bonferroni enclosures at \texorpdfstring{$n=100$, $200$ and $1000$}{n = 100, 200 and 1000}}
\label{spf:fd:sec:bonferroni}

Report~83's \path{code/06-fixed-disp-check_bonferroni.py} evaluates the
alternating partial sums $S_{J_{\mathrm B}}$ and $S_{J_{\mathrm B}+1}$ of
\eqref{spf:eq:bonferroni} for A189281 exactly, from the stable profile
counts \eqref{spf:eq:F}. It refuses to run outside the stable range: it
requires $\lfloor n/2\rfloor\ge2(J_{\mathrm B}+1)$, which is more than
\cref{spf:thm:stable} needs. Its output
\path{data/06-fixed-disp-check_bonferroni.json} stores both endpoints as
exact rationals. By the pointwise Bonferroni inequalities recalled after
\eqref{spf:eq:bonferroni}, the smaller and the larger of the two sums
enclose $\Pp(X=0)=\Aone(n)/n!$.

\Cref{spf:fd:tab:bonferroni} prints these enclosures in Part~I's
normalization \eqref{spf:eq:scaled-error}, at order $M=10$. The write
recomputed the table from the shipped rationals with $e$ bracketed by
rational Taylor sums, so every endpoint is rounded outward; the decimals
stored in the JSON file, computed with 70-digit decimal arithmetic, lie
inside these intervals.
