\paragraph{Checks against Parts~I and~III.}
[Note of the batch-101 write.] The intervals are consistent with the
coefficients printed elsewhere in the report, which they did not use. The
truncation $\sum_{j\le40}c_jn^{-j}$ of Part~III's coefficients
(\path{data/03-rational-collapse-coefficients.json}) lies inside all three
enclosures. The truncation at order~16 of Part~I's \cref{spf:tab:extra} lies
inside at $n=1000$ and just outside at $n=100$ and $n=200$, as the omitted
term of order $n^{-17}$ allows. At $n=1000$ the $E_{10}$ interval, of width
$9.35$, contains $c_{11}+c_{12}/n=-59103-9.448$, an independent check of
Part~I's $c_{11}$ and $c_{12}$, beyond the order through which Report~83
itself computed coefficients. As in Part~I, the widening of the $E_{10}$
interval at $n=1000$ is the factor $n^{11}$ magnifying the fixed Bonferroni
width; it is a precision limit of this finite computation, and the
convergence of $E_{10}(n)$ to $c_{11}$ is a consequence of
\cref{spf:thm:main}, not of three samples.

\section{The threshold inverse: a ceiling bracket and the exact-digamma display}
\label{spf:fd:sec:inverse}

Fix $r,s\ge1$ and $K\ge0$, put $\theta=1$, and write $A(n)=A^{(1)}_{r,s}(n)$,
$c_j=c_j^{(1)}(r,s)$ and
\[
 \mathcal A_K(x)=e^{-1}\Gamma(x+1)\sum_{j=0}^{K}c_jx^{-j},
\]
which is Part~I's smooth model of the last subsection of
\cref{spf:sec:inverse} with $M=K$. It is positive and strictly increasing
on a half-line $[x_*,\infty)$; let $x_K=\mathcal A_K^{-1}$ there, and let
$N(y)=\min\{n\ge0:A(n)\ge y\}$.

\begin{theorem}[Threshold bracket; Report~83, Theorem~6.1]
\label[theorem]{spf:fd:thm:bracket}
There are constants $C=C_{r,s,K}$ and $y_0$ such that for every $y\ge y_0$
\begin{equation}
 \left\lceil x_K(y)-\varepsilon_K(y)\right\rceil\le N(y)\le
 \left\lceil x_K(y)+\varepsilon_K(y)\right\rceil,\qquad
 \varepsilon_K(y)=\frac{C\,x_K(y)^{-K-1}}{\log x_K(y)}.
 \label{spf:fd:eq:bracket}
\end{equation}
In particular $N(y)=\lceil x_K(y)\rceil$ whenever the distance of $x_K(y)$
from the integers exceeds $\varepsilon_K(y)$.
\end{theorem}

This is the staircase theorem \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} of the transseries
volume~\cite{tai} applied to $\mathcal A_K$, as Part~I's remark at the end of
\cref{spf:sec:inverse} already says in words; neither the constant $C$ nor
the onset $y_0$ is explicit. Report~83 proves it in four sentences. The
following proof spells out the two endpoint comparisons.

\begin{proof}[Proof (completed by the batch-101 write)]
Put $g=\log\mathcal A_K$, so $g(x)=\log\Gamma(x+1)-1+\ell_K(x)$ with
$\ell_K(x)=\log\sum_{j\le K}c_jx^{-j}$, a convergent power series in $1/x$
for large $x$ with $\ell_K(x)=\bigO(1/x)$ and $\ell_K'(x)=\bigO(x^{-2})$.
Since $\psi(x+1)=\log x+\bigO(1/x)$, there is $x_1\ge\max(x_*,4)$ with
$g'(x)\ge\frac12\log x$ for $x\ge x_1$. By \cref{spf:thm:main} at $u=-1$ and
$\theta=1$ there are $C_1$ and $n_1\ge x_1$ such that
$\eta_n=\log A(n)-g(n)$ satisfies $|\eta_n|\le C_1n^{-K-1}$ for $n\ge n_1$.
Moreover $A(n+1)/A(n)=(n+1)\,\Pp_{n+1}(X=0)/\Pp_n(X=0)\to\infty$, so there
is $n_2\ge n_1$ with $A$ strictly increasing on $[n_2,\infty)$. We may take
$C_1>0$. Choose $C=2^{K+4}C_1$, and choose $y_0$ so large that for
$y\ge y_0$ the point $x=x_K(y)$ satisfies $x\ge 2n_2+2$ and
$\varepsilon_K(y)\le1$, and $y>\max_{n<n_2}A(n)$.

\emph{Upper bound.} Let $m=\lceil x+\varepsilon_K\rceil$, so
$m\ge x+\varepsilon_K$ and $x\le m\le x+2\le 2x$. By the mean value theorem
and monotonicity of $\log$,
$g(m)-g(x)\ge\frac12\log x\cdot(m-x)\ge\frac12C\,x^{-K-1}$. Hence, since
$m\ge x$ gives $m^{-K-1}\le x^{-K-1}$,
\[
 \log A(m)-\log y=g(m)-g(x)+\eta_m\ge\bigl(\tfrac12C-C_1\bigr)x^{-K-1}>0 .
\]
So $A(m)\ge y$ and $N(y)\le m$.

\emph{Lower bound.} Let $n$ be an integer with
$n<\lceil x-\varepsilon_K\rceil$, that is, $n<x-\varepsilon_K$. If $n<n_2$
then $A(n)<y$ by the choice of $y_0$. Otherwise let $n'$ be the largest
integer with $n'<x-\varepsilon_K$; then $n_2\le n\le n'$, $x-n'>\varepsilon_K$
and $n'\ge x-2\ge x/2$. For $\xi\in(n',x)$ we have
$g'(\xi)\ge\frac12\log n'\ge\frac12\log(x/2)\ge\frac14\log x$, because
$x\ge4$. Hence $g(x)-g(n')>\frac14\log x\cdot\varepsilon_K=\frac14C\,x^{-K-1}
=2^{K+2}C_1x^{-K-1}$, while $\eta_{n'}\le C_1(x/2)^{-K-1}=2^{K+1}C_1x^{-K-1}$.
Therefore
\[
 \log A(n')-\log y=\eta_{n'}-\bigl(g(x)-g(n')\bigr)
 <\bigl(2^{K+1}-2^{K+2}\bigr)C_1x^{-K-1}<0,
\]
and by monotonicity on $[n_2,\infty)$, $A(n)\le A(n')<y$. Thus every
$n<\lceil x-\varepsilon_K\rceil$ has $A(n)<y$, and
$N(y)\ge\lceil x-\varepsilon_K\rceil$.

The last assertion holds because, when $x$ is farther than $\varepsilon_K$
from the integers, both ceilings in \eqref{spf:fd:eq:bracket} equal
$\lceil x\rceil$.
\end{proof}

Report~83 also writes the model inverse as an all-orders formal Lagrange
reversion around the exact gamma core. Let $\mathfrak X=\mathfrak X(y)$ be
the root of
\begin{equation}
 \log\Gamma(\mathfrak X+1)-1=\log y,
 \label{spf:fd:eq:core}
\end{equation}
and put $\psi_{\mathfrak X}=\psi(\mathfrak X+1)$ and
$\psi'_{\mathfrak X}=\psi'(\mathfrak X+1)$. With $\ell(x)=\sum_{j\ge1}\ell_jx^{-j}$
the formal logarithm of the correction series (Part~I's
\eqref{spf:eq:log-recursion}), Report~83's (6.4) reads
\begin{equation}
 x(y)\sim\mathfrak X+\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}
 \left(\frac1{\psi_{\mathfrak X}}\frac{d}{d\mathfrak X}\right)^{k-1}
 \left(\frac{\ell(\mathfrak X)^k}{\psi_{\mathfrak X}}\right).
 \label{spf:fd:eq:reversion}
\end{equation}
Report~83 justifies only finite truncations of this series, by Taylor's
theorem for $\mathcal A_K$; the series itself is formal
(\cref{spf:fd:sec:questions}, question~2). Its first terms give a display
that Parts~I--V do not contain.

\begin{proposition}[A189281 inverse in the digamma coordinate; Report~83, (6.6)]
\label[proposition]{spf:fd:prop:digamma}
Let $(r,s)=(2,2)$, $K\ge3$, and $x=x_K(y)$. As $y\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 x=\mathfrak X-\frac3{\mathfrak X\psi_{\mathfrak X}}
 +\frac5{2\mathfrak X^2\psi_{\mathfrak X}}
 -\frac4{\mathfrak X^3\psi_{\mathfrak X}}
 -\frac9{\mathfrak X^3\psi_{\mathfrak X}^2}
 -\frac{9\psi'_{\mathfrak X}}{2\mathfrak X^2\psi_{\mathfrak X}^3}
 +\bigO\!\left(\frac{\mathfrak X^{-4}}{\log\mathfrak X}\right).
 \label{spf:fd:eq:digamma}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof (batch-101 write)]
Report~83 states the display with its derivation from
\eqref{spf:fd:eq:reversion}; here is the error bound. Write $G(x)=\log\Gamma(x+1)$ and $\ell_K$ as in the previous proof. For A189281,
$\ell_K(x)=3x^{-1}-\frac52x^{-2}+4x^{-3}+\bigO(x^{-4})$ when $K\ge3$
(Part~I's $\ell_1,\ell_2,\ell_3$; they depend only on $c_1,c_2,c_3$), and
since $\ell_K(x)=L(1/x)$ with $L$ analytic at $0$, also
$\ell_K'(x)=-3x^{-2}+\bigO(x^{-3})$ and $\ell_K''(x)=\bigO(x^{-3})$. On the
other side $G'(x)=\psi(x+1)=\log x+\bigO(1/x)$, $0<G''(x)=\psi'(x+1)\le1/x$
and $G'''(x)=\bigO(x^{-2})$. By \eqref{spf:fd:eq:core} the model equation
$\log\mathcal A_K(x)=\log y$ is $G(x)+\ell_K(x)=G(\mathfrak X)$. Put
$\delta=x-\mathfrak X$ and
\[
 \Phi(t)=G(\mathfrak X+t)-G(\mathfrak X)+\ell_K(\mathfrak X+t).
\]
For large $\mathfrak X$, $\Phi$ is increasing on $[-1,1]$ with
$\Phi'\ge\frac12\log\mathfrak X$ there, $0<\Phi(0)=\ell_K(\mathfrak X)\le4/\mathfrak X$
and $\Phi(-1)\le-\frac12\log\mathfrak X+\Phi(0)<0$; hence its root $\delta$
lies in $(-1,0)$ and, by the mean value theorem,
$|\delta|\le\Phi(0)/\inf\Phi'\le8/(\mathfrak X\log\mathfrak X)$.

Taylor's theorem to second order in $G$ and to first order in $\ell_K$ gives
\[
 0=\psi_{\mathfrak X}\delta+\tfrac12\psi'_{\mathfrak X}\delta^2
 +\ell_K(\mathfrak X)+\ell_K'(\mathfrak X)\delta+\rho,\qquad
 |\rho|\le\sup|G'''|\,\tfrac{|\delta|^3}6+\sup|\ell_K''|\,\tfrac{\delta^2}2
 =\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-5}),
\]
the suprema over $[\mathfrak X-1,\mathfrak X+1]$. Abbreviate $\ell=\ell_K(\mathfrak X)$,
$\ell'=\ell_K'(\mathfrak X)$. Dividing by $\psi_{\mathfrak X}\asymp\log\mathfrak X$,
\[
 \delta=-\frac{\ell}{\psi_{\mathfrak X}}-\frac{\ell'\delta}{\psi_{\mathfrak X}}
 -\frac{\psi'_{\mathfrak X}\delta^2}{2\psi_{\mathfrak X}}
 +\bigO\Bigl(\frac{\mathfrak X^{-5}}{\log\mathfrak X}\Bigr).
\]
The last three terms are $\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-3}/\log^2\mathfrak X)$, so
$\delta=-\ell/\psi_{\mathfrak X}+\epsilon$ with
$\epsilon=\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-3}/\log^2\mathfrak X)$. Substituting this on
the right,
\[
 -\frac{\ell'\delta}{\psi_{\mathfrak X}}=\frac{\ell\ell'}{\psi_{\mathfrak X}^2}
 +\bigO\Bigl(\frac{\mathfrak X^{-5}}{\log^3\mathfrak X}\Bigr),\qquad
 -\frac{\psi'_{\mathfrak X}\delta^2}{2\psi_{\mathfrak X}}
 =-\frac{\psi'_{\mathfrak X}\ell^2}{2\psi_{\mathfrak X}^3}
 +\bigO\Bigl(\frac{\mathfrak X^{-5}}{\log^4\mathfrak X}\Bigr),
\]
so that
$\delta=-\ell/\psi_{\mathfrak X}+\ell\ell'/\psi_{\mathfrak X}^2
-\psi'_{\mathfrak X}\ell^2/(2\psi_{\mathfrak X}^3)+\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-5}/\log\mathfrak X)$;
these are the terms $k=1,2$ of \eqref{spf:fd:eq:reversion}. Finally
$-\ell/\psi_{\mathfrak X}$ gives the first three correction terms of
\eqref{spf:fd:eq:digamma} with error $\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-4}/\log\mathfrak X)$;
$\ell\ell'=-9\mathfrak X^{-3}+\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-4})$ gives
$-9/(\mathfrak X^3\psi_{\mathfrak X}^2)$ with error
$\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-4}/\log^2\mathfrak X)$; and
$\ell^2=9\mathfrak X^{-2}+\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-3})$ with
$\psi'_{\mathfrak X}\le1/\mathfrak X$ gives
$-9\psi'_{\mathfrak X}/(2\mathfrak X^2\psi_{\mathfrak X}^3)$ with error
$\bigO(\mathfrak X^{-4}/\log^3\mathfrak X)$.
\end{proof}

The display concerns the smooth model. For the counts it combines with
\cref{spf:fd:thm:bracket}: if $\hat x(y)$ denotes the right side of
\eqref{spf:fd:eq:digamma} without its remainder, then, since
$x_K-\mathfrak X=\bigO(1/(\mathfrak X\log\mathfrak X))$ and $K\ge3$, there is
a constant $E$ with
$\lceil\hat x-E\mathfrak X^{-4}/\log\mathfrak X\rceil\le N(y)\le
\lceil\hat x+E\mathfrak X^{-4}/\log\mathfrak X\rceil$ for all large $y$; and
at the data $x_K(\Aone(n))=n+\bigO(n^{-K-1}/\log n)$. Since
$\psi'_{\mathfrak X}\sim1/\mathfrak X$, the last two displayed terms are both
of order $\mathfrak X^{-3}/\log^2\mathfrak X$ or smaller; keeping the
digamma function exact, rather than expanding it in $\log\mathfrak X$, is
what makes the display this short. Report~83 adds that the gamma core starts
at $\log(ey)/W(\log(ey)/e)$; that is its leading order only, the next
Stirling term being of order one (Part~I's $z$ of \eqref{spf:eq:Lambert} with
$\log y$ shifted by one). [Note of the batch-101 write: the intake evaluated
\eqref{spf:fd:eq:digamma} against the exact model root at 60 digits with
$K=10$: the error times $\mathfrak X^4\log\mathfrak X$ is $13.29$, $12.72$,
$12.14$, $11.60$, $11.14$, $10.75$ at $\mathfrak X=20$, $50$, $100$, $200$,
$400$, $800$, bounded as the proposition asserts. This is evidence, not part
of the proof.]

\section{Literature that the rest of the report does not cite}
\label{spf:fd:sec:literature}

Report~83's \path{06-fixed-disp-source_status.md} records a primary-source
check made on 1~October 2026. Four of its items are not cited elsewhere in
this report; they are Report~83's readings, and the intake did not open the
sources.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Mendelsohn (1956)}~\cite{fdMendelsohn} develops a calculus of
 Poisson and falling-factorial corrections for permutation problems under
 a constant-coefficient operator recurrence. By Report~83's reading of its
 printed page~240, the coefficient calculation \emph{assumes} that the
 complete asymptotic series exists; its examples are rencontres, m\'enages,
 rook-king successions and adjacent forbidden positions. It is an
 antecedent of the correction calculus, not a proof of the present
 remainder theorem. Part~VII cites it for the same reason.
\item \emph{Teguia Tabuguia (2025)}~\cite{fdTabuguia}, Section~5.2,
 re-obtains the A189281 recurrence by D-algebraic guessing and states it as
 Conjecture~5.1; it does not prove it.
\item \emph{Padhi's repository note}~\cite{fdPadhiNote}, \emph{Holonomicity
 of the restricted permutation counts $a_{r,s}$ and $b_{r,s}$}, August 2026
 (source blob \texttt{ec04817377b7531d19cbc0d9b0243242de682a95}), claims
 holonomicity for every fixed $(r,s)$ through a bounded-seam construction.
 It contains no asymptotic expansion and does not derive the guessed
 operator; its README says it is not submitted or externally reviewed.
 Part~I cites Padhi's arXiv preprint~\cite{padhi}, which the intake had
 confirmed only as a record; the note is its repository form.
\item \emph{The Spahn--Zeilberger author page}~\cite{fdSZpage} records, in
 an update of 14~August 2026, Padhi's proposed solution of the
 general-holonomicity challenge. The paper itself, cited by this report
 as~\cite{spahn}, appeared as Enumerative Combinatorics and
 Applications~3:2 (2023), S2R10.
\end{itemize}
Report~83 also reads Kauers and Koutschan~\cite{kauers} as calling the
order-eight recurrence conjectural (their Conjecture~10), and Tauraso as
proving the leading and first corrections of the absolute family; both are
cited in Part~I.

\section{Report 83's computations and shipped files}
\label{spf:fd:sec:files}

Report~83 ships five programs and seven recorded outputs, all prefixed
\path{06-fixed-disp-}; below, the prefix is omitted.
\begin{itemize}
\item \path{code/compute_coefficients.py} (SymPy) implements only the finite
 formula \eqref{spf:eq:F}, the expansion of logarithms of falling factorials
 and Touchard sums, and writes \path{data/coefficients_2_2_10.json} and
 \path{data/coefficients_2_2_5.json} (orders 10 and~5 at $(2,2)$).
\item \path{code/check_stable_tilings.py} (standard library) checks 180
 stable-profile identities by a path-composition dynamic program, 48
 brute-force permutation cases through $n=8$ and the first 13 values of
 A189281 (OEIS data, CC BY-SA~4.0), and writes
 \path{data/check_stable_tilings.json}.
\item \path{code/check_bonferroni.py} produces
 \path{data/check_bonferroni.json} and \cref{spf:fd:tab:bonferroni}.
\end{itemize}

The two programs of its \path{independent_review/} directory are a third
coefficient checker, independent of Report~83's generator and of Part~I's
\path{code/path_forests.py}: it expands normalized falling products as
truncated integer polynomials, inverts the denominator by a
complete-homogeneous recurrence, and sums the polynomial dependence on the
dimer count by Newton forward differences,
\[
 e\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^kP(k)}{k!}=\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(-1)^j\Delta^jP(0)}{j!},
\]
checking two interpolation points beyond the degree bound $2\ell$ at retained
order~$\ell$ rather than fitting them. Its first program
(\path{code/independent_review-check_coefficients.py}, prefix omitted as
above) reconstructs the A189281 coefficients through order six, 30
higher-defect profiles, 299 finite-difference checks, all 16 pairs
$1\le r,s\le4$ through order three and 25 profile counts by fresh edge-subset
enumeration; the second (\path{..._check_order_ten.py}) extends the
reconstruction to order ten (139 profiles, 423 checks). Their outputs are
\path{data/independent_review-check_coefficients.json},
\path{data/independent_review-order_ten.json} and the log
\path{..._order_ten_replay.log}, which holds both programs' output because the
second imports the first, whose module code reruns. The audit
\path{independent_review-AUDIT.md} at the report root reviews
\path{proof.md}, the plain-text version of the proofs.

All checks raise explicit exceptions, so Python's \texttt{-O} flag does not
disable them. Every program writes its output beside itself under its
delivered name and imports its sibling by that name; the report README gives
a recipe that restores the delivered layout in a scratch copy. The intake
ran the whole suite on such a copy in under three minutes: all outputs equal
the shipped ones after removing the carriage returns that Windows adds.

\begin{remark}[Text drift in Report~83, recorded]
\label[remark]{spf:fd:rem:drift}
[Batch-101 write.] Three sentences of the delivery are inaccurate, none of
them mathematically. Its article says that the independent checker reaches
order six; its audit says the reconstruction was ``subsequently extended''
to order ten, and the shipped \path{..._check_order_ten.py} does reach
ten, as its delivered README says. Its delivered README says that ``the
delivered PDF is the same manuscript under a descriptive filename'', but
the archive holds only \path{article.pdf}. And the gamma-core start
$\log(ey)/W(\log(ey)/e)$ is a leading order only (see after
\cref{spf:fd:prop:digamma}).
\end{remark}

\section{Further questions and research}
\label{spf:fd:sec:questions}

[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101.] Report~83 poses no research questions.
The following are its unproved or unchecked claims, moved here under the
intake's standing rule, with what is missing.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Explicit constants in the threshold bracket.}
 \Cref{spf:fd:thm:bracket} asserts the existence of $C_{r,s,K}$ and of an
 onset $y_0$; neither is computed. Explicit values need an effective
 remainder in \cref{spf:thm:main}. This is Part~III's open onset question for
 nearest-integer rounding and Part~I's question~8; Part~VII's effective
 Newton certificate (\cref{spf:cg:prop:effective}) covers its smooth model
 only.
\item \emph{The all-orders reversion.} \eqref{spf:fd:eq:reversion} is
 formal. Each finite truncation is justified, as in the proof of
 \cref{spf:fd:prop:digamma}, but no statement uniform in the number of terms
 is proved, and the series may diverge. Compare
 \texttt{p0:thm:perturbed-inversion} of~\cite{tai}.
\item \emph{Constants and sharpness of the flat comparison.} The constant
 $C$ of Report~83's Lemma~2.2 and the $\bigO_\gamma(n)$ of its Theorem~5.1
 are not explicit, and its range $\gamma<\min(\delta/2,1/4)$ is narrower
 than Part~I's. Whether a bound $\exp(-\gamma n\log n)$ is sharp in $\gamma$
 for some pair of forests is open; this is part of Part~I's question~3.
\item \emph{Reported, not verified.} Padhi's proposed general holonomicity
 proof and the August 2026 update of the Spahn--Zeilberger page are
 reported by Report~83 and were not checked by the intake.
\end{enumerate}
No claim of Report~83 was found to be wrong. Nothing in Part~VI is
formalized.

\part{Reciprocal coloured gaps: a joint expansion, the camel and knight counts, and threshold inverses}
\label{spf:cg:part}

\section{Provenance, scope and notation of Part VII}
\label{spf:cg:sec:front}

[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101.] Part~VII was added in the write of
batch~101. It is built from Report~232 of the session bundle, which treats
two forbidden gaps at once: for fixed $1\le r<s$, the violations of the
absolute gap $(r,s)$ (positions $r$ apart, values $s$ apart) and of the
reciprocal gap $(s,r)$, marked separately. It proves an all-orders expansion
of their joint marked generating function, uniform on compact sets of
$\mathbb C^2$, in which selected components may contain cycles; computes the
second correction in full for the camel and knight sequences A189358
and A137774; and inverts both counts. This answers, for reciprocal pairs, the
question of several forbidden gaps that the report asks four times: Part~I's
question~6 (\cref{spf:sec:questions}), Part~II's question~4
(\cref{fga:sec:questions}), Part~III's question~10
(\cref{spf:rc:sec:questions}) and Part~IV's \cref{spf:mg:q:several}; dated
notes there say what remains open. Each single-gap marginal of the joint
expansion is Part~I's absolute expansion ($\theta=2$), and the write checks
the printed coefficients against it (\cref{spf:cg:rem:marginals}). Report~232
is new throughout apart from those marginals, so it is printed nearly in
full, with its symbols renamed where they clash
(\cref{spf:cg:tab:dictionary}), its headings marked, and bracketed notes of
the write.

\subsection{Provenance}
\label{spf:cg:sec:provenance}

Report~232 is the archive \nolinkurl{Report232.zip} of the session bundle of
Reports 1--243, which arrived in commit \texttt{60f54ea06} (archive created
2026-10-05, 09:25~UTC); batch~101 placed it in this report in commit
\texttt{f7c612c72}, reversing the triage's proposal of a new report because
this report names the problem four times. The archive holds ten files under
\path{Report232/}, one of them the checksum manifest \path{SHA256SUMS}, which
lists the other nine (all matched at the intake). Its article,
\path{report.tex}, is titled \emph{Reciprocal coloured gap avoidance: Uniform
expansions and factorial growth inverses} (27~pages, dated 5~October 2026,
headed ``Report 232''); its author line reads ``A self-contained mathematical
and computational report'' and its PDF metadata names ``Research report'' as
author. It pins no repository commit and names no ProveIt path or file. One
sentence of its literature section (``treat the present family separately
from a one-source/one-target forest calculation'') echoes the non-claim of
Part~IV that its uniform theorem ``concerns one source forest and one target
forest, not unions of differently coloured gap constraints''; Part~IV was
written in commit \texttt{eea159818} about five hours before the archive was
made, so Report~232 probably saw it, but nothing records this. It does not
cite this report.

\paragraph{Scope and non-claims of Report~232, kept.}
The leading $e^{-4}$ limits of the two chess-piece counts and the general
methods (graph inclusion--exclusion, partition-lattice inversion, finite
transfer matrices, analytic perturbation, Poisson correction polynomials)
are classical and ``not claimed as inventions''; there is no worldwide
priority claim, and its source comparison is ``bounded'', ``not a proof that
no equivalent theorem exists elsewhere''; renaming a model or changing one
gap ``would not establish a new theorem''. ``Fixed order'' asserts neither
convergence of the $1/n$ series, nor uniformity for growing gaps, nor an
effective error bound at a stated small $n$. The polynomial-identity
thresholds ($n\ge12$ camel, $n\ge8$ knight) are not the onset of asymptotic
accuracy, and the camel counterexample at $n=9$ is kept on purpose. The
inverse constants and onset are not evaluated; $N(y)=\xi_K(y)+\smallo(1)$ is
not uniform in $y$; the effective Newton certificate for $\tilde z\ge32$
concerns the smooth model only; no exact rounding of a centre is asserted.
The core-edge lemma uses the reciprocal two-gap structure and ``should not be
silently extended'' to general unions of gaps. Third and higher coefficients
exist, but no value is claimed; the higher coefficients of its inverse
fixture are a method fixture, not coefficients of the sequences. Its
numerical tests are ``consistency checks, not a substitute for the analytic
remainder or an effective sequence onset''; it ships no third-party paper
and no private review material, used no network access, and recommends
external peer review for any publication claim.

\subsection{What Part VII adds}
\label{spf:cg:sec:claims}

\midrule

one gap $(r,s)$, one source and one target forest (Parts~I--IV) &

separately (\cref{spf:cg:sec:model})\\
Selected graphs &
unions of paths; profiles with a defect (\cref{spf:sec:tilings}) &
two-coloured graphs, cycles allowed; exact identity
(\cref{spf:cg:prop:identity}); compatible graphs triangle-free and
2-degenerate, with a \emph{deficit} (\cref{spf:cg:lem:coreedges})\\
All-orders law &
$e^{\theta u}\sum B_J(u)n^{-J}$ (\cref{spf:thm:main}) &
$e^{2u+2v}\sum\mathfrak B_j(u,v)n^{-j}$, uniform on compact sets of
$\mathbb C^2$, $\deg\mathfrak B_j\le2j$ (\cref{spf:cg:thm:main})\\
Cycles &

Lambert-$W$ inverse; ceiling bracket (\cref{spf:thm:inverse-all},

for the quadratic models and $N_{\mathrm{camel}}\le N_{\mathrm{knight}}\le
N_{\mathrm{camel}}+1$ (\cref{spf:cg:sec:coloured-inverses})\\

\paragraph{Source and novelty ledger of Report~232, restated.}
Report~232 credits the leading limits to Kot\v e\v sovec's
compilation~\cite{cgKotesovec}, higher-order Poisson corrections for
adjacency problems to Aspvall and Liang~\cite{cgAspvall}, the existence-assuming
correction calculus to Mendelsohn~\cite{fdMendelsohn}, the occupancy-mask
transfer construction and the Poisson limit for fixed forbidden exceedances to
Flajolet and Sedgewick~\cite{cgFS}, small-subgraph asymptotics for static
boards to Godsil and McKay~\cite{cgGodsil}, a one-direction generating
function for the knight objects to Bagno, Eisenberg, Reches and
Sigron~\cite{cgBagno}, and mod-Poisson methodology to M\'eliot, Nikeghbali
and Visentin~\cite{cgModPoisson}. It claims as its own the two-sided
reciprocal coloured finite-core expansion, its uniform factorial-scale
summation bound, and the explicit mixed and cycle corrections for the two
named sequences. It does not know this report; its marginal statements are
Part~I's.

\subsection{Notation}
\label{spf:cg:sec:notation}

Report~232 uses several letters for two or three objects, and several that
mean something else in Parts~I--VI. \Cref{spf:cg:tab:dictionary} lists every
renaming; no normalization is changed. The factorial power is written
$\fall nh=n(n-1)\cdots(n-h+1)$, as in the rest of the report (Report~232:
$(n)_h$).

\paragraph{Defect and deficit count different things.}
Part~I's \emph{defect} of a selected set is $d=k-c$, the number of selected
edges minus the number of components; for a forest it is $v-2c$, the number
of vertices minus twice the number of components. Report~232's
\emph{deficit} is $\delta(H)=v(H)-2c(H)$ by definition. The two agree on
forests and differ on graphs with cycles: the alternating square of
\cref{spf:cg:sec:square} has deficit $2$ but $e-c=3$. It is the deficit that
measures the order $n^{-\delta}$ at which a coloured graph enters, since each
component has one translational freedom on each side while every vertex costs
one power of $n$. The tempting false reading is that a selected graph with
$e$ edges and $c$ components enters at order $n^{-(e-c)}$, as a path forest
does; for the square that would be $n^{-3}$, and it enters at $n^{-2}$.

\section{The model and the conclusions (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:model}

\subsection{Two reciprocal constraints}
Fix integers $1\le r<s$. Write $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and let $\pi$ be uniform
on the permutations of $[n]$. Define
\begin{align}
 X_n&=\#\{i\le n-r:|\pi(i+r)-\pi(i)|=s\},\\
 Y_n&=\#\{i\le n-s:|\pi(i+s)-\pi(i)|=r\},\label{spf:cg:eq:model}\\
 G_n(u,v;r,s)&=\E\bigl[(1+u)^{X_n}(1+v)^{Y_n}\bigr].\label{spf:cg:eq:pgf}
\end{align}
Empty index sets contribute zero. A mark is the increment from $1$, so that
$u=v=0$ gives $G_n=1$ and $u=v=-1$ gives the avoidance probability. In
particular, the exponential factor below is $e^{2u+2v}$, not
$e^{2(u-1)+2(v-1)}$. [Note of the batch-101 write: in Part~I's notation
$X_n=X^{(2)}_{r,s,n}$ and $Y_n=X^{(2)}_{s,r,n}$, so $G_n(u,0;r,s)$ and
$G_n(0,v;r,s)$ are Part~I's $G^{(2)}_{r,s,n}(u)$ and $G^{(2)}_{s,r,n}(v)$.]

There are two coloured graphs on $[n]$. The source graph $S_n$ has colour $A$
for edges of length $r$ and colour $B$ for edges of length $s$. In the target
graph $T_n$, those lengths are reversed. A source edge is a violation exactly
when its image is an edge of the same colour in $T_n$. A selected subgraph
means a selected \emph{edge set}: other edges between its vertices are
irrelevant. This distinction matters for component counting and for the
packing construction.

Write $A^{\mathrm{cg}}_{r,s}(n)=n!\,G_n(-1,-1;r,s)$. For $r=1$, put $R=s$ and
abbreviate $\mathfrak B_j(u,v;1,R)$ by $\mathfrak B_{j,R}(u,v)$. A permutation
matrix already has one point in each row and column. Its points additionally
avoid camel attacks when $(r,s)=(1,3)$, and knight attacks when
$(r,s)=(1,2)$. Thus
\[
 A^{\mathrm{cg}}_{1,3}(n)=\mathrm{A189358}(n),\qquad
 A^{\mathrm{cg}}_{1,2}(n)=\mathrm{A137774}(n)
\]
\cite{cgOeisCamel,cgOeisKnight}. No cyclic wraparound is imposed on positions
or values.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform joint expansion; Report~232]
\label[theorem]{spf:cg:thm:main}
For fixed positive distinct integers $r<s$ and each integer $K\ge0$, there
are rational polynomials $\mathfrak B_j(u,v;r,s)$, $0\le j\le K$, such that
$\mathfrak B_0=1$, $\deg\mathfrak B_j\le2j$, and for every fixed $U<\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 G_n(u,v;r,s)=e^{2u+2v}\sum_{j=0}^{K}\frac{\mathfrak B_j(u,v;r,s)}{n^j}
       +\bigO_{r,s,K,U}(n^{-K-1})
 \label{spf:cg:eq:main}
\end{equation}
uniformly for complex $|u|,|v|\le U$. The coefficient polynomials admit a
finite exact-arithmetic construction. Equivalently, $\mathfrak B_j$ is
determined by the mixed binomial moments through total marked degree $2j$,
once the lower orders have been determined.
\end{theorem}
The quantifier ``fixed order'' permits every prescribed $K$, with constants
and an eventual onset depending on $K$. It does not assert convergence of the
infinite $1/n$ series, uniformity for growing gaps, or an effective numerical
error bound at a stated small $n$.

\begin{theorem}[The first two corrections; Report~232]
\label[theorem]{spf:cg:thm:two}
For $R\in\{2,3\}$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak B_{1,R}(u,v)=-2R(u+v)-2(u^2+v^2).
 \label{spf:cg:eq:B1}
\end{equation}
The second polynomials are
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak B_{2,3}(u,v)={}&2(u^4+v^4)+14(u^3+v^3)+22(u^2+v^2)\notag\\
              &+8u^2v^2+12(u^2v+uv^2)+44uv,\label{spf:cg:eq:B2camel}\\
 \mathfrak B_{2,2}(u,v)={}&2(u^4+v^4)+10(u^3+v^3)+10(u^2+v^2)\notag\\
              &+8u^2v^2+4(u^2v+uv^2)+24uv.\label{spf:cg:eq:B2knight}
\end{align}
Consequently,
\begin{align}
 A^{\mathrm{cg}}_{1,3}(n)&=e^{-4}n!\left(1+\frac8n+\frac{48}{n^2}+\bigO(n^{-3})\right),\label{spf:cg:eq:camel}\\
 A^{\mathrm{cg}}_{1,2}(n)&=e^{-4}n!\left(1+\frac4n+\frac{28}{n^2}+\bigO(n^{-3})\right).\label{spf:cg:eq:knight}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}
The coefficient theorem is not a conclusion drawn by fitting a few numerical
moments. Its logical order is: prove the uniform expansion and degree bound;
construct every necessary exact embedding polynomial; then extract the
finite coefficient data. \Cref{spf:cg:sec:exact,spf:cg:sec:cores,spf:cg:sec:packing,spf:cg:sec:assembly}
give the proof and \cref{spf:cg:sec:coefficients} gives the finite
calculation.

\begin{remark}[The marginals are Part~I's absolute expansions]
\label[remark]{spf:cg:rem:marginals}
[Batch-101 write.] For every $j$ and every $1\le r<s$,
\[
 \mathfrak B_j(u,0;r,s)=B_j^{(2)}(u;r,s),\qquad
 \mathfrak B_j(0,v;r,s)=B_j^{(2)}(v;s,r)=B_j^{(2)}(v;r,s).
\]
\emph{Proof.} By the note after \eqref{spf:cg:eq:pgf},
$G_n(u,0;r,s)=G^{(2)}_{r,s,n}(u)$. \Cref{spf:cg:thm:main} at $v=0$ and
Part~I's \cref{spf:thm:main} both expand this function, for each fixed
complex $u$, as $e^{2u}\sum_{j\le K}P_j(u)n^{-j}+\bigO(n^{-K-1})$. Subtracting,
$e^{2u}\sum_{j\le K}(\mathfrak B_j(u,0)-B_j^{(2)}(u))n^{-j}=\bigO(n^{-K-1})$;
multiplying by $n^0,n^1,\ldots$ in turn and letting $n\to\infty$ shows that
every difference vanishes at every $u$, so the polynomials agree. The same
argument at $u=0$ gives the second identity, and the last equality is the
symmetry in \eqref{spf:eq:arithmetic-preview}. $\square$

At $(r,s)=(1,R)$, \eqref{spf:cg:eq:B1} restricts to
$-2Ru-2u^2=B_1^{(2)}(u;1,R)$ of \eqref{spf:eq:B1}, and
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:B2camel}, \eqref{spf:cg:eq:B2knight} restrict to
$2u^4+14u^3+22u^2$ and $2u^4+10u^3+10u^2$, which are
\eqref{spf:eq:B2-absolute} at $(1,3)$ and $(1,2)$. The marginal avoidance
coefficients are therefore $c^{(2)}_1,c^{(2)}_2=4,10$ (camel) and $2,2$
(knight), as Report~232 states in \cref{spf:cg:sec:interaction}; they are
Part~I's and Part~II's values, a second route, and the printed polynomials
pass this check exactly.
\end{remark}

\subsection{What is proved and what is numerical}
The proofs establish \eqref{spf:cg:eq:main} and the inverse statements for all
sufficiently large $n$ or thresholds. The code supplies finite checks and
exact certificates, rather than proving an asymptotic remainder from
numerical agreement. It uses rational arithmetic throughout its certificate
path.

The eventual embedding polynomials for graphs with at most four edges are
safe for $n\ge12$ in the camel case and $n\ge8$ in the knight case. These are
\emph{polynomial-identity thresholds}, not the onset of asymptotic accuracy.
In fact, the camel fourth factorial-moment polynomial fails at $n=9$; the
report preserves that counterexample explicitly
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:counterexample}. Small-$n$ checks below the safe thresholds
use actual edge-set counts instead of extrapolating the eventual
polynomials.

\section{Exact graph identities (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:exact}

\subsection{Selected edges and injective embeddings}
A coloured graph has no isolated vertices unless it is the empty graph. For
such a graph $H$, let $h=|V(H)|$, $e_A(H),e_B(H)$ be the colour counts, and
$\operatorname{aut}(H)=|\operatorname{Aut}(H)|$ be the colour-preserving
automorphism count. Write $i_S(H;n)$ and $i_T(H;n)$ for the numbers of
injective colour-preserving homomorphisms from $H$ into $S_n$ and $T_n$.
These homomorphisms need not be induced.

\begin{proposition}[Exact coloured graph identity; Report~232]
\label[proposition]{spf:cg:prop:identity}
For every $n$,
\begin{equation}
 G_n(u,v;r,s)=\sum_{[H]}
 \frac{i_S(H;n)\,i_T(H;n)}{\operatorname{aut}(H)\fall n{|V(H)|}}
 u^{e_A(H)}v^{e_B(H)}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:identity}
\end{equation}
The sum is over colour-preserving isomorphism types of selected source-edge
sets, including the empty graph with contribution $1$. In particular only
$|V(H)|\le n$ occurs.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a source edge $e$ let $I_e$ indicate that its image is a target edge of
the same colour. Expanding the finite product
\[
 \prod_{e\in E_A(S_n)}(1+uI_e)
 \prod_{e\in E_B(S_n)}(1+vI_e)
\]
selects an arbitrary coloured source-edge subset $F$. If its isomorphism type
is $H$, the restriction of $\pi$ to its $h$ vertices is uniform over the
$\fall nh$ injections into $[n]$. Exactly $i_T(H;n)$ restrictions satisfy
every selected event. There are $i_S(H;n)/\operatorname{aut}(H)$ source-edge
sets of type $H$: every such edge set has precisely $\operatorname{aut}(H)$
isomorphisms from a fixed representative. Grouping the expectation by type
gives \eqref{spf:cg:eq:identity}. The argument neither deletes cycles nor
assumes the two colours independent.
\end{proof}
[Note of the batch-101 write: for a single gap the selected graphs are path
forests and \eqref{spf:cg:eq:identity} specializes to Part~I's
Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:exact}.]

Put $N_S(H;n)=i_S(H;n)/\operatorname{aut}(H)$, and similarly for $T$. Then
the contribution of $H$ may also be written
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\operatorname{aut}(H)N_S(H;n)N_T(H;n)}{\fall n{|V(H)|}}
       u^{e_A(H)}v^{e_B(H)}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:unlabelled}
\end{equation}
Because $T_n$ is obtained by swapping colours, $N_T(H;n)=N_S(H^*;n)$, where
$H^*$ is $H$ with its colours exchanged. This will simplify the exact checks.

\subsection{Partition-lattice certificates}
\begin{proposition}[Exact eventual embedding polynomials; Report~232]
\label[proposition]{spf:cg:prop:mobius}
For every finite coloured graph $H$, its injection counts into the two-gap
source and target graphs are eventual polynomials in $n$ with integer
coefficients. The unlabelled counts $N_S,N_T$ are eventual rational
polynomials. An exact finite construction is
\begin{equation}
 i_S(H;n)=\sum_{\rho\in\Pi(V(H))}\mu(\rho)\,
                 \operatorname{hom}(H/\rho,S_n),\qquad
 \mu(\rho)=\prod_{\mathsf b\in\rho}(-1)^{|\mathsf b|-1}(|\mathsf b|-1)!.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:mobius}
\end{equation}
Here $\Pi(V(H))$ is the set of partitions of the vertex set, and
$\mathsf b$ runs over the blocks of $\rho$. The polynomials are valid for
$n\ge s\,e(H)$; a more precise safe bound is the sum of the specified edge
lengths. The same assertion holds for $T_n$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A map from $V(H)$ to $[n]$ has a kernel partition recording equal images.
M\"obius inversion on the partition lattice isolates the discrete kernel,
giving \eqref{spf:cg:eq:mobius}. A quotient with a loop contributes zero,
since all gaps are positive. If two constraints on the same quotient pair
demand distinct lengths, that quotient also contributes zero. Repeated
identical constraints may be reduced to one.

Consider a connected quotient $Q$. Fix a root and a spanning tree. Give each
tree edge one of its two signs and propagate relative integer coordinates
from root coordinate $0$. There are finitely many sign assignments. Retain
an assignment precisely when every non-tree edge has its required absolute
length. At this homomorphism stage coincident coordinates at distinct
quotient vertices are allowed. Each valid relative assignment has span
\[
 d=\max_{w\in V(Q)}x_w-\min_{w\in V(Q)}x_w
\]
and exactly $\max(0,n-d)$ translations into $[n]$. Its span is at most the
sum of the tree-edge lengths, hence at most the sum of the original edge
lengths. For $n$ at least that bound its contribution is $n-d$. Thus the
connected homomorphism polynomial is $\alpha_Qn-\beta_Q$, with
$\alpha_Q,\beta_Q$ nonnegative integers. For a disconnected quotient its
components map independently, so their homomorphism polynomials multiply.
The finite integer linear combination \eqref{spf:cg:eq:mobius} is the claimed
polynomial. Division by $\operatorname{aut}(H)$ gives the unlabelled count.
All target lengths are also at most $s$, so the same safe threshold $se(H)$
applies to both sides.
\end{proof}
The formula is an arithmetic certificate, not interpolation. Every sign
assignment is tested against every constraint. In particular an incompatible
cycle vanishes for a mathematical reason, not because it happens to be absent
from a finite numerical sample.

\subsection{The alternating square}
\label{spf:cg:sec:square}
For $(r,s)=(1,R)$, consider vertices at $0,1,R,R+1$, with $A$ edges
$0$--$1$ and $R$--$(R+1)$ and $B$ edges $0$--$R$ and $1$--$(R+1)$.
The permutation of these coordinates
\[
 (0,1,R,R+1)\longmapsto(0,R,1,R+1)
\]
interchanges the required lengths, so this coloured square embeds on both
sides. Its colour-preserving automorphism group has order $4$. For
$R=2,3$ all injective realizations are the translated alternating rectangle
and its four labellings; the exact counts are
\begin{equation}
 i_S(H;n)=i_T(H;n)=4\max(0,n-R-1).
 \label{spf:cg:eq:squarecount}
\end{equation}
To check exhaustiveness, the two equal-colour opposite sides have signed
length $1$ and the other two have signed length $R$. Closing the square forces
the two $1$ steps to cancel and the two $R$ steps to cancel; otherwise one
would have $2=2R$. Distinctness forces the four corners of the stated
rectangle. Hence for $n\ge\max(4,R+1)$ its exact graph contribution is
\begin{equation}
 \frac{4(n-R-1)^2}{\fall n4}\,u^2v^2
       =\frac{4u^2v^2}{n^2}+\bigO(n^{-3}).
 \label{spf:cg:eq:squareterm}
\end{equation}
For the camel there is also a source four-cycle using three length-$1$ edges
and one length-$3$ edge. In the target, three signed $3$ steps cannot sum to
a signed $1$ step. That cycle is incompatible and contributes zero. Both
examples show why cycles must be tested rather than excluded wholesale.

\section{Connected cores and positive tail bounds (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:cores}

\subsection{Deficit measures the asymptotic cost}
Let $c(H)$ be the number of connected components and $v(H)=|V(H)|$. Define
the \emph{deficit}
\begin{equation}
 \delta(H)=v(H)-2c(H).
 \label{spf:cg:eq:deficit}
\end{equation}
[Note of the batch-101 write: not Part~I's defect $k-c$; the two agree on
forests only (\cref{spf:cg:sec:notation}).] A component consisting of one
edge has deficit zero. Every other connected component has at least three
vertices and positive deficit. The \emph{core} of $H$ is the union of its
nontrivial components; throughout, ``nontrivial'' means having at least three
vertices. If the core has $t$ components and $h_0$ vertices, then
\begin{equation}
 h_0=\delta+2t\le3\delta,
 \label{spf:cg:eq:corevertices}
\end{equation}
since $t\le\delta$. Both source and target placements of each component have
one translational freedom, whereas division by $\fall n{v(H)}$ costs one
power of $n$ per vertex. Thus its leading scale is $n^{-\delta}$. We next
make the required finite-core and tail claims precise.

\begin{lemma}[Compatible cores have few edges; Report~232]
\label[lemma]{spf:cg:lem:coreedges}
Every graph admitting embeddings in both $S_n$ and $T_n$ is triangle-free
and $2$-degenerate. Each of its connected components with $v\ge3$ vertices
has at most $2v-4$ edges. Consequently its nontrivial core satisfies
\begin{equation}
 e_{\rm core}\le2\delta,\qquad h_0\le3\delta.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:coreedges}
\end{equation}
For fixed $K$, only finitely many compatible core isomorphism types and
component multiplicity vectors have deficit at most $K$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In any subgraph of $S_n$, the vertex with least coordinate has at most two
neighbours to its right, at distances $r$ and $s$. Remove it and repeat. This
is a degeneracy ordering with at most two later neighbours per vertex.

Suppose a triangle is realizable in $S_n$. Order its three coordinates.
The largest gap must equal the sum of the other two. With only $r<s$
available, this forces $s=2r$ and length multiset $(r,r,s)$. After colour
reversal, the target triangle would require length multiset $(s,s,r)$.
The largest of these lengths cannot be the sum of the other two, so no
three distinct real coordinates can realize it. A compatible triangle
therefore cannot exist.

For a triangle-free $2$-degenerate graph on $v\ge3$ vertices, remove $v-3$
vertices in a degeneracy ordering. At most $2(v-3)$ edges are removed and
at most two remain among the final three vertices. Hence $e\le2v-4$.
Summing this inequality over core components gives $e_{\rm core}\le2\delta$.
Equation~\eqref{spf:cg:eq:corevertices} gives the vertex bound. There are
only finitely many coloured finite graphs on at most $3K$ vertices, proving
finiteness.
\end{proof}
This lemma uses the reciprocal two-gap structure. A general union of many
coloured gaps can admit triangles and has a different degree bound; the
present theorem should not be silently extended to that setting.

\begin{remark}[Cycles enter first at order $n^{-2}$]
\label[remark]{spf:cg:rem:cycles}
[Batch-101 write; this answers the cycle part of \cref{spf:mg:q:several}
for reciprocal pairs.] Let $1\le r<s$. A compatible graph containing a cycle
has a component with a cycle; by \cref{spf:cg:lem:coreedges} that cycle has
at least four vertices, so the component has deficit $v-2\ge2$. Graphs
containing cycles therefore enter \eqref{spf:cg:eq:identity} only at
order $n^{-2}$ or later. At order $n^{-2}$ exactly one cyclic core can occur,
a single component with four vertices and a cycle, that is a coloured
four-cycle (a fifth edge would create a triangle). Among the six colour
patterns of a four-cycle only the alternating one, $ABAB$, embeds on both
sides:
\begin{itemize}
\item $AAAA$ and $BBBB$: four signed steps of one length return to the start
 only by revisiting a vertex, so there is no source embedding;
\item $AAAB$: three signed $r$ steps must sum to $\mp s$, which needs
 $s\in\{r,3r\}$; in the target three signed $s$ steps must sum to $\mp r$,
 impossible since $r<s$; $ABBB$ is impossible in the source for the same
 reason;
\item $AABB$ (adjacent equal colours): the two $A$ steps must be equal (else
 a vertex repeats), so their sum is $\pm2r$, which must equal minus the sum
 of the two $B$ steps, in $\{0,\pm2s\}$; this is impossible;
\item $ABAB$: the steps close only as $a,b,-a,-b$, since $\pm2r=\pm2s$ is
 impossible, which is the rectangle $\{0,r,s,r+s\}$ of
 \cref{spf:cg:sec:square}, with $i_S=i_T=4\max(0,n-r-s)$ and
 $\operatorname{aut}=4$, exactly as there with $R+1$ replaced by $r+s$.
\end{itemize}
In the resummation of \cref{spf:cg:sec:assembly}, the alternating-square core
has one source and one target species, so $p_{0,\boldsymbol t}=1$ in
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:packingleading}, and \eqref{spf:cg:eq:autfactor},
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:normalization} and \eqref{spf:cg:eq:poissonsum} give its
sector as $4u^2v^2e^{2u+2v}n^{-2}+\bigO(n^{-3})$. Hence the selected graphs
with cycles contribute exactly $4u^2v^2$ to $\mathfrak B_2(u,v;r,s)$ for
every $1\le r<s$, and nothing earlier; incompatible cycles, such as the
camel's $AAAB$ square, contribute exactly zero.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A uniform exponential moment}
\begin{lemma}[Long selected-edge tail; Report~232]
\label[lemma]{spf:cg:lem:tail}
For every real $s_0\ge0$ and every $n$,
\begin{equation}
 \E e^{s_0(X_n+Y_n)}\le\exp\{2(e^{4s_0}-1)\}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:exponentialbound}
\end{equation}
For any fixed $U>0$, any $U'>U$, and any nonnegative integer $L$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k+l>L}U^{k+l}\E\left[\binom{X_n}{k}\binom{Y_n}{l}\right]
 \le (U/U')^L\,
    \exp\{2((1+U')^4-1)\}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:markedtail}
\end{equation}
In particular, the joint marked expansion can be truncated at
$L=C_K\log n$ with uniform error $\bigO(n^{-K-1})$ on $|u|,|v|\le U$, by
choosing $C_K$ sufficiently large.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A single-length source graph is a disjoint union of paths, one for each
residue class modulo its length. Alternately colouring each path's edges
splits it into two matchings. Both source lengths together therefore split
into four matchings. Each matching has at most $M=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ edges.
Let $Z$ count its violations.

Select $k$ specified matching edges. After assigning the image vertices of
the preceding $j$ edges, there are $n-2j$ unused target vertices. Whatever
the preceding assignment, there are at most $2(n-2j)$ ordered pairs of
unused vertices at a specified absolute difference: the graph of that
length has maximum degree two. Dividing by $(n-2j)(n-2j-1)$ bounds the
conditional probability by $2/(n-2j-1)$. Thus the probability of all $k$
events is at most
\[
 \frac{2^k}{\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}(n-2j-1)}.
\]
The coefficient $\E\binom Zk$ sums over at most $\binom Mk$ selections.
For $0\le j<k\le M$, $M-j\le n-2j-1$; hence
\[
 \E\binom Zk\le\frac{2^k}{k!}.
\]
For $t\ge0$, the binomial expansion gives
$\E(1+t)^Z\le e^{2t}$. Take $t=e^{4s_0}-1$, so that
$\E e^{4s_0Z}\le\exp\{2(e^{4s_0}-1)\}$, and apply H\"older's inequality with
four exponents equal to four to the four matching counts. This proves
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:exponentialbound}. No independence between the matchings
was used.

All mixed binomial moments are nonnegative. In the terms $k+l>L$, replace
$U^{k+l}$ by at most $(U/U')^LU'^{k+l}$, sum the full binomial expansion,
and apply \eqref{spf:cg:eq:exponentialbound} with $s_0=\log(1+U')$. This
proves \eqref{spf:cg:eq:markedtail}. Choosing $C_K$ with
$C_K\log(U'/U)>K+1$ gives the asserted error. At $U=0$ the statement is
immediate, and a larger positive $U$ can always bound a given compact set.
\end{proof}
The bound controls the sum of absolute marked contributions, so it applies
to complex marks and to $u=v=-1$ without invoking cancellation in
inclusion--exclusion. [Note of the batch-101 write: Report~232 writes
``Take $t=e^{s_0}-1$ and apply H\"older''; the exponent $4s_0$ is what
H\"older's inequality with four factors requires, and it is the exponent in
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:exponentialbound}. The write states the step that way. In
the role it plays, this lemma replaces Part~I's
Lemma~\ref{spf:lem:moment-bound}; the proof is different.]

\subsection{A geometric majorant for large deficits}
\begin{lemma}[High-deficit core tail; Report~232]
\label[lemma]{spf:cg:lem:highdeficit}
Fix $U,K$, and a cutoff $L=C\log n$. Within the exact selected-edge sum
restricted to at most $L$ edges, the total absolute contribution of cores
with deficit $\delta\ge K+1$ is $\bigO(n^{-K-1})$ uniformly on
$|u|,|v|\le U$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The number of vertices of any retained graph is at most $2L$. For all
sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{n^v}{\fall nv}=\prod_{i=0}^{v-1}(1-i/n)^{-1}\le2
 \qquad(0\le v\le2L),
 \label{spf:cg:eq:denbound}
\end{equation}
since the logarithm is $\bigO(L^2/n)$. This is a uniform positive bound.

A connected source component on $v$ vertices, anchored at its smallest
coordinate, has support in $[0,s(v-1)]$: follow paths from the anchor, whose
edge lengths are at most $s$. The number of possible supports of size $v$
in this interval is at most an exponential $C_1^v$ depending only on $s$.
There are at most $2v$ candidate source edges between the occupied vertices,
so the possible selected edge sets cost another factor $2^{2v}$. Thus there
are at most $C^v$ anchored connected source shapes (\cref{spf:cg:rem:shapes}).
Given one fixed source component, choose the image of a fixed root in at most
$n$ ways and the signs on a rooted spanning tree in at most $2^{v-1}$ ways.
Ignoring target collisions and cycle consistency increases this count, so the
component has at most $n2^{v-1}$ target maps.

Suppose the core has deficit $\delta$, $t$ components, and
$h_0=\delta+2t\le3\delta$ vertices. Write their positive deficits as an
ordered composition of $\delta$ into $t$ parts. There are
$\binom{\delta-1}{t-1}$ such compositions. For each one, ordered core source
placements cost at most $n^tC^{h_0}$. Divide by $t!$ for unordered
components. This division remains valid when abstract component types
repeat: in an actual valid packing the placed nonempty vertex supports are
disjoint and therefore the placed components are distinct; every unordered
packing has exactly $t!$ orderings. Allowing repeated or intersecting
placements in the ordered upper count only makes it larger.

Let $j$ be the number of remaining isolated edges, with either colour
allowed. Their source selections cost at most $(2n)^j/j!$, because the
source has at most $2n$ edges. For each of them there are at most $2n$
target assignments, giving $(2n)^j$. Core target assignments cost at most
$n^tC^{h_0}$ after enlarging $C$. Divide by the falling factorial for
$v=2j+h_0$ vertices and use \eqref{spf:cg:eq:denbound}. The absolute marking
factor is at most $U^j\max(1,U)^{2h_0}$, a deliberately loose bound valid
because the core has at most $2h_0$ edges. We obtain, with constants
depending only on $r,s,U$,
\begin{equation}
 2\,\frac{(4U)^j}{j!}\,
 C_U^{3\delta}n^{-\delta}
 \frac{\binom{\delta-1}{t-1}}{t!}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:sectorbound}
\end{equation}
This bounds the contribution directly in the marked graph sum; no
momentwise cancellation or collision expansion is being assumed.

Sum first over $j\ge0$, which contributes $e^{4U}$, and then over
$1\le t\le\delta$. The latter sum is at most
$\sum_t\binom{\delta-1}{t-1}=2^{\delta-1}$. Thus the full deficit-$\delta$
sector is bounded by $C'_U(C''_U/n)^\delta$. Once $n>2C''_U$, summing over
$\delta\ge K+1$ is a convergent geometric tail of order $n^{-K-1}$.
The empty core is handled separately in the finite-core part of the proof.
\end{proof}
The absence of a factor growing with $L$ in the final error is important.
Bounding each fixed-deficit moment crudely and then retaining a logarithmic
loss would not by itself prove the remainder in \cref{spf:cg:thm:main}.

\begin{remark}[The count of anchored shapes]
\label[remark]{spf:cg:rem:shapes}
[Batch-101 write: Report~232 asserts the bound $C_1^v$ without computing it;
here is a value.] A support of size $v$ contains the anchor $0$ and $v-1$
further points of $\{1,\ldots,s(v-1)\}$, so there are at most
$\binom{s(v-1)}{v-1}\le\bigl(es(v-1)/(v-1)\bigr)^{v-1}=(es)^{v-1}$ supports,
by $\binom mk\le(em/k)^k$. Each occupied vertex has at most two source
neighbours to its right, so at most $2v$ candidate edges join occupied
vertices and at most $4^v$ edge sets can be selected. Hence there are at most
$(es)^{v-1}4^v\le(4es)^v$ anchored connected source shapes on $v$ vertices,
and the proof above holds with $C_1=es$ and $C=4es$ before the enlargement
made for the target maps.
\end{remark}

\section{A uniform finite-range packing lemma (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:packing}

\subsection{Species and the exact finite matrix}
A polymer species consists of a finite nonempty support
$\mathsf P\subseteq\{0,\ldots,D\}$ with $\min\mathsf P=0$, together with a
species identity and a type label. A placement translates its occupied
support into $[n]$. A packing is an unordered set of placements whose
occupied supports are pairwise disjoint. Their containing intervals are
allowed to overlap. Distinct species may have the same support, and distinct
species may have the same type. The identity can encode a selected coloured
edge pattern, not just the occupied vertices.

Assume exactly one species has type $x$, exactly one has type $y$, and
$a_i$ species have type $w_i$, for $1\le i\le q$. Let
$Z_n(x,y,\boldsymbol w)$ be the packing polynomial, including the empty
packing, and for a fixed multi-index $\boldsymbol t\in\N^q$ put
\[
 N_{k,l,\boldsymbol t}(n)=[x^ky^l\boldsymbol w^{\boldsymbol t}]\,Z_n(x,y,\boldsymbol w),\qquad
 |\boldsymbol t|=t_1+\cdots+t_q.
\]
An absent type with $a_i=0$ and $t_i>0$ makes this coefficient identically
zero and may be discarded.

\begin{theorem}[Factorial-scale packing expansion; Report~232]
\label[theorem]{spf:cg:thm:packing}
For every fixed finite species dictionary, multi-index $\boldsymbol t$, order
$J\ge0$, and $C>0$, uniformly in nonnegative $k,l$ with $k+l\le C\log n$,
\begin{equation}
 n^{-(k+l+|\boldsymbol t|)}N_{k,l,\boldsymbol t}(n)
 =\frac1{k!\,l!}\bigg\{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\frac{p_{j,\boldsymbol t}(k,l)}{n^j}
 +\bigO\!\left(\frac{(1+k+l)^{2J+3}}{n^{J+1}}\right)\bigg\}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:packinglemma}
\end{equation}
The $p_{j,\boldsymbol t}$ are rational polynomials of total degree at most
$2j$, and
\begin{equation}
 p_{0,\boldsymbol t}=\prod_{i=1}^{q}\frac{a_i^{t_i}}{t_i!}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:packingleading}
\end{equation}
Constants may depend on the displayed fixed parameters, but not on $n,k,l$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
We give the finite matrix, the uniform analytic estimate, and the
coefficient extraction separately. These are all needed: an expansion at
fixed $k,l$ alone would not justify the final resummation.

\paragraph{The occupancy matrix and both boundaries.}
Use a $D$-bit mask $b=(b_0,\ldots,b_{D-1})$ immediately before scanning the
current site. A set bit records a site already occupied by a polymer that
began earlier, from the current site through $D-1$ sites ahead. If $b_0=1$,
no species can begin here; shift the mask one site left. If $b_0=0$, either
leave this site empty and shift, or begin one species $\mathsf P$ whose
occupied sites avoid the preexisting mask. Insert all of $\mathsf P$,
including its possible site $D$, then shift. This gives a $D$-bit next mask.
Assign weight $1$ for no start and the species' type variable for a start,
adding weights when transitions coincide. All supports contain their anchor
$0$, so two species cannot begin at the same site in a valid packing.

These rules define a finite matrix $\mathbf T(\boldsymbol z)$ polynomial in
the activities $\boldsymbol z=(x,y,w_1,\ldots,w_q)$. With $e_0$ the
empty-mask vector,
\begin{equation}
 Z_n(\boldsymbol z)=e_0^{\mathsf T}\,\mathbf T(\boldsymbol z)^n\,e_0.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:transfer}
\end{equation}
The initial empty mask enforces the left boundary. After $n$ sites have
been scanned, a polymer extending beyond $[n]$ leaves a nonempty mask.
Requiring the final empty mask therefore enforces the right boundary
exactly. Disjoint supports such as $\{0,3\}$ and $\{1,2\}$ are permitted:
the mask tests occupied sites, not containing intervals.

At zero activity every state shifts to zero within $D$ steps. Hence
$\mathbf T(0)^D$ has all its rows concentrated in column zero, has rank one,
and is idempotent. The eigenvalue $1$ of $\mathbf T(0)$ is algebraically
simple, and all other eigenvalues are zero. The case $D=0$, if present, is a
scalar matrix and follows by the same argument without the flushing step.

\paragraph{Analytic spectral decomposition.}
Finite-dimensional analytic perturbation at this isolated simple eigenvalue
gives a complex polydisc about $0$ on which an analytic eigenvalue
$\lambda(\boldsymbol z)$ and analytic spectral projection $P(\boldsymbol z)$
exist, with $\lambda(0)=1$. Put
\[
 Q(\boldsymbol z)=\mathbf T(\boldsymbol z)-\lambda(\boldsymbol z)P(\boldsymbol z).
\]
Then $PQ=QP=0$. On a sufficiently small closed polydisc the spectrum of
$Q$ is inside a circle $|\zeta|=\rho<1$. The contour formula for powers,
\[
 Q(\boldsymbol z)^n=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{|\zeta|=\rho}
 \zeta^n(\zeta I-Q(\boldsymbol z))^{-1}\,d\zeta,
\]
with a fixed uniformly bounded resolvent, gives
$\|Q(\boldsymbol z)^n\|\le C_0\rho^n$. Let
$\alpha(\boldsymbol z)=e_0^{\mathsf T}P(\boldsymbol z)e_0$. It satisfies
$\alpha(0)=1$. Shrinking the polydisc again gives analytic logarithms
$F=\log\lambda$ and $\Omega=\log\alpha$, and therefore
\begin{equation}
 Z_n(\boldsymbol z)=\exp\{nF(\boldsymbol z)+\Omega(\boldsymbol z)\}+\varepsilon_n(\boldsymbol z),\qquad
 |\varepsilon_n(\boldsymbol z)|\le C_0\rho^n.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:spectral}
\end{equation}
Both $F$ and $\Omega$ vanish at $0$.

The linear part of $F$ is
\begin{equation}
 F_1(\boldsymbol z)=x+y+\sum_ia_iw_i.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:linearF}
\end{equation}
Indeed a single fixed species of diameter $d$ has $n-d$ placements for
$n\ge D$. Compare its linear activity coefficient in
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:spectral}, divide by $n$, and let $n\to\infty$; Cauchy's
estimate makes the corresponding coefficient of $\varepsilon_n$
exponentially small. Each species contributes $1$ to $F_1$, and grouping
species by type gives $a_i$.

\paragraph{Scale every activity.}
Write now $\boldsymbol z=(X,Y,W_1,\ldots,W_q)$ and scale \emph{all}
activities by $1/n$, including the core markers $W_i$. Define
\begin{equation}
 \Phi(h,\boldsymbol z)=\frac{F(h\boldsymbol z)-hF_1(\boldsymbol z)}h+\Omega(h\boldsymbol z).
 \label{spf:cg:eq:Phi}
\end{equation}
The apparent singularity at $h=0$ is removable. If $F_d,\Omega_d$ are
homogeneous parts, then the coefficient of $h^j$ in $\Phi$ is
$F_{j+1}(\boldsymbol z)+\Omega_j(\boldsymbol z)$, of degree at most $j+1$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 e^{\Phi(h,\boldsymbol z)}=\sum_{j=0}^{J}P_j(\boldsymbol z)h^j+\text{remainder},
 \qquad P_0=1,\quad\deg P_j\le2j.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:Pj}
\end{equation}
For the degree bound, a product of exponent terms with positive orders
$j_1,\ldots,j_a$ summing to $j$ has degree at most
$\sum_i(j_i+1)=j+a\le2j$.

Put $s_*=1+\sum_i|z_i|$. On the circle $|h|=c/s_*^2$, with one sufficiently
small fixed $c>0$, the arguments $h\boldsymbol z$ lie in the analytic
polydisc. The quadratic-and-higher part of $F$ contributes $\bigO(|h|s_*^2)$
to \eqref{spf:cg:eq:Phi}, and $\Omega(h\boldsymbol z)$ contributes
$\bigO(|h|s_*)$. Hence $|\Phi|$ is bounded on the whole circle independently
of $\boldsymbol z$. Cauchy's Taylor estimate in $h$, evaluated at $h=1/n$
when $n\ge2s_*^2/c$, gives
\begin{equation}
 \left|e^{\Phi(1/n,\boldsymbol z)}-\sum_{j=0}^{J}P_j(\boldsymbol z)n^{-j}\right|
 \le C_Jn^{-J-1}s_*^{2J+2}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:analyticremainder}
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{spf:cg:eq:spectral}--\eqref{spf:cg:eq:Phi},
\[
 Z_n(\boldsymbol z/n)=e^{F_1(\boldsymbol z)}e^{\Phi(1/n,\boldsymbol z)}+\varepsilon_n(\boldsymbol z/n).
\]
Thus the analytic remainder from the first term is bounded by the right
side of \eqref{spf:cg:eq:analyticremainder} times
$e^{\Re F_1(\boldsymbol z)}$.

\paragraph{Extract coefficients at factorial-scale radii.}
Use the Cauchy circles
\[
 |X|=\max(1,k),\qquad |Y|=\max(1,l),\qquad |W_i|=1.
\]
If $m=k+l\le C\log n$, then $s_*\le m+q+3$, so the previous estimate
applies uniformly for all sufficiently large $n$. On the circles,
\[
 |e^{F_1(\boldsymbol z)}|\le C_1e^{\max(1,k)+\max(1,l)}.
\]
Stirling's upper bound for factorials gives, for every $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{e^{\max(1,k)}}{\max(1,k)^k}
       \le C_2\frac{\sqrt{k+1}}{k!}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:saddleradius}
\end{equation}
Cauchy's coefficient bound applied to \eqref{spf:cg:eq:analyticremainder},
followed by \eqref{spf:cg:eq:saddleradius} for $k$ and $l$, now yields
\[
 \bigO\!\left(\frac{(1+m)^{2J+3}}{k!\,l!\,n^{J+1}}\right).
\]
The two square roots cost at most one extra power of $1+m$. The fixed
$W_i$ coefficient circles have radius one and add only a constant. The
spectral remainder contributes at most
$C_0\rho^n/(\max(1,k)^k\max(1,l)^l)$, which is smaller than the same bound
uniformly for $m\le C\log n$.

Finally,
\[
 [X^kY^l\boldsymbol W^{\boldsymbol t}]\,Z_n(\boldsymbol z/n)
       =n^{-(k+l+|\boldsymbol t|)}N_{k,l,\boldsymbol t}(n).
\]
Since
\begin{equation}
 [X^kY^l]\,e^{X+Y}X^aY^b
       =\frac{\fall ka\,\fall lb}{k!\,l!},
 \label{spf:cg:eq:fallingextraction}
\end{equation}
coefficient extraction from $e^{F_1}P_j$ yields a polynomial in $k,l$ of
degree at most $2j$. The $W$ coefficients at $j=0$ give
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:packingleading}. Rationality follows by expanding the
finite matrix's characteristic equation at its simple eigenvalue $1$: its
coefficients are rational and the nonzero linear derivative permits a
rational recursive solution. The spectral projection and its amplitude
also have rational Taylor recursions, hence so do $F,\Omega,P_j$ and the
extracted coefficients (\cref{spf:cg:rem:rational}). This completes the
proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Rationality of the packing coefficients]
\label[remark]{spf:cg:rem:rational}
[Batch-101 write: the last step of the proof above is stated in two
sentences; here it is in full.] Let $\chi(\zeta,\boldsymbol z)=\det(\zeta I-\mathbf T(\boldsymbol z))\in\Z[\zeta,\boldsymbol z]$.
Since $1$ is a simple root of $\chi(\cdot,0)$,
$\partial_\zeta\chi(1,0)\ne0$, and the formal implicit-function theorem gives
a unique power series $\lambda(\boldsymbol z)$ with $\lambda(0)=1$ and
$\chi(\lambda,\boldsymbol z)=0$; comparing coefficients degree by degree
determines each coefficient by rational operations from earlier ones and
division by $\partial_\zeta\chi(1,0)\in\Z\setminus\{0\}$, so
$\lambda\in\Q[[\boldsymbol z]]$, and it is the Taylor series of the analytic
eigenvalue. For a simple eigenvalue the spectral projection is
$P=\operatorname{adj}(\lambda I-\mathbf T)/\partial_\zeta\chi(\lambda,\boldsymbol z)$
near $0$. Indeed, at each $\boldsymbol z$ near $0$ the matrix
$\lambda I-\mathbf T$ has rank one less than its size, so its adjugate is a
multiple of $xy^{\mathsf T}$, where $x$ and $y$ span its right and left
kernels; so is $P=xy^{\mathsf T}/(y^{\mathsf T}x)$; and the traces are
$\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{adj}(\lambda I-\mathbf T)=\partial_\zeta\chi(\lambda,\boldsymbol z)\ne0$
and $\operatorname{tr}P=1$. Its entries are therefore in $\Q[[\boldsymbol z]]$, hence so is
$\alpha=e_0^{\mathsf T}Pe_0$ with $\alpha(0)=1$, and $\log\lambda$,
$\log\alpha$, $\Phi$, $e^\Phi$, the $P_j$ and, by
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:fallingextraction}, the $p_{j,\boldsymbol t}$ have rational
coefficients. Independently, rationality of the $\mathfrak B_j$ themselves
also follows from \eqref{spf:cg:eq:finiteextract}: each mixed binomial moment
is, for $n\ge s(k+l)$, a finite sum of terms
$i_Si_T/(\operatorname{aut}\fall nv)$ with integer polynomials $i_S,i_T$
(\cref{spf:cg:prop:mobius}), so its expansion in $1/n$ has rational
coefficients.
\end{remark}

\section{Assembly of the uniform expansion (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:assembly}

We now prove \cref{spf:cg:thm:main}, including the normalization and the
infinite isolated-edge summation.

\subsection{Two finite polymer dictionaries for each core}
Fix a compatible multiset of nontrivial connected coloured graph types.
Let $t_Q$ be the multiplicity of type $Q$, let $\boldsymbol t=(t_Q)$,
$t=\sum_Qt_Q$, and let $h_0=\sum_Qt_Qv(Q)$. Its deficit is
$\delta=h_0-2t$. At fixed $\delta\le K$, there are finitely many such
vectors by \cref{spf:cg:lem:coreedges}.

For each connected type $Q$, list its normalized source realizations: the
selected coloured edge patterns with minimum occupied coordinate $0$.
Each has bounded diameter at most $s(v(Q)-1)$ and there are finitely many.
Treat every distinct selected edge pattern as a separate species, marked by
a common variable $w_Q$. Include one isolated $A$-edge species with marker
$x$ and one isolated $B$-edge species with marker $y$. This is the source
dictionary. Construct the target dictionary in the same fashion using the
reversed lengths. The two dictionaries may have different numbers of
species of a given type, but have the same finite type list.

Packings correspond bijectively to selected edge sets with the prescribed
component multiset. A component's minimum vertex is its unique anchor.
Separate component supports must be vertex-disjoint. Unselected ambient
edges joining distinct supports do not join components of the selected
graph, so no further restriction is imposed. Species identity retains the
selected edges even when distinct species have the same occupied support.
Thus the hypotheses of \cref{spf:cg:thm:packing} apply on both sides.

\subsection{Automorphisms and the denominator}
Add $k$ isolated $A$ edges and $l$ isolated $B$ edges. Write $m=k+l$. The
resulting abstract graph $H$ has $2m+h_0$ vertices and
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{aut}(H)=2^mk!\,l!\,\operatorname{aut}_{\boldsymbol t},\qquad
 \operatorname{aut}_{\boldsymbol t}=\prod_Q\operatorname{aut}(Q)^{t_Q}t_Q!.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:autfactor}
\end{equation}
The factors $2^m$ orient the isolated edges, $k!\,l!$ permute identical
isolated components within each colour, and $\operatorname{aut}_{\boldsymbol t}$
accounts for the core components. This is the automorphism factor in
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:unlabelled}; using the reciprocal factor here would be an
error.

Apply \cref{spf:cg:thm:packing} separately to source and target with
$J=K-\delta$. Each unlabelled count has normalization $n^{m+t}/(k!\,l!)$.
After multiplying the two counts, multiplying by \eqref{spf:cg:eq:autfactor},
and dividing by $\fall n{2m+h_0}$, the leading scale is
\begin{equation}
 n^{2t-h_0}\frac{2^m}{k!\,l!}
       =n^{-\delta}\frac{2^m}{k!\,l!}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:normalization}
\end{equation}
All fixed core constants are absorbed in the coefficient polynomials.

For completeness, the remaining denominator factor has its own uniform
expansion. Put $v=2m+h_0$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \log\frac{n^v}{\fall nv}
       =\sum_{a\ge1}\frac{n^{-a}}a\sum_{i=0}^{v-1}i^a.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:denlog}
\end{equation}
The inner power sum is a polynomial in $v$ of degree $a+1$. Its coefficient
at order $n^{-j}$ after exponentiation has degree at most $2j$ in $m$:
a product with total order $j$ has degree at most $j$ plus its number of
factors. For a uniform remainder, regard the product
$\prod_{i=0}^{v-1}(1-ih)^{-1}$ as analytic in $h$ on $|h|\le c/(1+m)^2$, with
$h_0$ fixed and $c$ sufficiently small. Its logarithm is bounded on that
circle, since $\sum_ii|h|=\bigO(1)$ and every factor stays away from zero.
Cauchy's estimate gives a remainder
\begin{equation}
 \bigO\!\left((1+m)^{2J+2}n^{-J-1}\right)
 \label{spf:cg:eq:denrem}
\end{equation}
uniformly for $m\le C\log n$.

Multiplying the two packing expansions and this denominator expansion is
legitimate on that range. Their fixed-order coefficients are polynomials
and their errors have a uniform polynomial majorant. The core sector is
therefore
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 &u^{e_A({\rm core})}v^{e_B({\rm core})}n^{-\delta}
 \frac{2^{k+l}u^kv^l}{k!\,l!}\\
 &\quad\times\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{K-\delta}
 \frac{q_{j,\boldsymbol t}(k,l)}{n^j}
 +\bigO\!\left(\frac{(1+k+l)^{d_K}}{n^{K-\delta+1}}\right)\right\},
 \end{split}
 \label{spf:cg:eq:coresector}
\end{equation}
with $\deg q_{j,\boldsymbol t}\le2j$ and some fixed exponent $d_K$. The
precise value of $d_K$ is immaterial because the factorially weighted
polynomial series is summable.

\subsection{Resummation and the degree bound}
Choose the logarithmic edge cutoff from \cref{spf:cg:lem:tail}. The actual
constraint in the fixed-core sector is
\[
 k+l+e_{\rm core}\le L,
\]
not merely $k+l\le L$. Since $e_{\rm core}\le2K$ is fixed, this is only a
bounded shift in the cutoff. For any fixed polynomial $q$, extending its
factorially weighted sum from that range to all $k,l\ge0$ makes a
superpolynomially small error. One direct bound groups by $m=k+l$:
\[
 \sum_{k+l=m}\frac{(2U)^{k+l}}{k!\,l!}(1+k+l)^d
       =\frac{(4U)^m}{m!}(1+m)^d.
\]
Its tail from $C\log n-\bigO(1)$ has logarithm at most
$-C\log n\log\log n+\bigO(\log n)$, so is smaller than every prescribed
inverse power of $n$ eventually.

The remainders in \eqref{spf:cg:eq:coresector} sum absolutely to
$\bigO(n^{-K-1})$ because
\[
 \sum_{k,l\ge0}\frac{(2U)^{k+l}}{k!\,l!}(1+k+l)^{d_K}<\infty.
\]
Expand $q_{j,\boldsymbol t}$ in the falling-factorial basis
$\fall ka\,\fall lb$. The exact Poisson summation identity is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k,l\ge0}\frac{2^{k+l}u^kv^l}{k!\,l!}\fall ka\,\fall lb
       =e^{2u+2v}(2u)^a(2v)^b.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:poissonsum}
\end{equation}
Hence the fixed-core order-$(\delta+j)$ contribution is $e^{2u+2v}$ times a
rational polynomial of degree at most
\[
 e_{\rm core}+2j\le2\delta+2j.
\]
There are finitely many core vectors with $\delta\le K$.
\Cref{spf:cg:lem:highdeficit} bounds all omitted deficits, and
\cref{spf:cg:lem:tail} bounds all omitted selected-edge counts. Together
these prove \eqref{spf:cg:eq:main}, uniformly for complex marks in the stated
compact set. The empty core at leading order has one isolated-edge species
of each colour on both sides. Its leading sum is exactly $e^{2u+2v}$, so
$\mathfrak B_0=1$. This proves \cref{spf:cg:thm:main}.

\subsection{Why finitely many moments suffice}
The theorem's degree bound gives a finite extraction rule, not an extra
hypothesis. Let $[n^{-j}]$ denote the coefficient in the proved expansion,
and truncate a bivariate formal series to total marked degree at most $2j$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak B_j(u,v)=\Bigl[e^{-2u-2v}\,[n^{-j}]\,G_n(u,v)\Bigr]_{\deg\le2j}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:finiteextract}
\end{equation}
The coefficients of $u^kv^l$ in $G_n$ are the mixed binomial moments.
Moments through total degree $2j$ therefore determine every coefficient of
$\mathfrak B_j$. Each such moment is obtained by the exact graph identity
from graphs with at most $2j$ edges, and \cref{spf:cg:prop:mobius} computes
every embedding polynomial. This supplies a second explicit finite
construction besides expanding the occupancy matrices. It is computationally
convenient at order two and remains finite at every fixed order.

\section{Explicit mixed corrections and exact checks (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:coefficients}

\subsection{The first correction by component sectors}
Let $(r,s)=(1,R)$ with $R=2$ or $3$, and write
$M_{k,l}=\E[\binom{X_n}{k}\binom{Y_n}{l}]$, $m=k+l$. The matching sector
has source count
\begin{equation}
 \frac{n^m}{k!\,l!}\bigg[1-\frac1n\bigg\{
 k+Rl+3\binom k2+3\binom l2+4kl\bigg\}
 +\bigO\!\left(\frac{(1+m)^d}{n^2}\right)\bigg].
 \label{spf:cg:eq:matchingcount}
\end{equation}
Here and below these expansions are uniform for $m\le C\log n$, with a
fixed polynomial remainder as guaranteed by the packing lemma. The terms
can also be read directly: a length-$d$ edge has $n-d$ placements; choosing
distinct same-colour edges costs $\binom k2/n$; excluding incident
same-colour pairs costs another $2\binom k2/n$; and cross-colour incidence
costs $4kl/n$. Multiple exclusions affect only the next order. In the
target count the linear boundary term is $Rk+l$.

Multiply the two matching counts, the automorphism factor $2^mk!\,l!$, and
the denominator expansion
$n^{2m}/\fall n{2m}=1+m(2m-1)/n+\bigO((1+m)^4/n^2)$. The relative first
coefficient in this sector simplifies to
\begin{equation}
 -(R-1)m-k^2-l^2-4kl.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:matchingfirst}
\end{equation}
The only other deficit-one graphs are one two-edge path plus isolated
edges. An $AA$ path contributes the relative coefficient $\binom k2$; a
$BB$ path contributes $\binom l2$. A mixed $AB$ path has $4n+\bigO(1)$
unlabelled source realizations and $4n+\bigO(1)$ target realizations, and
contributes $4kl$. Triangles are incompatible by
\cref{spf:cg:lem:coreedges}, so no further deficit-one term is missing.
Combining with \eqref{spf:cg:eq:matchingfirst} gives
\begin{equation}
 M_{k,l}=\frac{2^m}{k!\,l!}
 \left[1-\frac{Rm+\binom k2+\binom l2}{n}
 +\bigO_{k,l}(n^{-2})\right].
 \label{spf:cg:eq:momentfirst}
\end{equation}
This last display can be read for fixed $k,l$; the uniform resummation
is supplied by \cref{spf:cg:sec:assembly}. Applying
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:poissonsum} gives \eqref{spf:cg:eq:B1}.

\subsection{An exact cross moment}
For $R=2,3$ and $n\ge4$, the number of incident source $A,B$ edge pairs is
\begin{equation}
 \nu_{\mathrm{inc}}=4n-4R-2.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:incidence}
\end{equation}
This follows by summing the four possible placements of a short edge at
the endpoints of a long edge, with the boundary losses, or by a direct
intersection count. The number of disjoint pairs is
$\nu_{\mathrm{dis}}=(n-1)(n-R)-\nu_{\mathrm{inc}}$. An incident mixed path
has automorphism count one and $\nu_{\mathrm{inc}}$ source and target
realizations. A disjoint pair has automorphism count four and
$\nu_{\mathrm{dis}}$ realizations on each side. Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \E[X_nY_n]=\frac{\nu_{\mathrm{inc}}^2}{\fall n3}+\frac{4\nu_{\mathrm{dis}}^2}{\fall n4}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:EXY}
\end{equation}
For the camel, $\nu_{\mathrm{inc}}=4n-14$, $\nu_{\mathrm{dis}}=n^2-8n+17$,
and for $n\ge4$ this becomes
\begin{equation}
 \E[X_nY_n]=
 \frac{4(n^4-12n^3+58n^2-139n+142)}{\fall n4}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:EXYcamel}
\end{equation}
Also $\E X_n=\E Y_n=2-6/n$, so
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 \operatorname{Cov}(X_n,Y_n)
 &=\frac{4(2n^3-13n^2+7n+54)}{n^2(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)}\\
 &=\frac8{n^2}-\frac4{n^3}+\bigO(n^{-4}).
 \end{split}
 \label{spf:cg:eq:covariance}
\end{equation}
The first-order expansion has no mixed term, yet the variables are not
independent. Their covariance appears at second order.

\subsection{The complete second polynomial}
The degree bound $\deg\mathfrak B_2\le4$ reduces the computation to selected
graphs with one through four edges. The exact catalogue has $83$
source-realizable coloured types for $R=3$ and $87$ for $R=2$, including types
whose target count is zero. To generate it without choosing a large arbitrary
$n$, grow every connected selected edge set by adjoining an edge incident to
its support, normalize its minimum coordinate to zero, and stop at four edges.
Every connected graph has a connected edge ordering obtained from a spanning
tree followed by its remaining edges, so this process is complete.
Canonicalize the connected types and take all multisets with total edge
count at most four. Disjoint translations realize every such multiset in
a sufficiently large interval.

For each type compute $N_S,N_T$ by \eqref{spf:cg:eq:mobius}. If $v=|V(H)|$,
expand its denominator by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{n^v}{\fall nv}=1+\frac{\sigma_1(v)}n+
 \frac{\sigma_1(v)^2+\sigma_2(v)}{2n^2}+\bigO(n^{-3}),
 \label{spf:cg:eq:den2}
\end{equation}
where $\sigma_1(v)=v(v-1)/2$ and $\sigma_2(v)=v(v-1)(2v-1)/6$ (Report~232:
$S_1$, $S_2$). Multiply
$\operatorname{aut}(H)N_S(H;n)N_T(H;n)n^{-v}$ by this expression, retain
orders through two, attach the colour monomial, and sum. Multiplication by
the marked Taylor polynomial of $e^{-2u-2v}$ through degree four gives
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:B2camel} and \eqref{spf:cg:eq:B2knight}. This is a finite
rational calculation whose full graph-by-graph data are shipped in
\path{data/07-coloured-gaps-fixtures-exact.json}.

As compact checks on the coefficient extraction, \cref{spf:cg:tab:B2} lists
every nonzero orbit of marked monomials under exchanging $u,v$. Its entries
are coefficients of $\mathfrak B_2$.
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Monomial or symmetric pair & Camel $R=3$ & Knight $R=2$\\
\midrule
$u^4,v^4$ &2&2\\
$u^3,v^3$ &14&10\\
$u^2,v^2$ &22&10\\
$u^2v^2$ &8&8\\
$u^2v,uv^2$ &12&4\\
$uv$ &44&24\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The nonzero coefficients of $\mathfrak B_{2,R}(u,v)$, one per orbit
under $u\leftrightarrow v$ (Report~232's table).}
\label{spf:cg:tab:B2}
\end{table}
The constant and pure linear coefficients vanish. Evaluating at
$u=v=-1$ gives $48$ and $28$, respectively. The logarithms of the
avoidance probabilities consequently satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 \log\bigl(A^{\mathrm{cg}}_{1,3}(n)/n!\bigr)&=-4+\frac8n+\frac{16}{n^2}+\bigO(n^{-3}),\\
 \log\bigl(A^{\mathrm{cg}}_{1,2}(n)/n!\bigr)&=-4+\frac4n+\frac{20}{n^2}+\bigO(n^{-3}).
 \end{split}
 \label{spf:cg:eq:logavoid}
\end{equation}

\subsection{The mixed interaction missed by marginal multiplication}
\label{spf:cg:sec:interaction}
Let $G_n(u,0)G_n(0,v)$ be the product of the two marginal marked laws.
Subtract its second correction polynomial from the actual $\mathfrak B_2$.
The results are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 \Delta_3(u,v)&=4u^2v^2+8uv,\\
 \Delta_2(u,v)&=4u^2v^2-4u^2v-4uv^2+8uv.
 \end{split}
 \label{spf:cg:eq:interaction}
\end{equation}
The alternating square in \eqref{spf:cg:eq:squareterm} supplies the
$4u^2v^2$ term. The remaining terms come from mixed component and exclusion
interactions. It would therefore also be wrong to ascribe the whole
second-order discrepancy solely to the square.

For camel marginal avoidance, the first two coefficients are $4$ and $10$.
Their product predicts $10+10+4^2=36$, whereas the actual joint coefficient
is $48$, a difference of $12$. For knight they are $2$ and $2$; their
product predicts $2+2+2^2=8$, whereas the actual coefficient is $28$, a
difference of $20$. These are two instances of one coloured-gap theorem,
with genuinely different mixed corrections. [Note of the batch-101 write:
the marginal pairs $4,10$ and $2,2$ are Part~I's $c^{(2)}_1,c^{(2)}_2$ at
offsets $(1,3)$ and $(1,2)$ (\cref{spf:cg:rem:marginals}). The write
recomputed $\Delta_3$ and $\Delta_2$ from \eqref{spf:cg:eq:B1},
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:B2camel}, \eqref{spf:cg:eq:B2knight} and
\eqref{spf:eq:B2-absolute}, and the values $48$, $28$, $16$, $20$, by hand.]

\subsection{Independent finite enumeration and the threshold warning}
The checker separately enumerates all permutations for every $n=4,\ldots,9$
and both $R=2,3$. It records the complete distribution of $(X_n,Y_n)$ and
computes every mixed binomial moment through degree four. A different
computation enumerates selected source-edge subsets and uses
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:unlabelled} with actual finite-$n$ subgraph counts. All
these moments agree. This test uses no eventual polynomial below its
threshold. At $n=9$ the avoidance counts are $19480$ for knight and $22854$
for camel.

The camel fourth falling-factorial moment illustrates why this distinction
is necessary. At $n=9$ direct enumeration gives
\begin{equation}
 9!\,\E\fall{(X_9+Y_9)}4=12829728,
 \label{spf:cg:eq:counterexample}
\end{equation}
while evaluating the eventual graph-polynomial expression at $9$ gives
$13044000$, a discrepancy of $-214272$. Its safe validity threshold is
$n\ge12$, so this is neither a contradiction nor a negligible rounding
error. It is a concrete counterexample to an all-$n$ reading of that
certificate. Knight's fourth-moment certificate is already valid at $9$,
which exceeds its safe threshold $8$.

The source and target embedding polynomials are also checked by direct
selected-edge enumeration at their safe threshold, the next integer, and
$n=16$. At the threshold some types can have zero realizations; at $16$
every catalogue type occurs. The independent packing tests compare the
occupancy scan with direct enumeration of disjoint translated placements
for two dictionaries, $n=0,\ldots,9$, and all terms through four polymers.
The dictionaries include equal supports with distinct species, distinct
species of a common type, interlacing supports, and right-boundary cases.
These finite checks target the proof mechanisms but do not replace their
general proofs.

\section{Controlled finite-model inverses for the coloured-gap counts (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:coloured-inverses}

This section is a consequence of the all-fixed-order coloured-gap expansion.
