It does not posit a differentiable interpolation of the counting sequence.
All comparisons with that sequence are made at integer arguments.
The Lambert $W$ function and Stirling--Newton inversion are standard tools;
no priority claim for those methods, or global historical priority for this
specialization, is intended. [Note of the batch-101 write: the apparatus is
Part~I's (\cref{spf:sec:inverse}, Remark~\ref{spf:rem:inverse-apparatus}), an
instance of the transseries volume's gamma-carrier inversion and staircase
theorem~\cite{tai}; the coordinates below are Part~I's with $\log y$ shifted
by $4$ (\cref{spf:cg:tab:dictionary}).]

\subsection{Input, logarithmic coefficients, and eventual monotonicity}

Let $A(n)$ denote either $\mathrm{A189358}(n)$ (camel) or
$\mathrm{A137774}(n)$ (knight). The previously proved expansion says that for
every fixed $K\geq0$,
\begin{equation}
 A(n)=e^{-4}\Gamma(n+1)
 \left(C_K(1/n)+\bigO_K(n^{-K-1})\right),\qquad
 C_K(t)=1+\sum_{j=1}^Kc_jt^j.
 \label{spf:cg:inv:input}
\end{equation}
The coefficients are consistent as $K$ varies and are computable by the
finite coloured-core construction. In particular,
\[
 (c_1,c_2)=
 \begin{cases}(8,48),&\text{camel},\\(4,28),&\text{knight}.
 \end{cases}
\]
The logarithmic coefficients $\ell_j$ are determined, as in Part~I's
\eqref{spf:eq:log-recursion}, by
\[
 \log\left(1+\sum_{j\geq1}c_jt^j\right)=\sum_{j\geq1}\ell_jt^j,
 \qquad
 \ell_j=c_j-\frac1j\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}i\,\ell_i\,c_{j-i}.
\]
Thus $(\ell_1,\ell_2)=(8,16)$ for camel and $(4,20)$ for knight.
Since $A(n)/n!\to e^{-4}>0$, the counts are positive eventually, tend to
infinity, and
\[
 \frac{A(n+1)}{A(n)}=(n+1)
 \frac{A(n+1)/(n+1)!}{A(n)/n!}\sim n+1.
\]
Consequently $A(n)$ is strictly increasing eventually. This proof needs no
interpolation or differentiation. The second-order expansion also gives
\[
 \frac{A(n+1)}{A(n)}=n+1-\frac{c_1}{n}+\bigO(n^{-2}).
\]
Indeed the difference of the two logarithmic expansions is
$-c_1n^{-2}+\bigO(n^{-3})$; an uncontrolled derivative of a remainder has not
been taken.

Define the upper threshold by
\[
 N(y)=\min\{n\geq0:A(n)\geq y\}.
\]
For large $y$, this threshold lies in the strictly increasing tail. Any
finitely many exceptional initial terms are excluded by taking $y$ above
their maximum. Equalities at $A(n)=y$ are included in this definition.

\subsection{Finite smooth model and a threshold-envelope theorem}

For a fixed $K$, set $p=K+1$ and define, on a sufficiently large real
half-line,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal F_K(x)=\log\Gamma(x+1)-4+\log C_K(1/x).
 \label{spf:cg:inv:finite-model}
\end{equation}
Here $C_K(1/x)>0$ eventually because it tends to $1$. The logarithm is the
real logarithm. Direct differentiation of this explicitly given function
and standard gamma-function estimates give
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal F_K'(x)&=\psi(x+1)-\frac{C_K'(1/x)}{x^2C_K(1/x)}
             =\log x+\bigO(x^{-1}),\\
 \mathcal F_K''(x)&=\psi'(x+1)+\frac{2C_K'(1/x)}{x^3C_K(1/x)}
 +\frac{C_K''(1/x)}{x^4C_K(1/x)}
 -\frac{C_K'(1/x)^2}{x^4C_K(1/x)^2}
             =x^{-1}+\bigO(x^{-2}).
\end{align*}
In particular, $\mathcal F_K$ is eventually increasing and convex, tends to
infinity, and has a unique inverse $\xi_K(y)$ there, defined by
$\mathcal F_K(\xi_K(y))=\log y$.

\begin{theorem}[Threshold envelope; Report~232]
\label[theorem]{spf:cg:thm:envelope}
There exist $C_K>0$ and $n_K$ such that
\begin{equation}
 |\log A(n)-\mathcal F_K(n)|\leq C_K n^{-p}\qquad(n\geq n_K).
 \label{spf:cg:inv:integer-log-error}
\end{equation}
After enlarging the real lower endpoint if needed, the functions
$\mathcal F_K(x)+C_Kx^{-p}$ and $\mathcal F_K(x)-C_Kx^{-p}$ are both strictly
increasing. Let $\xi_K^-(y)$ and $\xi_K^+(y)$ be their respective inverse
roots:
\[
 \mathcal F_K(\xi_K^-)+C_K(\xi_K^-)^{-p}=\log y,
 \qquad
 \mathcal F_K(\xi_K^+)-C_K(\xi_K^+)^{-p}=\log y.
\]
Then, for all sufficiently large $y$,
\begin{equation}
 \lceil \xi_K^-(y)\rceil\leq N(y)\leq\lceil \xi_K^+(y)\rceil.
 \label{spf:cg:inv:threshold-exact-envelope}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 \xi_K^{\pm}(y)=\xi_K(y)\pm
 \frac{C_K\xi_K(y)^{-p}}{\mathcal F_K'(\xi_K(y))}\,(1+\smallo(1)).
 \label{spf:cg:inv:root-displacements}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for some $D_K>0$ and every sufficiently large $y$,
\begin{equation}
 \left\lceil \xi_K(y)-D_K\frac{\xi_K(y)^{-p}}{\log \xi_K(y)}\right\rceil
 \leq N(y)\leq
 \left\lceil \xi_K(y)+D_K\frac{\xi_K(y)^{-p}}{\log \xi_K(y)}\right\rceil.
 \label{spf:cg:inv:threshold-simple-envelope}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since $C_K(1/n)\to1$, taking the logarithm in \eqref{spf:cg:inv:input} proves
\eqref{spf:cg:inv:integer-log-error}. The envelope derivatives differ from
$\mathcal F_K'$ by only $\bigO(x^{-p-1})$, so both envelopes eventually
increase. If an integer $n<\lceil\xi_K^-\rceil$, then $n<\xi_K^-$ and
$\log A(n)\leq\mathcal F_K(n)+C_Kn^{-p}<\log y$, whenever $n$ is in the tail.
The finitely many preceding indices are excluded by the choice of $y$.
At $m=\lceil\xi_K^+\rceil$, instead,
$\log A(m)\geq\mathcal F_K(m)-C_Km^{-p}\geq\log y$.
These facts prove \eqref{spf:cg:inv:threshold-exact-envelope}, including its
endpoint convention. The mean-value theorem, $\mathcal F_K'\sim\log x$, and
then local comparison with $\xi_K$ give \eqref{spf:cg:inv:root-displacements}
and \eqref{spf:cg:inv:threshold-simple-envelope}. For example, evaluating
either envelope at $\xi_K\pm A'\xi_K^{-p}/\log\xi_K$, with $A'>C_K$ fixed,
first traps its root in that interval and then permits the stated asymptotic
evaluation.
\end{proof}
[Note of the batch-101 write: \eqref{spf:cg:inv:threshold-simple-envelope}
is the bracket of Part~VI's \cref{spf:fd:thm:bracket} for these counts, and
\eqref{spf:cg:inv:threshold-exact-envelope} its sharper form with explicit
envelope roots; both are instances of \texttt{p0:thm:staircase}~\cite{tai}.
In the lower-bound step, ``$n$ in the tail'' must include that the envelope
$\mathcal F_K+C_Kx^{-p}$ is increasing on $[n,\xi_K^-]$, which the choice of
the lower endpoint provides.]

The constants and sequence onset in this theorem are not numerically
certified by the present argument. The all-order proof supplies existence
of remainder bounds; this section has not evaluated those constants or the
associated onset. Thus these are eventual rigorous envelopes, not a claim
that a particular finite $y$ has certified rounding merely because it is
large in a numerical experiment. This is an absence of an explicit onset
certificate, not a claim that effective constants cannot in principle be
extracted from the finite proof.

\subsection{Lambert--Newton inversion to any fixed order}

Put
\begin{equation}
 \tilde L=\log y+4,\qquad
 \tilde z=\frac{\tilde L}{W_0(\tilde L/e)},\qquad \tilde w=\log\tilde z.
 \label{spf:cg:inv:W-seed}
\end{equation}
For large $y$, $\tilde L>0$ and $W_0$ is the positive real branch. Its
defining identity gives the exact equation $\tilde z(\log\tilde z-1)=\tilde L$.
Stirling's formula gives
\[
 \mathcal F_K(\tilde z)-\log y=\tfrac12\log(2\pi\tilde z)+\bigO(\tilde z^{-1}),
 \qquad \xi_K(y)=\tilde z-\tfrac12+\bigO(\tilde w^{-1}).
\]
In particular, $\xi_K(y)=\tilde z+\bigO(1)$, and eventually
$\tilde z-1<\xi_K(y)<\tilde z$. Define the fully specified finite iteration
\begin{equation}
 x_0=\tilde z,\qquad
 x_{j+1}=x_j-
 \frac{\log\Gamma(x_j+1)-4+\log C_K(1/x_j)-\log y}
 {\psi(x_j+1)-C_K'(1/x_j)/(x_j^2C_K(1/x_j))}.
 \label{spf:cg:inv:Newton}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Newton iteration; Report~232]
\label[proposition]{spf:cg:prop:newton}
For every fixed number $m$ of iterations,
\begin{equation}
 0\leq x_m-\xi_K(y)
 =\bigO_{K,m}\left((\tilde z\tilde w)^{-(2^m-1)}\right).
 \label{spf:cg:inv:Newton-error}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For all large $\tilde z$, the model is increasing and convex on
$[\tilde z-1,\tilde z]$, with $\mathcal F_K'\geq\tilde w/2$ and
$0<\mathcal F_K''\leq2/\tilde z$ there. Convexity implies
$\xi_K\leq x_{j+1}\leq x_j\leq\tilde z$ for a Newton step starting above the
root. Taylor's theorem at $x_j$, with a point $\eta_j$ between $\xi_K$ and
$x_j$, then gives
\[
 x_{j+1}-\xi_K=
 \frac{\mathcal F_K''(\eta_j)}{2\mathcal F_K'(x_j)}(x_j-\xi_K)^2
 \leq \frac{2}{\tilde z\tilde w}(x_j-\xi_K)^2.
\]
The initial error is at most $1$, so induction even yields
\begin{equation}
 0\leq x_m-\xi_K(y)\leq
 \left(\frac{2}{\tilde z\tilde w}\right)^{2^m-1}
 \label{spf:cg:inv:Newton-explicit-bound}
\end{equation}
whenever the stated interval bounds hold. This proves
\eqref{spf:cg:inv:Newton-error}.
\end{proof}

Taking
\[
 m\geq\left\lceil\log_2(K+2)\right\rceil
\]
makes the Newton error $\bigO_K(\tilde z^{-K-1}/\log\tilde z)$, no larger in
asymptotic order than the counting-expansion uncertainty. Thus a finite
number of standard special-function evaluations gives an all-fixed-order
inverse centre. Combining \eqref{spf:cg:inv:threshold-simple-envelope} with the
Newton estimate, for a suitable $E_K>0$ one obtains
\begin{equation}
 \left\lceil x_m-E_K\frac{\tilde z^{-K-1}}{\log\tilde z}\right\rceil
 \leq N(y)\leq
 \left\lceil x_m+E_K\frac{\tilde z^{-K-1}}{\log\tilde z}\right\rceil
 \label{spf:cg:inv:Newton-threshold}
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $y$. If fewer iterations are used, add
$\bigO_{K,m}((\tilde z\log\tilde z)^{-(2^m-1)})$ to the envelope radius.

\subsection{An effective Newton certificate for the displayed quadratic models}

\begin{proposition}[Effective model bounds; Report~232]
\label[proposition]{spf:cg:prop:effective}
For $C_2(t)=1+8t+48t^2$ or $C_2(t)=1+4t+28t^2$, all the interval conditions
in the proof of \cref{spf:cg:prop:newton} hold for every real
$\tilde z\geq32$. Consequently \eqref{spf:cg:inv:Newton-explicit-bound} is
then an effective bound for the exact finite-model root. The same bounds
cover the truncations $C_0$ and $C_1$ of these two polynomials.
\end{proposition}
This certifies the finite models themselves, separately from the
unevaluated counting remainder.
\begin{proof}
Write $C_2(1/x)=q(x)=1+a/x+b/x^2$ with $0\leq a\leq8$, $0\leq b\leq48$.
For $x\geq31$, the standard positive real Stirling and digamma bounds give
\[
 0<\log\Gamma(x+1)-x\log x+x-\tfrac12\log(2\pi x)<\frac1{12x},
 \qquad \psi(x+1)\geq\log x.
\]
Also $0<\psi'(x+1)=\sum_{j\geq1}(x+j)^{-2}\leq1/x$.
The numerator of $(\log q)''$ is
\[
 q''q-(q')^2=2ax^{-3}+(a^2+6b)x^{-4}+4abx^{-5}+2b^2x^{-6}\geq0.
\]
It follows that on $[\tilde z-1,\tilde z]$,
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal F_2'(x)&\geq\log x-8x^{-2}-96x^{-3}\geq\tilde w/2,\\
 0<\mathcal F_2''(x)&\leq x^{-1}+16x^{-3}+288x^{-4}\leq2/\tilde z.
\end{align*}
The last inequalities follow by replacing $x$ by $\tilde z-1\geq31$ and using
$\tilde z/(\tilde z-1)\leq32/31$; all remaining rational errors decrease with
$\tilde z$. At $x=\tilde z$, $\mathcal F_2(\tilde z)-\log y>0$. With
$x_-=\tilde z-1\geq31$, the opposite endpoint satisfies
\[
 \mathcal F_2(x_-)-\log y
 \leq-\tfrac12\log\frac{x_-}{2\pi}+\frac{97}{12x_-}+\frac{48}{x_-^2}<0.
\]
The right side decreases with $x_-$ and is negative at $31$: indeed
$31/(2\pi)>4>e$, and $97/(12\cdot31)+48/31^2<1/2$.
This proves the bracket and all claimed effective model bounds.
No bound on the actual counting error has been inserted into this argument.
\end{proof}
[Note of the batch-101 write: the intake checked the two endpoint estimates
and the derivative bounds by hand. In the first displayed bound,
$C_2'(1/x)/(x^2C_2(1/x))\le(a+2b/x)x^{-2}\le8x^{-2}+96x^{-3}$; in the second,
the three correction terms of $\mathcal F_2''$ are bounded by
$2(a+2b/x)x^{-3}+2bx^{-4}$, since $q\ge1$ and the last term is negative.]

\subsection{Explicit Lambert corrections}

For the second-order models define
\[
 \tilde t=\frac{\log(2\pi\tilde z)}{2\tilde w},\qquad
 a_1=c_1+\frac1{12},\qquad a_2=c_2-\frac{c_1^2}{2},\qquad
 \tilde B=a_1+\frac{\tilde t^2-\tilde t}{2}.
\]
[Note of the batch-101 write: $a_1$ and $a_2$ are Part~I's logarithmic
coefficients \eqref{spf:eq:a-coeff} for these counts (Report~232: $b_1$,
$b_2$).]

\begin{proposition}[Second-order inverse; Report~232]
\label[proposition]{spf:cg:prop:second-inverse}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\begin{aligned}
 \xi_2(y)={}&\tilde z-\tilde t-\frac{\tilde B}{\tilde z\tilde w}\\
 &-\frac{a_2+a_1\tilde t+\tilde t^3/6-\tilde t^2/4+(\tilde t-1/2)\tilde B/\tilde w}{\tilde z^2\tilde w}
 +\bigO\left(\frac{\tilde z^{-3}}{\tilde w}\right).
 \end{aligned}}
 \label{spf:cg:inv:explicit-second-inverse}
\end{equation}
The parameters are
\[
 (a_1,a_2)=
 \begin{cases}(97/12,16),&\text{camel},\\(49/12,20),&\text{knight}.
 \end{cases}
\]
\end{proposition}
In particular the familiar common correction
$-\tilde t=-1/2-\log(2\pi)/(2\log\tilde z)$ precedes the coloured-gap
corrections. For the actual integer threshold, replace $\xi_2$ in
\eqref{spf:cg:inv:threshold-simple-envelope} by the displayed expression
without its remainder and enlarge the envelope constant. Two Newton steps
from $\tilde z$ give another centre with the same $\bigO(\tilde z^{-3}/\tilde w)$
counting precision.

\begin{proof}[Derivation]
Set $f(x)=x\log x-x$. Stirling and the explicit logarithm of $C_2$ give
\[
 \mathcal F_2(x)+4=f(x)+\tfrac12\log(2\pi x)+a_1x^{-1}+a_2x^{-2}+\bigO(x^{-3}).
\]
Substitute $x=\tilde z+D$ and $D=d_0+d_1/\tilde z+d_2/\tilde z^2$ into
$\mathcal F_2(x)=\log y$, using $f(\tilde z)=\tilde L$. The coefficients of
$1,\tilde z^{-1},\tilde z^{-2}$ are respectively
\begin{align*}
 &\tilde wd_0+\tilde w\tilde t,\\
 &\tilde wd_1+(d_0^2+d_0)/2+a_1,\\
 &\tilde wd_2+(d_0+1/2)d_1-d_0^3/6-d_0^2/4-a_1d_0+a_2.
\end{align*}
Their vanishing yields $d_0=-\tilde t$, $d_1=-\tilde B/\tilde w$, and the
displayed $d_2$. The substitution residual is $\bigO(\tilde z^{-3})$,
uniformly for the actual $\tilde t=1/2+\log(2\pi)/(2\tilde w)$, whose value
is bounded. Since $\mathcal F_2'\asymp\tilde w$, the mean-value theorem turns
that residual into $\bigO(\tilde z^{-3}/\tilde w)$ root error.
\end{proof}

The same calculation provides an explicit recurrence at every fixed order.
Let $a_j$ be Part~I's coefficients \eqref{spf:eq:a-coeff} for these counts,
$a_j=\ell_j+s_j$ with $s_{2r-1}=\mathcal B_{2r}/(2r(2r-1))$, $s_{2r}=0$ and
$\mathcal B_{2r}$ a Bernoulli number (Report~232: $\beta_j=d_j+s_j$). Use the
formal variable $\varsigma=1/\tilde z$, set $D(\varsigma)=\sum_{j=0}^Kd_j\varsigma^j$,
and cancel successive coefficients through $\varsigma^K$ in
\begin{equation}
 \tilde w(D+\tilde t)+
 \sum_{\rho=2}^{K+1}\frac{(-1)^\rho D^\rho\varsigma^{\rho-1}}{\rho(\rho-1)}
 +\frac12\log(1+\varsigma D)
 +\sum_{j=1}^Ka_j\varsigma^j(1+\varsigma D)^{-j}.
 \label{spf:cg:inv:all-order-formal}
\end{equation}
Terms above degree $K$ are discarded. The coefficient at degree $j$ is
$\tilde wd_j$ plus an expression depending only on earlier $d_i$, so the
recurrence is finite and unique. For $j\geq1$, the resulting $d_j$ are
bounded $\bigO_K(1/\tilde w)$ as $\tilde z\to\infty$, whereas
$d_0=-\tilde t=\bigO(1)$. The same residual argument proves
\[
 \xi_K(y)=\tilde z+\sum_{j=0}^K\frac{d_j(\tilde w)}{\tilde z^j}
           +\bigO_K\left(\frac{\tilde z^{-K-1}}{\tilde w}\right).
\]
When $K=0$, the sum over $\rho$ and the sum over $j$ in
\eqref{spf:cg:inv:all-order-formal} are empty and its constant coefficient still
correctly gives $d_0=-\tilde t$. Only the established $c_j$ through order $K$
are needed. Expanding $\log C_2$ beyond second order does not establish the
third coefficient of the counting sequence. [Note of the batch-101 write:
this is Part~I's recursion \eqref{spf:eq:inverse-recursion} in the shifted
coordinates; there $\kappa_\theta$ carries the constant $\frac12\log(2\pi)$,
here $\tilde t$ does.]

\subsection{Rounding and the distinction between a root and a staircase}

Suppose a centre $\vartheta(y)$ has been proved to satisfy an envelope
\[
 \lceil \vartheta(y)-\varepsilon(y)\rceil\leq N(y)
 \leq\lceil \vartheta(y)+\varepsilon(y)\rceil,
 \qquad \varepsilon(y)\longrightarrow0.
\]
If $\operatorname{dist}(\vartheta(y),\mathbb Z)>\varepsilon(y)$, both ceilings
coincide and $N(y)=\lceil \vartheta(y)\rceil$. If $2\varepsilon(y)<1$, the two
endpoints differ by at most one, so at most two adjacent threshold values
remain. Close to an integer, an exact evaluation of $A(n)$ or an effective
one-sided remainder bound is needed to resolve the ambiguity.
In particular an assertion of unqualified eventual exact rounding of the
centre is not supplied by a big-$\mathrm O$ root error. Nor is
$N(y)=\xi_K(y)+\smallo(1)$ valid uniformly for all large real $y$: the
threshold is an integer-valued staircase. The inequalities above retain the
essential integer convention.

At the special level $y=A(n)$, one does have
$\xi_K(A(n))=n+\bigO_K(n^{-K-1}/\log n)$, by
\eqref{spf:cg:inv:integer-log-error} and the mean-value theorem. This useful
inverse-at-the-data statement should not be confused with a uniform
noninteger interpolation of $N(y)$.

For comparison, the two counting expansions imply
\[
 \frac{A^{\rm camel}(n)}{A^{\rm knight}(n)}
 =1+\frac4n+\frac4{n^2}+\bigO(n^{-3}),
\]
so eventually $A^{\rm camel}(n)>A^{\rm knight}(n)$ and
$A^{\rm camel}(n)<A^{\rm knight}(n+1)$. Thus, at every sufficiently large
level $y$, $N_{\rm camel}(y)\leq N_{\rm knight}(y)\leq N_{\rm camel}(y)+1$.
This also follows directly at integer indices and makes no smoothness
assumption about either sequence.

\paragraph{Source boundary and reproducibility.}
The only sequence-specific input is the independently audited coloured-core
expansion. Standard special-function facts used here are the defining
Lambert identity and the positive-real Stirling/digamma expansions and
bounds; see the DLMF~\cite{dlmfW,dlmfGamma,cgDlmfPsi}. Those sources do not
provide the coloured-gap inverse theorem. The accompanying script verifies
the inverse algebra by exact symbolic arithmetic, checks the finite-model
Newton bound numerically at high precision, and independently enumerates
small avoidance counts. Numerical tests are consistency checks, not a
substitute for the analytic remainder or an effective sequence onset.

\section{Literature comparison and scope of the result (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:literature}

\subsection{Leading limits and earlier corrections}
The leading $e^{-4}$ limits for the two chess-piece permutation counts are
classical. Kot\v e\v sovec's sixth-edition compilation gives the general
finite-leaper leading-limit discussion on printed pages 622--624, tables
including A137774 and A189358 on page 636, probability plots on page 637,
and successive-ratio plots on page 638~\cite{cgKotesovec}. Those passages
support the leading equivalences; they do not display the two correction
polynomials proved here. The book's separate treatment of unrestricted
leaper placements on pages 685--686 concerns a different model from a
permutation with exactly one point in every row and column.

Aspvall and Liang's \emph{Dinner Table Problem}~\cite{cgAspvall} is a
particularly important antecedent. Part~I, printed pages 5--6, gives
higher-order corrections for its original circular adjacency problem,
derived from a recurrence and also accessible from its exact block formula.
Higher-order Poisson corrections for permutation adjacency problems are
therefore not a new general idea. Part~II, printed pages 7--12, proves a
Poisson limit under factorial-moment bounds; its concluding estimate on
page~10 is additive $\bigO(1/n)$. It does not by itself supply the
arbitrary-order compact-complex remainder for the simultaneous swapped-gap
model in \eqref{spf:cg:eq:model}. [Parts~I and~II here are those of
Aspvall and Liang's report, not of this one.]

Mendelsohn's 1956 paper~\cite{fdMendelsohn} develops asymptotic-series
machinery for permutation restrictions, including a relative-position
example. Its operator-recurrence framework in Section~5 requires a
fixed-length constant-coefficient polynomial-in-shift recurrence; the
subsequent coefficient calculation explicitly assumes existence of the
complete asymptotic series. No such recurrence for the present counts is
assumed or established here. The present proof supplies the existence and
uniform remainder directly from finite cores and finite-range packings.
[Note of the batch-101 write: Report~83 reads the same paper the same way;
see \cref{spf:fd:sec:literature}.]

\subsection{Which static-board results apply directly}
Flajolet and Sedgewick's Proposition~V.11~\cite{cgFS}, in the discussion on
pages 369--373, gives a leading Poisson limit for fixed forbidden
exceedances $\pi(i)-i$. The finite occupancy-mask transfer construction on
pages 370--371 is methodological prior art and is credited as such.
Their Note~V.51 discusses succession gaps and broader forbidden gap sets.
The source restrictions in the present problem concern \emph{two}
permutation entries at a time and their difference; they are not fixed
forbidden positions in a static permutation board.

Similarly, Godsil and McKay~\cite{cgGodsil} develop sophisticated sparse
subgraph asymptotics, including small-cycle corrections, for Latin
rectangles using fixed forbidden-position bipartite graphs and rook
polynomials. The conceptual use of small subgraphs is classical. Their
static-board formula cannot simply be applied by calling the present
selected-event graph a forbidden-position board: its edges constrain pairs
of images. The exact two-sided product $i_S(H)i_T(H)$ in
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:identity} records that difference.

For the knight sequence, Bagno, Eisenberg, Reches and Sigron~\cite{cgBagno}
identify the no-separator permutation objects and derive expectation
results. Their Theorem~6.1 gives a generating function for one separator
direction, equivalently one marginal orientation. It does not state the
joint all-orders avoidance expansion of both reciprocal constraints.
General mod-Poisson approximation schemes such as~\cite{cgModPoisson}
provide broader approximation methodology under suitable residue-control
hypotheses. They do not remove the need to prove the fixed-parameter
$n^{-j}$ residue expansion in this particular coloured model.

\subsection{A bounded comparison rather than a priority claim}
The mathematical result established here is the two-sided reciprocal
coloured finite-core expansion, its uniform factorial-scale summation
bound, and the explicit mixed and cycle corrections for the two named
sequences. Graph inclusion--exclusion, partition-lattice inversion,
finite-state transfer matrices, analytic perturbation, and Poisson
correction polynomials are standard tools. They are not claimed as
inventions of this report.

The inspected sources do not give the theorem in the form proved here.
That is a bounded source comparison, not a proof that no equivalent theorem
exists elsewhere. In particular, finding only the leading equivalent on an
OEIS entry does not establish novelty, and renaming a model or changing
one gap would not establish a new theorem. The reason to treat the present
family separately from a one-source/one-target forest calculation is the
simultaneous coloured structure and its compatible cyclic components.
External peer review remains appropriate for any publication claim.
[Note of the batch-101 write: the one-source/one-target forest calculation
is Parts~I--IV of this report, whose marginal statements
\cref{spf:cg:thm:main} reproduces (\cref{spf:cg:rem:marginals}).]

\section{Reproducibility and certificate design (Report 232)}
\label{spf:cg:sec:repro}

\subsection{Contents and exact arithmetic}
The companion archive is a complete source package. Its principal files are
\begin{itemize}
 \item \texttt{report.tex}, the single-file editable manuscript source;
 \item \texttt{certificates.py}, the standard-library exact graph, moment,
       catalogue, and packing checks;
 \item \texttt{inverse\_checks.py}, exact inverse algebra and clearly labelled
       high-precision finite-model diagnostics;
 \item \texttt{build.py}, the deterministic full builder with guarded output;
 \item \texttt{guard\_tests.py}, adversarial output-path and mutation checks;
 \item \texttt{fixtures/exact.json} and \texttt{fixtures/inverse.json}, the
       full expected outputs, regenerated and byte-compared during a build;
 \item \texttt{Report232.pdf}, the compiled report, and a digest manifest.
\end{itemize}
[Note of the batch-101 write: here the four programs are shipped in
\path{code/} and the two fixtures in \path{data/}, each with the prefix
\path{07-coloured-gaps-} (the fixtures with \path{fixtures-} after the
prefix); \texttt{report.tex}, the
PDF, the delivered README and the digest manifest are not shipped. The
report README says how to rerun the programs.] The graph certificates use ascending arrays of rational
coefficients, encoded as integers or strings such as \texttt{5/2}; there is
no expression parser or arbitrary evaluation of fixture contents. Graph
labels in the machine files use colours $0,1$ for $A,B$. Every graph row
includes the canonical component list, automorphism order, and source and
target unlabelled embedding polynomials. The safe polynomial threshold is
stored at model level and stated in this report.

The checker reconstructs the catalogue and every polynomial from scratch,
then verifies the full $\mathfrak B_1,\mathfrak B_2$, the independent
permutation distributions, and the counterexample
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:counterexample}. Stored fixtures are expected outputs, not
computational inputs to the mathematical routines. All graph and packing
arithmetic is exact. The inverse checker uses exact symbolic arithmetic for
coefficient identities and separately uses 100-digit floating arithmetic for
model roots. Its freely chosen higher-order method fixture exercises the
formal inverse recurrence; it does not assert any uncomputed third or fourth
coefficient of the counting sequence.

\subsection{Running the build}
The full build requires Python 3.11 or later, SymPy, mpmath, pdfLaTeX, and
the ordinary LaTeX packages listed at the start of \texttt{report.tex}.
No network access is used. From an extracted source package, run
\begin{quote}
\verb|python3 -B build.py --output /absolute/path/to/new-directory|
\end{quote}
The output directory must not exist, even as a dangling symlink. The builder
rejects targets inside the source tree, targets whose parents cross a
symlink, and existing targets. It uses exclusive creation after validation,
so it never silently reuses a previous output directory. It snapshots the
complete regular-file inventory and verifies the files' bytes after the
build. Intermediate TeX files and regenerated fixtures are written in a
separate temporary workspace. The output directory contains the PDF, the
source ZIP, the flattened manuscript, the two regenerated fixtures, and an
external build receipt.

All Python guard checks are explicit exceptions rather than
\texttt{assert} statements, so optimization does not disable them. Every
child Python invocation includes \texttt{-B}, and optimized parent runs
also pass \texttt{-O}. The decimal integer-string limit is set to 640.
No bytecode cache is written. The package uses fixed ZIP timestamps and
member order, fixed TeX metadata, and no volatile build-time text in the
PDF. The build receipt records the environment separately, so it does not
change the mathematical artifacts. [Note of the batch-101 write: the build
needs the delivered layout (\texttt{report.tex}, \texttt{README.md},
\texttt{SHA256SUMS} and \texttt{fixtures/} beside the scripts) and so runs
only from a re-extraction of the archive in its arrival commit; the report
README says so.]

\subsection{What a successful replay establishes}
A successful full build regenerates exact and inverse fixtures, compares
them with the packaged fixtures, compiles the manuscript three times, checks
for TeX errors and overfull boxes, and creates a deterministic archive. The
normal and optimized builds must agree byte-for-byte on the PDF, ZIP, TeX,
and fixture files. The release procedure tests this from two fresh
extractions of the \emph{actual produced ZIP}, rather than merely rerunning
a working directory. The complete regular-file inventory of each extraction
is hashed before and after, and those files must remain unchanged. The
supplied guard tests challenge existing files and directories, a dangling
symlink, a symlinked parent, a path inside the source tree, and tampered or
missing source manifests.

The proof establishes the asymptotic theorem. These checks establish that
the packaged finite calculations and build are replayable and that common
boundary and normalization mistakes are caught. Neither a small-$n$
comparison nor a high-precision model inverse is an effective certificate
for the unknown onset of the sequence's asymptotic remainder. The package
contains no third-party paper PDFs and no private review materials.

\section{Further questions and research}
\label{spf:cg:sec:questions}

[Added 5 October 2026, batch 101.] This section keeps Report~232's six
questions, under its title ``Further questions''. They are the unproved
claims of Report~232 that this write found: Report~232 asserts the existence
of effective constants (question~1), of higher coefficients (question~2) and
of extensions (questions~3--6) without proving them, and the write found no
demonstrably wrong claim. Its two steps argued at sketch level, the count of
anchored shapes in \cref{spf:cg:lem:highdeficit} and the rationality step of
\cref{spf:cg:thm:packing}, are completed in
\cref{spf:cg:rem:shapes,spf:cg:rem:rational}; its slip in the H\"older step
of \cref{spf:cg:lem:tail} is corrected in place with a note. Notes of the
write after the list say what each continues.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Q\arabic*.},leftmargin=*]
 \item \textbf{Effective constants.} The proof supplies finite constructions
 and eventual bounds. Extracting usable explicit constants and onsets for
 the complex-mark remainder would turn the threshold envelopes into
 practical certified finite-level inverse algorithms. The effective
 $\tilde z\ge32$ Newton result concerns only the displayed smooth model.
 \item \textbf{Higher coefficients.} The moment rule reduces order $j$ to
 graphs with at most $2j$ selected edges. Efficient canonicalization,
 quotient caching, and sparse transfer operators could make orders three
 and above accessible. Their existence is proved here, but no numerical
 values for them are claimed.
 \item \textbf{More colours and general gap sets.} The positive tail method
 and finite dictionaries suggest extensions to arbitrary fixed finite gap
 correspondences. Compatible triangles and denser cores change the
 deficit-to-edge relation and hence the marked-degree bound. Each such
 extension needs a stated structural inequality and its own proof.
 \item \textbf{Growing offsets.} All constants here depend on fixed gaps.
 If $r$ or $s$ grows with $n$, dictionary sizes, spans, and analytic
 neighbourhoods change. The present compact-uniform theorem does not
 automatically control that regime.
 \item \textbf{Alternative exact recurrences.} A finite-state recurrence
 for the full permutation counts, rather than the fixed-core packing
 polynomials, might yield efficient numerical counts or independent
 remainder estimates. No recurrence is inferred from the finite fixtures.
 \item \textbf{Distributional refinements.} Uniformity on complex compact
 marking sets gives access to fixed mixed derivatives and correction
 measures. Quantitative norms for the whole joint distribution require
 stating and proving an appropriate inversion estimate, rather than
 reading a total-variation rate off a formal generating series.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Notes of the batch-101 write on these questions.}
Question~1 is Part~VI's question~1 for these counts. Question~2: at order
three \cref{spf:cg:rem:cycles} already says which cyclic cores enter
(coloured cycles and other cores of deficit three); the intake's
Richardson extrapolation of the knight b-file (OEIS data, $n\le30$) gives
$27.2$, $28.24$, $28.13$ for the second coefficient at orders 3--5, and of
the camel b-file ($n\le24$) $49.1$, $46.6$, $50.0$ at orders 2--4, consistent
with $28$ and $48$; this is evidence for \cref{spf:cg:thm:two}, not a third
coefficient. Question~3 continues Part~I's question~6, Part~II's question~4
and Part~III's question~10, and with it \cref{spf:mg:q:several}, all of which
Part~VII answers for reciprocal pairs only (the dated notes there say so):
still open are two pairs sharing one gap (Part~II's ``first target''), the
directed two-gap model (Part~III), more than two colours, and moving gaps.
Question~4 is Part~I's question~5 and Part~IV's \cref{spf:mg:cor:moving} for
two colours; Part~IV's local-density method is the natural starting point.
Question~5 is the two-gap analogue of Part~I's question~2. Question~6 is the
two-mark analogue of Corollary~\ref{spf:cor:distribution} and
\cref{spf:mg:cor:law}, which Part~VII does not prove. Cyclic wraparound of
positions or values, the other half of Part~I's question~6, is not treated
by Report~232. Nothing in Part~VII is formalized.

\section{A worked partition-lattice certificate (Report 232, Appendix A)}
\label{spf:cg:app:worked}

Consider two disjoint $A$ edges in a single-length graph with gap $d$.
There are $n-d$ total edges. Unordered distinct pairs number
$\binom{n-d}{2}$. Incident pairs occur along each length-$d$ path and total
$\max(0,n-2d)$. For $n\ge2d$ the exact matching polynomial is therefore
\begin{equation}
 N_{2A}(n)=\binom{n-d}{2}-(n-2d)
 =\frac{n^2}{2}-\frac{2d+3}{2}n+\frac{d^2+5d}{2}.
 \label{spf:cg:eq:twoedges}
\end{equation}
Its abstract graph has automorphism order $2^2\cdot2!=8$. For the camel,
the source uses $d=1$ and target $d=3$, giving
\[
 N_S=\frac{n^2-5n+6}{2},\qquad
 N_T=\frac{n^2-9n+24}{2}.
\]
Its exact graph term is $8N_SN_Tu^2/\fall n4$ where these polynomials are
valid. The partition-lattice algorithm obtains the same expressions by
summing quotient homomorphisms with their M\"obius factors, and does so for
all graph types rather than requiring a separate geometric formula for each.
For a mixed incident path the automorphism order is one, the source and
target polynomial are both $4n-4R-2$, and its term is the first summand in
\eqref{spf:cg:eq:EXY}. For a mixed disjoint pair the automorphism order is
four, recovering the second summand. These small examples fix the
normalization conventions of the machine-readable certificates.

\section{Proof dependencies and limitations (Report 232, Appendix B)}
\label{spf:cg:app:dependencies}

The main theorem rests on four independently stated ingredients:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item the exact two-sided injection identity, including cyclic graphs;
 \item a uniform exponential-moment tail for the number of selected edges;
 \item a positive geometric bound for high-deficit cores;
 \item the factorial-scale packing expansion for the finite low-deficit
       dictionaries and its source--target normalization.
\end{enumerate}
The degree bound is proved only after those ingredients are combined.
It is what makes the finite four-edge computation conclusive for
$\mathfrak B_2$. The inverse section then uses the resulting integer
asymptotic expansion, standard gamma-function estimates, and monotonicity of
an explicitly defined finite model. It never differentiates an unknown
sequence remainder.

The reported computations establish exact rational identities and finite
checks at stated ranges. They do not certify an effective counting-sequence
onset, convergence of an infinite correction series, universal exact
rounding of a smooth inverse, or worldwide historical priority. These
limitations leave the proved compact-complex all-fixed-order expansion and
the displayed mixed corrections intact.

\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendices}

\appendix
\section{Proof dependencies and archive guide}

The main theorem uses the following chain, all proved in the body of the
article:
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \text{selected-edge tilings}
 &\Longrightarrow \text{stable polynomial moments}\\
 &\Longrightarrow \text{uniform profile expansion}\\
 &\Longrightarrow \text{finite coefficient formula}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The separate factorial-moment bound justifies discarding the tail and also
proves the total-variation universality theorem. Stirling's expansion and the
definition of Lambert $W$ are standard external analytic inputs for the inverse
section. The recurrence conjectures, the rational-collapse conjecture, and the
2026 holonomicity report are not dependencies of the all-orders theorem.

The archive contains the \LaTeX{} source and compiled PDF, the core exact
coefficient/enumeration library, a validation program, the rational-certificate
program, two optional exploratory programs, and their outputs. The README gives
commands to reproduce the computations and compile the article. The symbolic
collapse script asserts only its explicitly bounded range of identities; its
success is not presented as an infinite proof.
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 73O2: in the shipped report the compiled PDF is
a new build of this text, and the archive's README is replaced by the report
README; \cref{spf:app:provenance} lists what is shipped.]

\section{Provenance and editorial notes}
\label{spf:app:provenance}

[Added 1 October 2026, batch 73O2.]

\paragraph{Source.}
The report was built from one manuscript, manuscript~60 of batch~73 of
ProveIt's incoming-reports intake: the archive \texttt{oeis\_path\_forests.zip}
(main file \texttt{article.tex}, a 21-page PDF titled \emph{Unconditional
all-orders asymptotics for two OEIS permutation sequences}). It arrived in

deleted the archive from \texttt{docs/incoming}; it survives in
\texttt{aa43cc555}. The manuscript pins no repository revision. It cites the
repository only by the path of the Fabius completion audit~\cite{proveit},
which it uses as an editorial example and calls ``not a dependency''. This
report does not continue that audit, and nothing was filed in the Fabius tree.
[Added 1 October 2026, batch 74: since batch~74 the report is built from
two manuscripts; Part~II and the paragraph ``Part~II (batch~74)'' below
describe the second.]

\paragraph{What the write changed.}
\begin{itemize}
\item Every label gained the prefix \texttt{spf:}; no label was renamed
 otherwise and none was removed. The manuscript had 78 labels.
\item Added: the editorial note after the status paragraph;
 Remark~\ref{spf:rem:offsets-one-one} on the offsets $(1,1)$ (A000255 and
 A002464); Remark~\ref{spf:rem:inverse-apparatus} on the repository's inversion
 apparatus; bracketed notes on Padhi's preprint, on integer thresholds and on
 the shipped files; three bibliography entries~\cite{oeis000255,oeis002464,tai};
 and this section. A separate reciprocal note, Remark~\ref{spf:rem:clique-analogue},
 points to the local law of the companion report on balanced Smirnov words.
\item Nothing was removed. Every non-claim of the manuscript is kept: the status
 paragraph, the Poincar\'e-only reading of Table~\ref{spf:tab:extra}, the
 limitation after \cref{spf:thm:universality}, the fixed-offset limitation
 after the proof of \cref{spf:thm:main}, the conditional nature of
 Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:collapse-implication}, and the statement that the
 recurrences of A189281 and A110128 are not proved.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Status of the conjecture.}
Conjecture~\ref{spf:conj:collapse} (rational collapse) is a conjecture. It has been
checked as an identity of rational functions for $h=4,\ldots,16$ only, and the
integrality of every $c_J^{(1)}(2,2)$ is conditional on it
(Proposition~\ref{spf:prop:collapse-implication}).
[Added 3 October 2026, batch 85: this paragraph describes the report before
batch~85. The conjecture is proved in Part~III
(\cref{spf:rc:thm:A-collapse}), and the integrality is unconditional
(\cref{spf:rc:cor:unconditional,spf:rc:thm:integrality}); see the
paragraph ``Part~III (batch 85)'' below.]

\paragraph{Overloaded letters.}
The manuscript reuses a few letters; none is renamed here. $C$ is a tiling
count $C_{\mathcal P}(\mathbf b)$ in \cref{spf:sec:tilings,spf:sec:stable}, the
number of non-dimer components in \cref{spf:sec:coefficients}, a constant
$C_{r,s}$ in Lemma~\ref{spf:lem:Qbound}, and the series $C(x)$ in
\cref{spf:sec:inverse}. $K$ is a moment cutoff in
Lemma~\ref{spf:lem:moment-bound} and Theorem~\ref{spf:thm:universality} but the edge degree of the
non-dimer part of a profile in \cref{spf:sec:coefficients}. $d$ is the
defect of a profile, while $d_j(w)$ are the inverse coefficients of
\cref{spf:sec:inverse}. $\mathcal D$ is a formal series in
\cref{spf:sec:stable} and $D$ the index correction in \cref{spf:sec:inverse}.
In each case the section determines the meaning.

\paragraph{Checks by the intake.}
The intake reran every shipped program on a copy (all passed; the
regenerated outputs equal the shipped ones apart from line endings, a final
newline and timings), recounted both sequences for $n\le9$ by brute force,
compared the two order-ten expansions with the OEIS entries digit for digit,
rederived $d_1$ in \cref{spf:thm:inverse-first} by hand, and made the
$(1,1)$ checks of Remark~\ref{spf:rem:offsets-one-one}. These checks do not
replace an independent review of the proofs.
